2
Synthetic approach

A special advantage in studying the Geometric Langlands Conjecture for
curves of genus
2
is that we can utilize two independent geometric
approaches to the problem. We can study our various objects modularly,
in term of their interpretation as moduli spaces of bundles with
various decorations, or synthetically, in terms of the geometry of the
intersection of two quadrics. In this section we follow the synthetic
approach. In the rest of this work we follow the modular approach. The
modular techniques we develop are considerably more difficult, but
they are less dependent on specifics of our particular situation, so
it is more likely that they can be extended to more general cases of
GLC.

In this section we will describe synthetically the geometric
correspondences between most fundamental objects of interest in this
paper - the base locus
X_{1}
of a general pencil of quadrics in

{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
and a ruling
X_{0}
of a general quadric in the pencil. We
will also see synthetically how this geometry ties up with the genus
two curve
C
parametrizing the rulings of the quadrics in the pencil
and with the geometry of the Jacobian of
C
.

The significance of the synthetic considerations stems from the fact
that, through the works of Narasimhan-Ramanan
[
NR69
]
and Newstead

[
New68
]
, the objects
X_{1}
and
X_{0}
are identified with
the moduli of semistable rank
2
bundles of fixed determinant of degree
1

and
0
on the curve
C
. Later on we will refine these
identifications and will in particular show that synthetic
constructions of this section reproduce the Hecke correspondences and
their discriminant (wobbly) loci in moduli. This will give us easy
geometric proofs of most of the important geometric properties needed
for the analysis of the Hecke eigensheaf condition.

2.1
Pencils of quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}

We will study the geometry of a general pencil of quadrics in

{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
by analyzing the families of linear subspaces contained in
these quadrics.

2.1.1. Notation
Our notation in this section is:

X=X_{1}=\cap_{x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}Q_{x}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}

is the smooth
intersection of a generic pencil of quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
.

\ell\subset X

is a line in
X
.

v\in X

is a point in
X
.

\mathsf{Grass}(3,6)

is the Grassmannian of projective planes
\Pi\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
.

The universal ruling is
\mathscr{R}:=\left\{(x,\Pi)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times\mathsf{Grass}(3,6)\,\left|\,\Pi\subset Q_{x}\right.\right\}
. The
fiber of
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathscr{R}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
over a general
x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

has 2 components, the rulings of the quadric
Q_{x}
. We can thus
consider the Stein factorization of
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathscr{R}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
:

\textstyle{\mathscr{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{C}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}.}

Then
C
is a hyperelliptic curve of genus
2
, with
6
Weierstrass
points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}
, which are the ramification points of the hyperelliptic
map
\mathsf{h}_{C}
. They correspond to the rulings on the
6
singular quadrics
in the pencil. We fix one of them, labelled
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\in C
.

The fibers of
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}
, parametrizing planes in a given ruling
t\in C
, are spinor varieties, isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. The subvariety
parametrizing planes in ruling
t
that pass through a specified point

v\in X
is a line in this
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. Through a specified line
\ell\subset X
there is a unique plane in each ruling.

We choose coordinates on
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
that are adapted to our pencil,
meaning that the
6
coordinate points are the vertices of the
6
quadric
cones in our pencil. All the quadrics
Q_{x}
become simultaneously
diagonalized. If we take the matrix of one of the quadrics to be the
identity and that of another quadric in the pencil to be

diag(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{6})
, the equation of
C
becomes
y^{2}=\Pi_{i=1}^{6}(x-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i})
, and
\mathsf{h}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i})=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}
.

The modular approach studies bundles on this
genus
2
hyperelliptic curve
C
. When we wish to compare the
synthetic and modular approaches, the line
\ell
will correspond to a
line bundle
L\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(C)
, and the point
v
will correspond to a
rank
2
vector bundle
V
on
C
with determinant
\det(V)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

2.2
Basic geometry of the lines

Let
\mathbf{A}
denote the variety of lines
\ell\subset X
in
X
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Through any point
v\in X
there are four lines
\ell\in\mathbf{A}
(counting
multiplicities).

Proof.

For a point
v\in X
let

{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
denote the projective
tangent space to
X
at
v
. A line
\ell\in{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
passing
through
v
is in
X
iff it is in
X\cap{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X
. But
X\cap{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X
is a curve in
{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
that has degree

4
and is a cone with vertex
v
, so it consists of
4
lines.
∎

In any ruling of a quadric
Q_{x}
there is a unique plane

\Pi
containing any
\ell\subset Q_{x}
, and in particular any
\ell\subset X
. In the latter case, the intersection
\Pi\cap X
consists
of two lines, the given
\ell
and another line
\ell^{\prime}\subset X
. We
get a natural map

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}:C\times\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}

sending
(t,\ell)\mapsto\ell^{\prime}
, where
t\in C
labels a ruling

\mathscr{R}_{t}
of the quadric
Q_{\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)}
,
\ell\subset X
is a
line,
\Pi
is the plane in
\mathscr{R}_{t}
containing
\ell
, and

\ell^{\prime}
is the other line in
\Pi\cap X
. Fixing
\ell\in\mathbf{A}
we get
a map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\to\mathbf{A}
. Fixing
t\in C
we get an involution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}
. We will be particularly interested in

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\in C
is our chosen Weierstrass point. The
action of
C\times C
on
\mathbf{A}
sending
\ell\to\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t_{1}}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t_{2}}(\ell)
is easily seen
[
Don80
]
to descend to

\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(C)
and further to the Jacobian
\mathsf{J}=\operatorname{Jac}(C)=\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(C)\cong\operatorname{Pic}^{2}(C)
, where the last
identification sends
0
to the canonical bundle
\omega_{C}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The resulting map

\mathsf{J}\times\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}

turns
\mathbf{A}
into a
\mathsf{J}
-torsor. The choice of
\ell\in\mathbf{A}

thus gives an isomorphism

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}:\mathsf{J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathbf{A}

sending the origin to
\ell
and restricting to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}
on
C
,
embedded in
\mathsf{J}
via Abel-Jacobi with
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
mapped to the
origin. Note that if
\ell,\mathsfit{m}\in\mathbf{A}
are two lines in
X
, then

\mathsfit{m}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}(M)
for a unique
M\in\mathsf{J}
, and

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\mathsfit{m}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}\circ\boldsymbol{t}_{M},

where
\boldsymbol{t}_{M}:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J}
is translation by
M
:

\boldsymbol{t}_{M}(N):=M\otimes N
.

As above, given a smooth point
z
of a subvariety
Z\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
in a projective space, we let
{\mathbb{T}}_{z}Z\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{\dim Z}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
denote the projective tangent space at
z
, i.e. the linear
subspace of
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}
containing
z
and pointing in the direction of the
usual tangent space
T_{z}Z
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There are 16 lines
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}\in\mathbf{A}
that are fixed by the involution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
:

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}.

Proof.

We start with an initial
\ell\in\mathbf{A}
and will see how to modify it to be
a fixed point. Conjugating
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
by
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}
we get an
involution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J}.

This sends
L\in\mathsf{J}
to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{U}}_{\ell}\otimes L^{-1}
,
where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{U}}_{\ell}:=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\ell)\in\mathsf{J}
. This involution of
\mathsf{J}
has
16
fixed points,
namely the square roots
M
of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{U}}_{\ell}
. Replacing our initial
\ell

by
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}:=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}(M)
gives a new line
O
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}\circ\boldsymbol{t}_{M}
, so

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}=\boldsymbol{t}_{M}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}\circ\boldsymbol{t}_{M}

is just inversion, so
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}
as desired. (The
existence of
16

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}
’s with this property was shown in

[
Don80
]
via a Schubert cycle calculation.)
∎

If we choose one of these fixed points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}\in\mathbf{A}
as origin, then
the composition

(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}})^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\times\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}):C\times\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J}

becomes the Abel-Jacobi map

(t,L)\mapsto L(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),

so its restriction:

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}:C\to\mathbf{A}\to\mathsf{J}

becomes the Abel-Jacobi map

t\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),

and the involutions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{r}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J}

L\mapsto L^{-1}(t-{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}).

so as we have seen,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\circ become:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J}
is just inversion.

For the modular/synthetic dictionary, we set

\ell=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}(L),\qquad L=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}(\ell).

Note that while
\mathbf{A}
depends only on
X
, the isomorphism

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}
depends on the auxiliary choices of the Weierstrass
point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
and the origin
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}
.

2.2.1. Line incidence and special lines
Given
\ell\in\mathbf{A}
, consider the family

I_{\ell}\subset\mathbf{A}

of lines
\mathsfit{m}\in\mathbf{A}
that intersect
\ell
.
By definition, this is the closure (in
\mathbf{A}
) of:

I^{\circ}_{\ell}:=\left\{\mathsfit{m}\in\mathbf{A}\,\left|\,\mathsfit{m}\neq\ell,\mathsfit{m}\cap\ell\neq\varnothing\right.\right\}.

For general
\ell
,
I^{\circ}_{\ell}=I_{\ell}
is closed, so we do not
need the closure. We say that the line
\ell
is
special
if it “intersects itself”, in the sense that
\ell\in I_{\ell}
, so
I^{\circ}_{\ell}\neq I_{\ell}
(Compare with
[
GH94
, page 792]
).

Our isomorphism

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}:J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathbf{A}

identifies
I_{\ell}
with the theta divisor

\Theta_{\ell}=\Theta_{L}=\left\{M\in\mathsf{J}\,\left|\,h^{0}(L\otimes M(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})>0\right.\right\}=\left\{{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\otimes L^{-1})\,\left|t\in C\right.\right\}\subset\mathsf{J},

where
\ell=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}(L)
.

I_{\ell}
is the image of our map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\to\mathbf{A}
, and in fact
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}
induces an isomorphism
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}I_{\ell}
. The composed map
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}})^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\to\Theta_{L}
is just the
Abel-Jacobi map:

t\mapsto(L(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))^{-1}(t).

Consider the curve

{\overline{C}}:=\left\{\,(L,t)\ \left|\ L^{2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\,\right.\right\}\subset\mathsf{J}\times C.

The second projection realizes
{\overline{C}}
as the
16
-sheeted cover
{\mathsf{sq}}:{\overline{C}}\to C
induced from the doubling map
\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{J},\ \ L\mapsto L^{2}
and the Abel-Jacobi map
C\to\mathsf{J},\ \ t\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The first projection
identifies
{\overline{C}}
with its image in
\mathsf{J}
. Composing with the
isomorphism
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}
, we identify
{\overline{C}}
as a subvariety of

\mathbf{A}
. In summary, this gives the following

Corollary 2.3
.

The line
\ell
is special iff
L=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}(\ell)\in{\overline{C}}\subset\mathsf{J}
, iff
L^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
is effective.

From now on we will think of
{\overline{C}}
as the subvariety of
\mathbf{A}

parametrizing special lines. It is intrinsic to
X
, independent of
choices of
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}
.

A central object in our study is the Wobbly locus in
X
. We define it
to be:

{\rm Wob}:=\bigcup_{\ell\in{\overline{C}}}\ \ell\ \subset\ X,

i.e. the union of all the special lines.

2.2.2. Trigonal bundles
Via the map
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\to I_{\ell}
, we have identified
C
with the family
I_{\ell}
of
lines
\mathsfit{m}
meeting a given line
\ell
, while
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}

identifies
I_{\ell}
with
\Theta_{\ell}
. The composition

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}:C\to\Theta_{\ell}
sends
t\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\otimes L^{-1}
, where again
L=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}(\ell)
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
L=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}(\ell)
.
The map
\mathsf{a}^{\circ}=\mathsf{a}_{\ell}^{\circ}:I_{\ell}^{\circ}\to\ell
sending
\mathsfit{m}\to\mathsfit{m}\cap\ell
extends
to
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{a}_{\ell}:C\cong I_{\ell}\to\ell
, where
it is given by the linear system of sections of the degree
3
line
bundle
L^{2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

Proof.

We have a morphism
X\to\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(C)
sending
v
to

\otimes_{i=1}^{4}L_{i}
, where the
L_{i}
are the line bundles
corresponding to the
4
lines
\ell_{i}
through
v
, i.e.
L_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}}^{-1}(\ell_{i})
. Since
X
is unirational (in fact,
rational), this map must be constant. Our choice of origin assures us
that this constant value of this map is the origin in

\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(C)
. So for each point
v
of
\ell
, the sum of the
three lines other than
\ell
through
v
(i.e. the product of the
corresponding line bundles) must be
L^{-1}\in\mathsf{J}
. The
identification of
\Theta_{\ell}
with
C
involves a translation by

L(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Suppose that the four lines
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}

through
v\in\ell
are labeled so that
\ell_{4}=\ell
. So when we
convert the three
L_{i}
to points
t_{i}\in C
, we see that their sum,
in
\operatorname{Pic}^{3}(C)
, is:

{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\Sigma_{i=1}^{3}t_{i})=L^{-1}\otimes(L(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})^{\otimes 3})=L^{2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

as claimed.
∎

There are two possibilities:
L(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
could be base-point free and
therefore give a genuine trigonal map, or it could have a base
point. This happens iff
L(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})=\omega_{C}(t)
for some
t\in C
, or
equivalently iff
L^{2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. We immediately get

Corollary 2.5
.

The trigonal bundle
L^{2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
has a base point iff
\ell
is special,
or equivalently
(L,t)
belongs to
{\overline{C}}
.

In the case of a special
\ell
, the map
\mathsf{a}_{\ell}:C\dashrightarrow\ell
sending
\mathsfit{m}\to\mathsfit{m}\cap\ell
is still given
by the linear system of sections of
L^{2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, but now this linear
system has the base point
t
, so it is only a rational map. That
means that the trigonal curve is now reducible,
C\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with

t\in C
glued to
\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, where
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

is the hyperelliptic double cover. The rational map

\mathsf{a}_{\ell}
lifts to a morphism

\widetilde{\mathsf{a}}_{\ell}:C\cup{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to\ell
. When
restricted to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
this gives a natural isomorphism

\mathsf{a}^{\circ}_{\ell}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to\ell
, and its restriction to

C
agrees with
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
followed by this isomorphism

\mathsf{a}^{\circ}_{\ell}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to\ell
. In this case the
3

lines meeting
\ell
at any
v\in\ell
consist of the fixed line

\ell
, plus the moving hyperelliptic pair
\mathsf{h}_{C}^{-1}(v)
, or more
precisely, their images under
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\ell}
. So the
4
lines through
any such
v
consist of twice
\ell
, plus the pair

\mathsf{h}_{C}^{-1}(v)
.

Going back to a general
\ell
, the trigonal map
\mathsf{a}:C\to\ell
has
8
branch points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{b}}_{i}\in\ell
, with

\mathsf{a}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{b}}_{i})=2r_{i}+s_{i}
consisting of the ramification
point
r_{i}
and one other point
s_{i}
. The corresponding
4
lines
through such a
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{b}}_{i}
are then
\ell,\mathsfit{m}_{i}
, and
\ell_{i}
occurring
with multiplicity
2
. Here
\ell_{i}
corresponds to the degree
0
line
bundle
L^{-1}(r_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, and similarly
\mathsfit{m}_{i}
corresponds to the
degree
0
line bundle
L^{-1}(s_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Here
\ell
is arbitrary, but the

\ell_{i}
are in
{\overline{C}}
. In fact, the
8
ramification points are the
intersection of
{\overline{C}}
with an appropriate translate of the
\Theta

divisor.

2.2.3. Patterns of lines

We have now proved
everything we need in order to describe all possible patterns of the
4

lines through some
v\in X
. These are summarized in the follwoing:

Theorem 2.6
.

For a point
v
in a line
\ell\in\mathbf{A}
, the possible patterns of lines
through
v
are:

•

2\ell+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
:
\ell
counts twice among the

4
lines through
v
iff
\ell\in I_{\ell}
, iff
\ell
is
special, iff the trigonal line bundle
L^{2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
has a base
point.

•

\ell+2\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
: One of the lines in

(\mathsf{a}_{\ell})^{-1}(v)
counts twice iff
v
is a branch point
of
\mathsf{a}_{\ell}:C\to\ell
.

•

2\ell+2\ell_{1}
: The fiber
(\mathsf{a}_{\ell})^{-1}(v)

consists of two lines
\ell,\ell_{1}
, each counted twice, iff the
lines
\ell,\ell_{1}\subset X
intersect at the point
v
, which is
then a singular point of
{\rm Wob}
. This occurs iff

\ell_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i})=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i},\ell)
where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}:=(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell})^{-1}(\ell_{1})
is one of the 6 Weierstrass
points of the hyperelliptic
C
, and
v=\mathsf{h}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i})
is its image.

•

3\ell+\ell_{1}
: The line
\ell
counts
3
times among the

4
lines through
v
iff
\ell
is special, corresponding to
(L,t)\in{\overline{C}}
, and
v=\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)
.

•

\ell+3\ell_{1}
:
(\mathsf{a}_{\ell})^{-1}(v)
consists of a
single line
\ell_{1}
counted
3
times iff

(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell})^{-1}(\ell_{1})
is a total ramification point of

\mathsf{a}_{\ell}:C\to\ell
, and
v=\mathsf{h}_{C}((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{j}}_{\ell})^{-1}(\ell_{1}))
is the corresponding total branch
point.

•

4\ell
: The lines that count
4
times in a fiber are the

16
origin-candidates
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{O}}
from Lemma
2.2
.

Note in particular that pattern
2\ell+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
is
independent of
v\in\ell
. We can prove this directly:

Lemma 2.7
.

If
\ell
counts twice through some
v\in\ell
, it does so for every

v\in\ell
.

Proof.

Given
\ell\subset X=\cap_{x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}Q_{x}
, let
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}^{3}_{\ell}
be
the quotient projective space
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}/\ell
, parametrizing planes in

{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
through
\ell
, and let
{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
denote its dual,
parametrizing hyperplanes in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
through
\ell
. Let the line

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v}
denote the projection of
{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X
to
{\mathbb{P}}
, and

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}^{\vee}_{v}
the dual line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
.

Consider the morphism
\ell\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
sending

(v,x)\mapsto{\mathbb{T}}_{v}Q_{x}
. It is well-defined (each
v
is a
non-singular point of each
Q_{x}
) and linear in each of its arguments

v,x
, i.e. it is given by a base-point free sublinear system of

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}}(1,1)
. There are only two
possibilities: the map can be an embedding, identifying
\ell\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with a smooth quadric
\mathsfit{quad}^{\vee}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
, or it
can be
2
-to-
1
onto a plane
\Pi\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
, with branch
locus a conic
\mathsfit{Conic}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{\vee}
. In the first case, the lines

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}^{\vee}_{v}
form a ruling of
\mathsfit{quad}^{\vee}
, and dually the
lines
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v}
form a ruling of the dual quadric
\mathsfit{quad}\subset{\mathbb{P}}
. In the second case, the lines
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}^{\vee}_{v}
are the
tangents in
\Pi
to
\mathsfit{Conic}
, and dually the lines
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v}
form the
ruling of a quadratic cone
\mathsfit{quad}\subset{\mathbb{P}}
.

Now the line
\ell
counts more than once among the
4
lines in
X

through
v\in\ell
if and only if the intersection
{\mathbb{T}}_{X}\cap X
is
singular at every
v^{\prime}\in\ell
. This happens if and only if
{\mathbb{T}}_{v}X

is not transversal to
{\mathbb{T}}_{v^{\prime}}X
, if and only if the lines

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v^{\prime}}
intersect in
{\mathbb{P}}
. This happens if and only
if our quadric
\mathsfit{quad}\subset{\mathbb{P}}
is singular, i.e. the second case
above. But that means that all the lines
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{l}}_{v^{\prime}}
intersect
each other (at the vertex of
\mathsfit{quad}
).
∎

2.3
The wobbly divisor

We defined
{\rm Wob}
as the union in

X
of the special lines. For a general line
\ell\subset X
, the
family
I_{\ell}
of lines intersecting
\ell
is given by
I_{\ell}\cong C
, which comes with a trigonal map
\mathsf{a}_{\ell}:I_{\ell}\to\ell
. According to the first pattern in
Theorem
2.6
, the
8
branch points of
\mathsf{a}_{\ell}

are the intersection
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}
. In particular, the class of

{\rm Wob}
is
8H
, where
H
is the positive generator of
\operatorname{Pic}(X)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Consider the surface

D:={\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=\left\{\,(\ell,x)\ \left|\ {\ell\ \text{special}},\,x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\,\right.\right\}\cong\left\{\,(\ell,v)\ \left|\ {\ell\ \text{special}},\,v\in\ell\right.\right\},

where
v=\mathsf{a}^{\circ}_{\ell}(x)
, using the canonical
identification
\mathsf{a}^{\circ}_{\ell}:{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to\ell
of each
special
\ell
with the hyperelliptic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Let
\Gamma\subset D
be the graph of the map
{\overline{C}}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

sending
(A,t)\to\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)
. For each of the
6
Weierstrass points

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}
, let
\Gamma_{i}:={\overline{C}}\times\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\}\subset D={\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}:=\mathsf{h}_{C}(p_{i})
. The involution

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}
acts on
\Gamma_{i}
. In terms of the

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
-dependent identification of
{\overline{C}}
with its image in

\mathsf{J}
, this involution sends
A\to A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. So it
has
16
fixed points, and the quotient
\Gamma_{i}/\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}
has
genus
5
.

Theorem 2.8
.

The map
\boldsymbol{\nu}:D\to X
sending
(\ell,v)\to v
is a finite morphism and
maps
D
birationally onto
{\rm Wob}
, so it gives its normalization. It
is an isomorphism away from
\Gamma
and the
\Gamma_{i}
. The map
\boldsymbol{\nu}

is
2
-to-
1
on
\Gamma_{i}
, with
16
fixed points.
D
has normal
crossings along the genus
5
curve
\Gamma_{i}/\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}
, except
at the 16 fixed points. The fixed points correspond to pattern

4\ell
, and the other points of
\Gamma_{i}/\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}
correspond
to pattern
2\ell+2\ell_{1}
. The restriction of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
to
\Gamma
is
an embedding, and the image parametrizes pattern
3\ell+\ell_{1}
.
But
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is not immersive along
\Gamma
- the image
{\rm Wob}=\boldsymbol{\nu}(D)

has a curve of cusps along it.

Proof.

The two distinct points
(\ell,v),(\mathsfit{m},v)\in D
map to the same
v\in X
iff the special lines
\ell,\mathsfit{m}
intersect at
v\in X
. This means
that
\mathsfit{m}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{r}(\ell)
for some
r\in C
, According to pattern

2\ell+2\mathsfit{m}
in Theorem
2.6
, this occurs iff
\mathsfit{m}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}(\ell)
for one of the Weierstrass points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}\in C
. This
means that we are on one of the
\Gamma_{i}
, and
\ell,\mathsfit{m}
are related by
the involution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}}
.

Pattern
3\ell+\mathsfit{m}
in Theorem
2.6
shows that
\Gamma

is the inverse image under
\boldsymbol{\nu}
of the locus of
(\ell,v)
such that

(\ell,v)
counts
3
times among the lines through
v
. Finally we
claim that special line
\ell\subset X
is tangent to
\boldsymbol{\nu}(\Gamma)\subset X
at the point
v\in\ell
at which the patern of lines in

X
is
3\ell+\mathsfit{m}
. We will leave it to the reader to verify this
tangency in the synthetic language but we will give a modular proof of
this statement in section
4.4
.
∎

2.4
Synthetic correspondences and rigidification

2.4.1. The connection
The universal ruling
\mathscr{R}
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}:\mathscr{R}\to C
over our hyperelliptic curve
C
.

Lemma 2.9
.

The pullback

\overline{\mathscr{R}}:=\mathscr{R}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}

is a product,

\overline{\mathscr{R}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\overline{C}}
.

Proof.

We work with a coordinate system adapted to our pencil, so the
Q_{x}

become diagonal matrices. For
x,x_{0}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{6}\}
, let
S:=S_{x_{0},x}
be a diagonal matrix such that

S^{2}=Q_{x_{0}}^{-1}Q_{x}
. Then
S
acts on
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
, taking
Q_{x_{0}}

to
Q_{x}
. The set of these square roots
S_{x_{0},x}
has cardinality

2^{6}
, and forms a torsor under the action of the group
G:=({\mathbb{Z}}/2)^{6}
. If
t,t_{0}\in C
lie above
x,x_{0}
, i.e.
\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)=x,\ \mathsf{h}_{C}(t_{0})=x_{0}
, then half of these
S
’s take the ruling

\mathscr{R}_{t_{0}}
of
Q_{x_{0}}
to the ruling
\mathscr{R}_{t}
of

Q_{x}
, while the other half take
\mathscr{R}_{t_{0}}
to the other
ruling of
Q_{x}
. The set of square roots
S_{x_{0},x}
taking
t_{0}

to
t
is a torsor under the subgroup
G_{0}\cong({\mathbb{Z}}/2)^{5}\subset G

which is the kernel of the sum map
({\mathbb{Z}}/2)^{6}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2
. Further,
we have the diagonal embedding
{\mathbb{Z}}/2\to G_{0}
. Its non-zero element
exchanges
S
with
-S
, so it does not affect the transformation of

{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
. The quotient
G_{0}/({\mathbb{Z}}/2)
is canonically identified with

\mathsf{J}[2]
, the
2
-torsion subgroup of the Jacobian of
C
. We
see that the family of quadrics
\{Q_{h_{C}(t)}\ |\ t\in C\setminus\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{6}\}\ \}
has a flat connection with monodromy

J[2]
, and it becomes a product when pulled back to
{\overline{C}}
minus the
inverse image of the Weierstrass points. The same holds therefore for

\mathscr{R}
. But since
\rho:\mathscr{R}\to C
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
bundle
(with no degenerations) over
C
, including the Weierstrass points,
the flat connection extends to all of
\mathscr{R}
,with the same
monodromy
\mathsf{J}[2]
, and therefore the pullback to
{\overline{C}}
is a
product as claimed.
∎

A fancier way to understand this is by identifying the
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
fiber
of
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}:\mathscr{R}\to C
over
t\in C
with a translated
“
2\Theta
” linear system on
\mathsf{J}
. The right translation is
seen to be by a square root of
{\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, which gives rise to the

\mathsf{J}[2]
-monodromy and to the cover
{\overline{C}}\to C
. The
projective connection we described here explicitly becomes a special
case of the general theory of theta groups and their actions, due to
Kummer, Heisenberg, Mumford etc. This approach is discussed in section

6.5
.

2.4.2. The Hecke correspondence
We will encounter
several versions of the Hecke correspondence. The basic one is the
incidence correspondence:

\mathcal{H}:=\{(v,\Pi)\ |\ v\in\Pi\}\subset X\times\mathscr{R}

between points of
X
and planes contained in some quadric through

X
. Fixing
t\in C
, i.e. fixing the quadric and one of its rulings,
we have

\mathcal{H}(t):=\mathcal{H}\cap(X\times\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t))=\{(v,\Pi)\ |\ v\in\Pi,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}(\Pi)=t\}\ \subset\ X\times\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t)\cong X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}.

The fiber of
\mathcal{H}
over
\Pi\in\mathscr{R}
(or the fiber of

\mathcal{H}(t)
over
\Pi\in\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t)
) is the conic
X\cap\Pi
,
which could be smooth, a pair of intersecting lines, or a double
line. The fiber of
\mathcal{H}(t)
over
v\in X
is a line in
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t)\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. The fiber of
\mathcal{H}
over
v\in X
is
therefore a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle
{\mathbb{P}}E_{v}
over
C
. (In the modular
approach, points
v\in X
will correspond to rank-2 vector bundles

E_{v}
on
C
whose determinant is
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, and our
{\mathbb{P}}E_{v}
will
be the projectivization of
E_{v}
.)

Recall from Lemma
2.9
that the cover
\overline{\mathscr{R}}=\mathscr{R}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}
is a product,
\overline{\mathscr{R}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\overline{C}}
. A rigidified Hecke correspondence is obtained by
pulling back to
\overline{\mathscr{R}}
via
\overline{\mathscr{R}}\to\mathscr{R}
:

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}:=(X\times\overline{\mathscr{R}})\times_{X\times\mathscr{R}}\mathcal{H}\ \subset\ X\times\overline{\mathscr{R}}\cong X\times{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\times{\overline{C}}.

From the modular point of view, we will be interested in two moduli
spaces of bundles:
X_{1}=X
parametrizes rank
2
bundles on
C
with
determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, while
X_{0}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
parametrizes rank
2

bundles on
C
with determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
. The big Hecke correspondence
can then be understood as a subvariety:

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\subset X_{1}\times X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}.

The group
\mathsf{J}[2]
acts on all three factors. The action of

\mathsf{J}[2]=G_{0}/({\mathbb{Z}}/2)
on
X_{1}=X
was described in the proof
of lemma
2.9
. Let
\mathsf{M}_{1}:=[X_{1}/\mathsf{J}[2]]
denote
the quotient. Since
X_{0}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
was defined as the family of
flat sections of
\overline{\mathscr{R}}\to{\overline{C}}
, we get an action
of
\mathsf{J}[2]
on
X_{0}
induced from its action on

\overline{\mathscr{R}}
. Let
\mathsf{M}_{0}
denote the quotient
[X_{0}/\mathsf{J}[2]]
. The action of
\mathsf{J}[2]
on
{\overline{C}}
is the
familiar one, with quotient
C
. The Hecke correspondence is
compatible with these actions, so we get another version of the Hecke
correspondence

\mathbf{Hecke}\subset\mathsf{M}_{1}\times\mathsf{M}_{0}\times C.

This is the Hecke correspondence for the group
{\mathbb{P}}GL(2)
.

2.5
The quadric line complex

Starting with the intersection
of quadrics
X
we retrieve the curve
C
, its cover
{\overline{C}}
, the
universal ruling
\mathscr{R}
, and its cover
\overline{\mathscr{R}}\to{\overline{C}}
. We can therefore recover ‘the’
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
as the family of flat
sections of
\overline{\mathscr{R}}\to{\overline{C}}
. We can also go in the
opposite direction.

The Grassmannian
\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}={\mathbb{P}}(V)

can be identified, via the Plücker embedding, as a smooth quadric
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}={\mathbb{P}}(\wedge^{2}V)
. All smooth quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
are
isomorphic by a projective transformation, and the automorphisms of

{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
correspond to automorphisms of
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
preserving the quadric
and each of its rulings, via the group isomorphism
{\mathbb{P}}GL(4)\cong{\mathbb{P}}SO(6)
. Thus, any smooth quadric with a specified ruling may be
viewed as being the Grassmanian
\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
up to adjustment
of the identification
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}={\mathbb{P}}(\wedge^{2}V)
, in a way that is
unique up to projective transformations of
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
.

The Grassmanian has two rulings: a point
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
determines the
plane
\Pi=\Pi_{p}\subset\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
of all lines in

{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
through
p
, while a plane
P\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
determines the
plane
\Pi=\Pi_{P}\subset\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
of all lines in
P
.
Each of these rulings may be viewed as being specified, once chosen
the isomorphism of a certain quadric with the Grassmanian.

If we choose another quadric meeting
\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)

transversally, then these two quadrics span a pencil giving the
situation at the start of this chapter. The intersection of

\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
with the other quadric is known as the

quadric line complex
. In the classical terminology cf

[
GH94
]
, the “line complex” means the family of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}

parametrized by this quadric section of
\mathsf{Grass}(2,4)
, the

“lines of the quadric line complex”
.

Thus the quadric line complex is the intersection
X
of a general pencil of
quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
. It carries a bit more information though:
specifying our
X
is equivalent to specifying the hyperelliptic curve

C
, while specifying a quadric line complex is equivalent to
specifying the hyperelliptic curve
C
together with one
non-Weierstrass point
t\in C
: the point
\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

corresponds to the quadric we identify as the Grassmannian, the point

t\in C
corresponds to its ruling by planes
\Pi_{p}
for points
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
.

In the other direction, saying that
X
is the quadric line complex
depends on the choice of
t\in C
or indeed on its lifting to

(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
, because the correspondence between points of
X
and
lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
is exactly the Hecke correspondence depending on
t
,
with furthermore the identification between the ruling and a fixed

{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
being dependent upon the lifting to
{\overline{C}}
.

2.6
Hecke curves and Kummer surfaces

Consider the incidence:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}=\left\{\,(\ell,\Pi)\ |\ \ell\subset\Pi\,\right\}\ \subset\ \mathbf{A}\times\mathscr{R}.

We have an isomorphism

C\times\mathbf{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}

sending
(t,\ell)\mapsto(\ell,\Pi)
, where
\Pi\subset Q_{\mathsf{h}_{C}(t)}
is the plane spanned by
\ell
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}(t,\ell)
.
The involution
\ell\mapsto\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}(t,\ell)
acts on

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}
, with quotient:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}um}:=\left\{\,\Pi\in\mathscr{R}\ |\ \Pi\cap X\ \text{contains a line}\,\right\}.

We have maps

\textstyle{C\times\mathbf{A}\ \cong\ \boldsymbol{\mathcal{J}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2:1}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}um}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{R},}

or, if we fix
t\in C
:

\mathbf{A}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2:1}}{{\to}}\ \mathsf{Kum}\ \hookrightarrow\ {\mathbb{P}}^{3}.

Here
\mathsf{Kum}
is the Kummer surface, image of
\mathbf{A}\cong\mathsf{J}
by
the
2\Theta
linear system, which embeds
\mathsf{Kum}=\mathbf{A}/\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t}\cong\mathsf{J}/{\pm 1}
into
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
as a singular hypersurface of degree
4
.
Note that the Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
has
16
nodes, images of the
16
fixed points of the
involution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{A}
. These are the points where the fiber

\ell\cup\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}_{t}(\ell)
becomes a double line.

A basic
fact is that
\mathsf{Kum}
is isomorphic to its dual
[
GH94
,
Keu97
]
.

Given a point
p\in{\mathbb{P}}^{3}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t)
, the fiber of the Hecke correspondence over

p
is a subset of
X
that was denoted by

X_{p}
in
[
GH94
]
. In the terminology of the quadric line complex
it is the set of lines in the line complex that pass through
p
.

Corollary 2.10
.

Fix
t\in C
and denote
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}:=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{rul}}^{-1}(t)
.

•

If
p
is a
point of
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}\setminus\mathsf{Kum}
, then
X_{p}
is a smooth conic
\Pi\cap X
.

•

If
p
is a smooth point of

\mathsf{Kum}
, then
X_{p}
is a union of two lines
\ell\cup\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{i}}(t,\ell)
that touch.

•

If
p
is one of the
16
nodes of

\mathsf{Kum}
, then
X_{p}
is a double line
2\ell
.

This was stated in
[
GH94
, pp 762-763]
.

The locus of points in
X
that are intersection points of the two
lines, for the second case of
X_{p}
, has closure that is a surface
denoted
\Sigma\subset X
in
[
GH94
]
. This is seen to be the

Kummer K3 surface
obtained by resolving the
16
nodes of

\mathsf{Kum}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
(indeed, over the nodes we get not a single point
but a line of points in
\Sigma
corresponding to the full double
line).

The K3 surface will show up in our situation as a locus of apparent
singularities for the Hecke transform from
X_{0}={\mathbb{P}}^{3}
to
X_{1}=X
. For the
main local systems constructed here, it turns out that the
singularities along
\Sigma
are removable. On the other hand for the
third construction in Chapter
11
, the
Hecke transform will have singularities along
\Sigma
. We refer the
reader to that chapter for more discussion.

In the synthetic picture, the trope planes may be characterized as
planes in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
that contain
6
of the
16
nodes. There are
16
of
these. They are also the planes that correspond to the
16
nodes of
the dual Kummer surface. The trope planes meet the Kummer surface in
plane conics counted with multiplicity two; these conics are
characterized also by passing through the
6
nodes that define the
plane.

The
16_{6}
property, saying that each plane contains
6
nodes (by
definition) and each node is contained in
6
planes, has been well-known since the 19th century.
Modern discussions can be found in

[
GH94
,
Bea96
,
Keu97
,
Dol20
]
.

3
General considerations

Throughout this paper, we consider a smooth projective curve
C
of
genus
2
. It is therefore hyperelliptic, with hyperelliptic
involution denoted
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}:C\rightarrow C
, and the quotient
projection denoted by
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Let

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{6}\in C
denote the Weierstrass points,
i.e. the ramification points for the map
\mathsf{h}_{C}
, and let

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{6}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
denote the corresponding branch
points. For brevity we will choose one of the Weierstrass points and
drop the index, writing
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}:=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{1}
. Thus the canonical line
bundle of
C
is given by
\omega_{C}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

If
x\in C
we will usually denote by
x^{\prime}:=\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}(x)
its image by
the hyperelliptic involution; then
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(x+x^{\prime})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})=\omega_{C}
.

Fix a line bundle
\mathbf{d}
to be either
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
or
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.
We will look at the moduli space
X
of polystable rank
2
vector
bundles
E
provided with an isomorphism
\det(E)\cong\mathbf{d}
. The
choice of
\mathbf{d}
, that is to say degree
0
or
1
, will be made
according to section in the paper—we prefer not to overload the
notations by indexing on this choice. When it becomes necessary to
distinguish them, we will denote the two moduli spaces by
X_{0}
and

X_{1}
and similarly for their associated constructions (e.g.
Y_{0}
,

Y_{1}
).

By Narasimhan-Ramanan
[
NR69
]
, for degree
0
we have

X={\mathbb{P}}^{3}
and for degree
1
we have
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
is a smooth
complete intersection of two quadrics. These descriptions will be
developed in more detail below.

Let
\mathsf{Higgs}
denote the coarse moduli space of semistable Higgs
bundles
(E,\theta)
of rank
2
with isomorphism
\det(E)\cong\mathbf{d}
and satisfying
\operatorname{tr}\theta=0
. In particular,

X\subset\mathsf{Higgs}
is the subset of Higgs bundles with Higgs field
equal to zero. The
Hitchin map
on rank
2
Higgs
bundles was defined by Hitchin
[
Hit87a
,
Hit87b
]

and is hiven by

\boldsymbol{h}:\mathsf{Higgs}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}:=H^{0}(\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2}),\quad(E,\theta)\mapsto\det(\theta)

The space of quadratic differentials
{\mathcal{B}}:=H^{0}(\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2})
is called the
Hitchin base
and in our case is a
complex vector space of dimension
3
.

We will write
T^{\vee}C
for the total space of

\omega_{C}
.
\boldsymbol{\pi}:T^{\vee}C\to C
for the natural
projection, and
\lambda\in H^{0}(T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\omega_{C})
for the tautological
section. For any quadratic differential
\mathsf{b}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, the
associated
spectral curve
is defined as the curve

{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathsf{b}}\subset T^{\vee}C
given by the equation

{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathsf{b}}\,:\,\lambda^{2}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\mathsf{b}=0.

In other words, the spectal curve correcsponding to
\mathsf{b}
is
the zero divisor of the holomorphic section
\lambda^{2}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\mathsf{b}\in H^{0}(T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\omega_{C})
. By definition, the
spectral curve associated to a Higgs bundle
(E,\theta)
is the
spectral curve corresponding to
\boldsymbol{h}(E,\theta)
. It is given explicitly
the equation

\det(\lambda\cdot\operatorname{id}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\theta)=\lambda^{2}-\det(\theta)=0,

where
\lambda\cdot\operatorname{id}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\theta
is viewed as a map

\lambda\cdot\operatorname{id}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\theta:\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}E\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}E\otimes\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\omega_{C}.

For future reference we record the following

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathsf{Higgs}
be the moduli
space of rank
2
Higgs bundles with fixed determinant
\mathbf{d}
. Then

(a)

The Hitchin map
\boldsymbol{h}:\mathsf{Higgs}\to{\mathcal{B}}
is proper and surjective.

(b)

For any
(E,\theta)\in\mathsf{Higgs}
the spectral curve

{\widetilde{C}}\subset T^{\vee}C
of
\theta
is a curve of arithmetic genus
5

and the projection

\pi=\boldsymbol{\pi}_{|{\widetilde{C}}}:{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow C

is the degree
2
covering branched over the bicanonical divisor

\det(\theta)=0
. For a general
(E,\theta)
the spectral curve

{\widetilde{C}}
is smooth and connected.

(c)

For any
\mathsf{b}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, the branch divisor of the
associated spectral curve
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathsf{b}}\to C
is the

\mathsf{h}_{C}
- pullback of a degree
2
effective divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}}
on

\mathbb{P}^{1}
. For a general
\mathsf{b}
the branch divisor

\mathsf{zero}(\mathsf{b})=\mathsf{h}_{C}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}})=\tilde{y}+\tilde{y}^{\prime}+\tilde{z}+\tilde{z}^{\prime}
consists of two

\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
-conjugate pairs of points in
C
.

(d)

For general
\mathsf{b}\in{\mathcal{B}}
the Hitchin fiber

\boldsymbol{h}^{-1}(\mathsf{b})
is identified with the
3
-dimensional abelian
variety
{\mathcal{P}}
of line bundles
L
on
{\widetilde{C}}
such that

\det(\pi_{*}(L))\cong\mathbf{d}
, or equivalently

\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)=\omega_{C}\otimes\mathbf{d}
. The Higgs bundle
corresponding to
L
is
(E,\theta)=(\pi_{*}L,\pi_{*}(\lambda\otimes(-)))
.

Proof.

Parts (a), (b), and (d) are standard and are proven in many classical
sources, e.g.
[
Hit87a
,
Hit87b
,
BNR89
]
. For part (c)
we only need to note that
\omega_{C}=\mathsf{h}_{C}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)
,
and that
\mathsf{h}_{C}^{*}:H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}(2))\to H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2})
is injective, Since both spaces are

3
-dimensional this shows that the pullback map is an isomorphism,
and so any quadratic differential on
C
is a pullback from an
effective degree
2
divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This proves (c) and
completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Throughout the paper, we will fix a point
\mathsf{b}
and the
corresponding spectral curve
{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathsf{b}}
. Since

\mathsf{b}
will be fixed we will drop the subscript from the
notation and will simply write
{\widetilde{C}}
finstead of

{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathsf{b}}
. We will also fix
\Lambda
the rank
2
local
system corresponding to a rank
2
Higgs bundle with trivial
determinant
(E,\theta)
.

3.1
Curves

Let us make the following notations and definitions.

C

is a curve of genus
g(C)=2
with hyperelliptic
map
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\in C

is a fixed Weierstrass point.

{\overline{C}}

:=\left\{(A,t)\ \left|A\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\;\;t\in C,\;\;A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right.\right\}
.

Note that the map

{\mathsf{sq}}:{\overline{C}}\to C,\quad{\mathsf{sq}}(A,t)=t

is a
16
-sheeted
étale cover of
C
, while the map

\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}}:{\overline{C}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\quad\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}}(A,t)=A

is a closed embedding. In particular

{\overline{C}}
is a smooth connected curve of genus
g({\overline{C}})=17
.

\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow C

is a fixed spectral curve,
assumed to be smooth. We also assume
that the spectral curve has two branches over
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
.

{\widehat{C}}:={\widetilde{C}}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}

is a smooth connected curve
of genus
g({\widehat{C}})=65
which fits in the fiber square

\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}}
\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{sq}}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{C}

Suppose
(E,\theta)
is a Higgs bundle with trivial
determinant on
C
, i.e.
\det(E)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
and
tr(\theta)=0
.
Our main condition is that
{\widetilde{C}}
is the spectral cover of

(E,\theta)
, this means that there is a line bundle
L
on
{\widetilde{C}}

such that
E=\pi_{*}L
and
\theta=\pi_{*}(\lambda\otimes(-))
.

The choice of
(E,\theta)
will determine
(via the construction we are going to do,
using its spectral data

({\widetilde{C}},L)
) two tame parabolic Higgs bundles of rank
8
,

({\mathscr{F}}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})/X_{0},\;\;\;\;({\mathscr{F}}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})/X_{1}.

(1)

Our goal is to give a detailed construction of these Higgs bundles and
to show that they satisfy the Hecke eigensheaf property on

X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}
.

3.2
Moduli spaces

The coarse moduli spaces of semistable bundles will be denoted by
X

with, if necessary, a subscript depending on the degree. These have
coverings destined to become the modular spectral covers of the parabolic
Higgs bundles we are going to construct. The notations are as follows.

X_{0}

is the moduli space of rank
2
bundles
F
with
\det(F)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}
. We have
X_{0}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}

[
NR69
]
.

X_{1}

is the moduli space of rank
2
bundles
E
with
\det(E)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(p)
. We have
[
NR69
,
New68
]

X_{1}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5},\;\;\;\;X_{1}=\bigcap_{x\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}Q_{x},

where
\{Q_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{P}^{!}}
is a pencil of quadrics in

\mathbb{P}^{5}
parametrized by the hyperelliptic
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of

C
, with a discriminant divisor being exactly the ramification divisor
of the hyperelliptic map
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}

is the degree two Prym for
{\widetilde{C}}
, that is to say

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}:=\left\{\left.L\in\operatorname{Jac}^{2}({\widetilde{C}})\;\;\right|\;\;\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)\cong\omega_{C}\right\}.

This is identified with the Hitchin fiber over
[\lambda:{\widetilde{C}}\hookrightarrow T^{\vee}C]\in{\mathcal{B}}
(corresponding point in the
Hitchin base) for the
SL(2)
Hitchin fibration
\boldsymbol{h}:\mathsf{Higgs}_{0}\to{\mathcal{B}}
.

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}

is the degree three Prym:

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}:=\left\{\left.L\in\operatorname{Jac}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\;\;\right|\;\;\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)\cong\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right\}.

It is the Hitchin fiber over
[\lambda:{\widetilde{C}}\hookrightarrow T^{\vee}C]\in{\mathcal{B}}
for the odd degree Hitchin fibration
\boldsymbol{h}:\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}\to{\mathcal{B}}
(Higgs bundles whose determinant Higgs bundle is

({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),0)
).

Y_{0}

is the blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
at the pullbacks of the theta
characteristics on
C
. More precisely, consider the un(semi)stable locus
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm unss}

\displaystyle=\left\{L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\;\;\left|\;\;\pi_{*}L\text{ is un(semi)stable }\right.\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\pi^{*}\kappa\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\;\;\left|\;\;\kappa\in\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(C),\text{ s.t. }\kappa^{\otimes 2}=\omega_{C}\right.\right\}

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}\mathsf{Spin}(C)

which has
16
points, and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
is the
blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm unss}\cong\mathsf{Spin}(C)
.

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}\subset Y_{0}

denotes
the exceptional divisor, it has
16
connected components

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}=\sqcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}.

f_{0}

is a morphism
f_{0}:Y_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
such that if

L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
is a point not on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}^{\rm unss}
we have

f_{0}(L)=\pi_{*}(L)
. In Theoroem
3.6
the morphism

f_{0}
is constructed as the minimal resolution of the rational map

\pi_{*}(-):{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}
. From the
construction it follows that for a general spectral curve
f_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
is finite and by Lemma
4.9
and
Remark
4.10
has degree
8
.

Y_{1}

is the blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
in the un(semi)stable
(
=
unstable) locus

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}^{\rm unss}=\left\{L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\;\;\left|\;\;\pi_{*}L\text{ is un(semi)stable }(=\text{is unstable})\right.\right\}\cong{\widehat{C}}.

To see that the unstable locus is related to
{\widehat{C}}
, suppose we have
a line bundle
L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
such that
\pi_{*}L
is unstable. Then

\pi_{*}L
will have a destabilizing line subbundle
M\subset\pi_{*}L
of degree one. By adjunction we get an injective map of
locally free rank one sheaves
\pi^{*}M\hookrightarrow L
and for
degree reasons we must have a short exact sequence

0\to\pi^{*}M\to L\to\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{t}}\to 0

for some point
\tilde{t}\in{\widetilde{C}}
. Writing
M=A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for some line bundle of degree
zero, we see that
\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-\pi(\tilde{t}))=\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=A^{-2}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Since
\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)=\mathcal{O}_{C}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
we
have that
A^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})=\mathcal{O}_{C}(\pi(\tilde{t}))
. Thus

(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
and
L=\pi^{*}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))(\tilde{t})
.
In Lemma
3.2
below we show that the map
{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
,
(A,\tilde{t})\mapsto\pi^{*}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))(\tilde{t})
is
a closed embedding which gives the identification
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}^{\rm unss}={\widehat{C}}
.

Again we will write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
for the blow-up
map.

f_{1}

is a morphism
f_{1}:Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
such that if

L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
is a point not on
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}^{\rm unss}
we have
f_{1}(L)=\pi_{*}L
. In Theoroem
3.6
the morphism

f_{1}
is constructed as the minimal resolution of the rational map

\pi_{*}(-):{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}
and by construction is finite for a general spectral cover. By Lemma
4.9

f_{1}
has degree
8
.

{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}_{1}\subset Y_{1}

denotes the exceptional divisor, it is a

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}=\mathbb{P}(N_{{\widehat{C}}/{\mathcal{P}}_{3}})\rightarrow{\widehat{C}}.

In Theoroem
3.6
we will see that in fact

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\cong{\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1},

where the second factor is naturally idenitified with the hyperelliptic

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
of
C
, that is it is identified with the projective line

\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee})
. We have a diagram

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}}

and in section
4
we will see that the map on the
bottom factors as

{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\rm Wob}_{1}\hookrightarrow X_{1}

where the surface
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is the wobbly divisor discussed in the
next section, and the map
{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\rm Wob}_{1}
is
the normalization.

To understand the map

{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}={\overline{C}}\times\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee})\to X_{1}.

(2)

in more concrete terms fix points
(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
and
x\in\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee})
. Any non-split extension of

A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
by
A
will be a stable rank two bundle with determinant

\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, i.e. will give us a point in
X_{1}
. However
the space of extensions of
A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
by
A
is canonically
identified with the space
H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee}
. Indeed we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),A)

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C,A^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))

(3)

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})

\displaystyle=H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee},

where
t^{\prime}
is the hyperelliptic conjugate of
t
, and the
identification
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})
is induced by the natural inclusion of
sheaves
\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

With this picture in mind we can now describe the map
(
2
). It sends a point
((A,t),x)\in{\overline{C}}\times\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee})
to the vector bundle
E\in X_{1}
, where
E
is defined as the unique up to isomorphism extension

0\to A\to E\to A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0

which corresponds to the extension class
x\in\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})^{\vee})\cong\mathbb{P}(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),A))
under the identification
(
3
).

Finally, note that the curve
{\overline{C}}
embeds in
{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
as
the graph of the composition

\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{sq}}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{C}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},}

with
\mathsf{h}_{C}
denoting the hyperelliptic map. It is easy to check
that restricting the map (
2
) to this embedded copy of

{\overline{C}}
yields a closed embedding

\textstyle{\mathsf{ql}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ \subset\mathbb{P}^{5}}

of
{\overline{C}}
in the intersection of two quadrics
X_{1}
.

We conclude this subsection with the promised check that the curve

{\widehat{C}}={\widetilde{C}}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}
embeds in
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The map

\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3},\quad(A,\tilde{t})\mapsto\pi^{*}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}).

(4)

embeds the curve
{\widehat{C}}
inside

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
. In particular
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}=\mathbb{P}(N_{{\widehat{C}}/{\mathcal{P}}_{3}})
.

Proof.

Suppose
(A_{1},\tilde{t}_{1}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2})\in{\widehat{C}}
. If
these two points map to the same point in
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, then we will
have

(\pi^{*}A_{1}^{-1})(\tilde{t}_{1})=(\pi^{*}A_{2}^{-1})(\tilde{t}_{2}).

(5)

Squaring this identity and using the fact that

A_{i}^{-2}(\pi(\tilde{t}_{i}))=\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for
i=1,2
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}

\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}A_{2}^{2})(-2\tilde{t}_{2})\otimes(\pi^{*}A_{1}^{-2})(2\tilde{t}_{1})

\displaystyle=\left(\pi^{*}(A_{2}^{2}(-\pi(\tilde{t}_{2}))\right)\left(\pi^{*}(\pi(\tilde{t}_{2})-2\tilde{t}_{2}\right)\otimes\left(\pi^{*}A_{1}^{-2}(\pi(\tilde{t}_{1})\right)\left(-\pi^{*}(\pi(\tilde{t}_{1})+2\tilde{t}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})-\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})-\tilde{t}_{2}),

where, as usual,
\tau:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}
denotes the covering
involution for the map
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
.

Thus either
\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
and

\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})+\tilde{t}_{2}
are equal as divisors or

\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
and
\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})+\tilde{t}_{2}
span a
g^{1}_{2}
linear system on
{\widetilde{C}}
, that is

\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
and
\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})+\tilde{t}_{2}
are disjoint divisors in the hyperelliptic linear
system on
{\widetilde{C}}
. This gives the following possibilities

Case 1.
We have
\tilde{t}_{1}=\tilde{t}_{2}
. In this
case the equality (
5
) implies
\pi^{*}A_{1}=\pi^{*}A_{2}
. Since
\pi
is ramified, the pullback
\pi^{*}:\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
is injective, and so
A_{1}=A_{2}
.

Case 2.
We have
\tilde{t}_{1}=\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})

and
\tilde{t}_{2}=\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
, and
\tilde{t}_{1}\neq\tilde{t}_{2}
, i.e.
\tilde{t}_{1}
and
\tilde{t}_{2}
are two
distinct ramification points of
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
. This
violates the identity (
5
) which
implies that

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2})=\pi^{*}\left(A_{1}\otimes A_{2}^{-1}\right).

However the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2})
can not be a pullback of a line bundle from
C
.
Indeed, every line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on
{\widetilde{C}}
which is a pullback
from a line bundle on
C
admits a
\tau
-equivariant structure which
acts trivially on the fibers of
\mathscr{L}
at all ramification points
of
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
. On the other hand, if
\tilde{r}
is a fixed
point of
\tau
, then
\tau
preserves the ideal subsheaf

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\tilde{r})
and so the locally free sheaf

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\tilde{r})
is equipped with a canonical

\tau
-equivariant structure in which
\tau
acts as multiplication by

(-1)
on the fiber of
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\tilde{r})
at

\tilde{r}
and acts as multiplication by
1
on the of

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\tilde{r})
at any fixed point different from

\tilde{r}
. By duality
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{r})
has an
equivariant structure with the same exact property and so by tensoring
we see that for two distinct ramification points
\tilde{t}_{1}
and

\tilde{t}_{2}
we see that
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2})
has a
\tau
-equivariant structure in which
\tau

acts by multiplication by
(-1)
on the fibers at
\tilde{t}_{1}
and

\tilde{t}_{2}
and by multiplication by
1
on the fibers at the
other two ramification points. But the only other
\tau
-equivariant
structure on this line bundle will be obtained by multiplying the
given equivariant structure by the sign charater
\langle\tau\rangle\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
\tau\mapsto-1
. In either of these
structures
\tau
acts non-trivially on the fibers of

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2})
at two of the
ramification points, and so this line bundle is not a pullback of a
line bundle on
C
. This shows that in this case equation
(
5
) does not have a solution.

Case 3.
We have that
\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
and
\tilde{t}_{2}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{1})
are
disjoint divisors in the hyperelliptic linear system on
{\widetilde{C}}
. To
analyze this case better we first recall the basic diagram governing
the geometry of the curve
{\widetilde{C}}
. To construct the spectral curve

{\widetilde{C}}
we start with a quadratic differential
\beta\in H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2})
having simple zeroes and we take

{\widetilde{C}}\subset\operatorname{tot}(\omega_{C})
to be the unique double cover
of
C
branched at the zeroes of
\beta
. Let
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the hyperelliptic map. Since
\omega_{C}=\mathsf{h}_{C}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)
and it is easy to see
that every section of
\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2}
is a pullback of a
unique section in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)
. Thus the divisor
of
\beta
is the pullback of a degree two divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}}
in

\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
are two distinct
points, neither of which is a branch point of
\mathsf{h}_{C}
. In
particular, the degree
4
cover
\mathsf{h}_{C}\circ\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
factors as
{\widetilde{C}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the

, and

is
the hyperelliptic map on
{\widetilde{C}}
which we will denote by

\mathsf{h}_{{\widetilde{C}}}
. This also implies that the covering involution

for

commutes with the

and that the
composition

is a fixed point free involution
with quotient
D={\widetilde{C}}/\langle\rho\rangle
which is a smooth
hyperelliptic curve of genus
3
. All these data can be organized in
the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{{\widetilde{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{a}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{D}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}_{C}}

(6)

where the maps

, and

Therefore the divisor
\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau(\tilde{t}_{2})
is a

if and only if

. So in this case the

) becomes

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}-\rho(\tilde{t}_{1}))=\pi^{*}(A_{1}\otimes A_{2}^{-1}).

(7)

Note that the line bundle

is a

-antiinvariant line bundle on

, i.e. as a point in

it belongs to the
subgroup

\ker\left[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern \subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}}).

From the classical theory of Prym varieties

[
Mum74
]
of étale double covers, it is known that
\ker(1+\rho)=\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}
is a disconnected abelian

the identity equal to the (degree zero) Prym variety for the pair

({\widetilde{C}},D)
:

\mathsf{Prym}({\widetilde{C}},D)=\operatorname{im}\left[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.20143pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.20143pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.54417pt\raise 5.25555pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1-\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.20143pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.20143pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right].

Next consider the Abel-Prym map

\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{k}:\operatorname{Sym}^{k}{\widetilde{C}}\longrightarrow\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}}),\ \mathfrak{d}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\mathfrak{d}-\rho(\mathfrak{d})).

As explained in
[
Mum74
]
the image of this map lands in the
identity component

\mathsf{Prym}({\widetilde{C}},D)=\left(\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}\right)_{o}\subset\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}

if and only if

is even. Thus
the image of Abel-Prym map

\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{1}:{\widetilde{C}}\longrightarrow\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}}),\ \tilde{t}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}-\rho(\tilde{t})),

is contained in the non-neutral component of
\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}
and so the curve

\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{1}({\widetilde{C}})\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
is disjoint
4
4

4

In our setting we can see
this fact directly without appealing to Mumford’s parity analysis

[
Mum74
]
. Indeed, by definition the curve
D
is the double
cover of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
branched at the
8
points which are the

6
branch points of
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

and the two points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
\mathsf{Y}
denote the

\mathsf{h}_{D}:D\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, sitting over

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
\mathsf{a}^{-1}(\mathsf{Y})
consists of two
distinct points
\tilde{y},\rho(\tilde{y})
, which are both ramification points
of
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
. But in
Case 2.
above we argued
that the line bundle

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{y}-\rho(\tilde{y}))

cannot be a pullback from
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
. Thus the point

\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{1}(\tilde{t})
must belong to the

\ker\mathsf{Nm}_{\mathsf{a}}
. Since

\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{1}({\widetilde{C}})
is connected, this implies
that
\mathfrak{ap}_{({\widetilde{C}},D)}^{1}({\widetilde{C}})
is entirely contained
in the
non-neutral component.

from the two dimensional abelian subvariety

\mathsf{Prym}({\widetilde{C}},D)\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
inside

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
. But the configuration (
6
)
of double covers implies that

\mathsf{Prym}({\widetilde{C}},D)=\pi^{*}\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\ \subset\ \operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}}),

and hence the equation (
7
) has no solution.

This completes the analysis of the third case and shows that the map
(
4
) is injective. To check that the map
(
4
) also separates tangent directions, consider the
degree six version of the Prym for
({\widetilde{C}},C)
:

{\mathcal{P}}_{6}=\left\{\left.M\in\operatorname{Jac}^{6}({\widetilde{C}})\,\right|\,\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(M)=\omega_{C}^{\otimes 3}\right\}.

We have a natural multiplication-by-2 map

\mathsf{mult}_{2}:{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6},\quad M\mapsto M^{\otimes 2},

which fits in a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{mult}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{6}}

(8)

where the top horizontal map is the map (
4
), while the
bottim horizontal map
{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
is given by

\tilde{t}\mapsto\pi^{*}(\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}-\tau(\tilde{t}))
.

Now observe that for any
\tilde{t}\in{\widetilde{C}}
the divisor

\tilde{t}+\sigma(\tilde{t})
is in the hyperelliptic linear system
on
{\widetilde{C}}
. By the same token
\rho(\tilde{t})+\sigma\circ\rho(\tilde{t})=\rho(\tilde{t})+\tau(\tilde{t})
is in the hyperelliptic linear system on

{\widetilde{C}}
. Thus we have a linear equivalence

\tilde{t}+\sigma(\tilde{t})\sim\rho(\tilde{t})+\tau(\tilde{t})

and so

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}-\tau(\tilde{t}))\cong\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\rho(\tilde{t})-\sigma(\tilde{t}))=\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\rho(\tilde{t})-\tau(\rho(\tilde{t}))).

Hence the map
{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
factors through
D
. The induced
map
D\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
is given by
s\mapsto a^{*}\mathcal{O}_{D}(s)\otimes\mathsf{h}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)
for all
s\in D
. This shows that
the map
{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
factors as

\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{a}}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{aj}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{*}(-)\otimes\mathsf{h}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)\otimes\pi^{*}(\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{6}.}

The map
\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(D)\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
is an étale double cover
which is isomorphic to the quotient of
\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(D)
by the the translation
action of the
2
-torsion line bundle defining the cover
{\widetilde{C}}\to D

[
Mum74
]
. From the diagram (
6
) we see
that this
2
-torsion line bundle is given explicitly as

\mathcal{O}_{D}(\mathsf{Y}-\mathsf{Z})
, where
\mathsf{Y}
and

\mathsf{Z}
are the two Weierstrass points in
D
that sit over
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{y}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{z}}\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Since the Abel-Jacobi map
\mathfrak{aj}:D\to\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(D)
is a closed embedding, this shows that the map
D\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
embeds
D-\{\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{Z}\}
and glues

\mathsf{Y}
and
\mathsf{Z}
into a node. If we write
\underline{D}

for the iimage of this nodal curve in
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
we get that the map

{\widetilde{C}}\to\underline{D}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
is injective on tangent
spaces. But the map
\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}
is étale, and so
the composition
{\widehat{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}\to\underline{D}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
is injective on tangent spaces. From the diagram
(
8
) we see that this map also factors as
{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{6}
and so the
{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
is injective
on tangent spaces. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

3.3
The wobbly locus

We recall that Laumon defines the notion of
very stable

vector bundle as one that does not admit a non-zero nilpotent Higgs
field. Such bundles are automatically stable

[
Lau88
]
. The first two authors therefore introduced
the complementary notion of
wobbly
as a semistable vector bundle
that isn’t very stable. The
wobbly locus

{\rm Wob}\subset X
thus
consists of those polystable vector bundles that admit a nonzero
nilpotent Higgs field.

With indexation on the degree our notations for the wobbly loci are:

{\rm Wob}_{0}\subset X_{0},\;\;\;\;{\rm Wob}_{1}\subset X_{1}.

These will be the supports for the parabolic structures and
logarithmic poles of the Higgs fields for the Higgs bundles
(
1
).

In section
5
we show that the wobbly locus
{\rm Wob}_{0}

is a divisor in the
2
-theta space of
C
comprising several familiar
players in the classical geometry of the quadric line complex.
Specifically the divisor
{\rm Wob}_{0}
in
X_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}

has
17
irreducible components: the quartic Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
and its
16
trope planes
\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
,
labeled by the theta characteristics of
C
. Thus

{\rm Wob}_{0}=\mathsf{Kum}\cup\left[\cup_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}\right].

From the classical
16_{6}
configuration in the quadratic line
complex
[
GH94
]
we know that each trope plane

\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
is tangent to the Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}
along
a trope conic
\mathfrak{C}_{\kappa}
. Hence
{\rm Wob}_{0}
fails to be
normal crossing along the trope conics

\left\{\mathfrak{C}_{\kappa}\right\}_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}
.

It is also easy to characterize the components of the wobbly divisor
from the moduli point of view. The Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}
parametrizes

S
-equivalence classes of semistable bundles with polystable
representatives of the form
\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{a}^{-1}
for

\mathfrak{a}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
. The trope plane
\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}

can be canonically identified with

\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\kappa^{-\otimes 2}))=\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\omega_{C}^{-1}))
and parametrizes all bundles
that can be realized as non-split extensions
0\to\kappa^{-1}\to F\to\kappa\to 0
.

Similarly, in section
4
we show that
{\rm Wob}_{1}\subset X_{1}
is an irreducible divisor. Specifically, as mentioned in the
previous section,
{\rm Wob}_{1}
parametrizes all vector bundles
E\in X_{1}
which arise as non-split extensions
0\to A\to E\to A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0
for some point
(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
. In fact we will
see that
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is the tangent developable of the map
\mathsf{ql}:{\overline{C}}\to X_{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}
, i.e. the union of all
projective tangent lines to points in the curve
\mathsf{ql}({\overline{C}})
. This
shows that
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is also a non normal crossings divisor in

X_{1}
. It has the curve
\mathsf{ql}({\overline{C}})
as its curve of cusps.

3.4
Hecke correspondences

The
big Hecke correspondence

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\rightarrow X_{1}\times X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
is the moduli of quadruples

(E,F,(A,t),\beta)
such that
(E,F,(A,t))\in X_{1}\times X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
and
\beta:F\otimes A^{-1}\to E
is a map that fits in a
short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\otimes A^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{C}}_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We will view
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
either as a correspondence from
X_{1}

to
X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
or as a correspondence from
X_{0}
to

X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
. We will label the respective projections as

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}}

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}}

(9)

where
p=\operatorname{pr}_{X_{1}}
,
d=\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}
,
q=\left(\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}},\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}\right)
, and
b=\left(\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}},\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}\right)
. In fact the moduli

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
is a subvariety in the triple product
X_{0}\times X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
. To see this, note that when
\beta
exists for a
given point
(E,F,(A,t))\in X_{0}\times X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
, then it
is unique up to scale:
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t)
, and hence
t
, is determined as
the ratio of determinants, and then for this given
t
, the Hecke
fiber is a line in one direction, a conic in the other which excludes
any self-intersections.

In some parts of the discussion we will look at Hecke transformations
supported at a single point. In those situations we will write

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)=\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}^{-1}(\{a\})
for the preimage of

a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
and will view
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)

as a correspondence between
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
.

The (big) Hecke correspondences appeared in Chapter
2
, Sections
2.4

and
2.6
, from the synthetic viewpoint.
We will review the comparison between the synthetic and modular viewpoints in Section
6.3
.

The
big abelianized Hecke correspondence

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is the blow-up of

Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
along a copy of
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
.
As we will see in section
7.2
the
map

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}}
\textstyle{((A_{1},\tilde{t}_{1}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\pi^{*}\left(A_{2}\otimes A_{1}^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2})\right),}

is a closed embedding.
This embedding clearly preserves the second projections to
{\widehat{C}}
and
the strict transform of
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
in
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
is
isomorphic copy of
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
embedded as a subvariety of

Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
. The blow-up of
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
along this copy
of
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
is the big abelianized Hecke correspondence

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
.

The variety
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
maps to
Y_{0}\times Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
and thus gives correspondences

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}}

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}}

There is also map

g:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}},

such that altogether we get a map of abelianized and usual Hecke
correspondences.
The formulas for the maps
p^{\rm ab}
,
q^{\rm ab}
,
d^{\rm ab}
,
b^{\rm ab}
, and
g
are given in section
7.2
.

Again, most of the time, we will work with a single fiber of the
abelianized Hecke correspondence. If
\tilde{a}=(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
is a
fixed point we will focus on the fiber
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tilde{a})=\operatorname{pr}_{{\widehat{C}}}^{-1}(\tilde{a})
.

3.5
Spectral line bundles

In the abelianization strategy to the Hecke eigensheaf problem we use
spectral data on
C
to describe the eigenvalue Higgs bundle

(E,\theta)
and spectral data on
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
