D={\rm Wob}
generally
does not have normal-crossings singularities:

•

How to calculate effectively
the parabolic
c_{2}
or the parabolic Bogomolov
\Delta
-invariant?

•

How to recognize if the Higgs field
\Phi
will correspond
to a genuinely logarithmic Higgs field on a normal crossings
resolution?

Over the degree
0
moduli space
X_{0}
, the crude parabolic structure
is going to be trivial. The divisor
{\rm Wob}
then represents the
location of singularities of the Higgs field. In that case, the
strategy will be to follow the prescription in Theorem

3.13
to make a resolution of singularities of the divisor

{\rm Wob}
into a normal crossings divisor, and look for a parabolic
extension whose parabolic Chern class vanishes. In this case, the
parabolic structures will have more than two jumps so one needs to
consider filtrations of bigger length.

For the degree
1
moduli space
X_{1}
, we will be using a crude
parabolic structure with parabolic weight
\alpha=1/2
. This was
found partly by computation and partly by guessing. Our strategy for
the degree
1
case consists of passing to a ramified covering in
order to remove the singularities of
D={\rm Wob}
or transform them to
normal crossings. Although the pullback of a parabolic bundle with
arbitrary weight under a ramified covering may be difficult to
compute, in the case
\alpha=1/2
we can give a description.

Proposition 3.15
.

Suppose
\underline{{\mathcal{V}}}=({\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime},\alpha,\Phi)
is a crude
parabolic pre-logarithmic Higgs bundle on
(X,D)
with parabolic
weight
\alpha=1/2
. Suppose
g:Z\rightarrow X
is a Galois
covering from a smooth variety, such that
g
is ramified of order
2

along
B^{1}=g^{-1}(D^{\circ})^{\rm red}
over
D^{\circ}
. Then the
pullback
g^{*}(\underline{{\mathcal{V}}})
has trivial parabolic structure,
so it corresponds to a reflexive sheaf
{\mathcal{V}}_{Z}
with logarithmic Higgs
field that is described in the following way. Let
{\mathcal{U}}:={\mathcal{V}}/{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
, let
{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}:=g^{*}({\mathcal{U}})
, and use a superscript
()^{\circ}

to denote the restriction over
X^{\circ}
. Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{B^{\circ}}
be
the restriction of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\circ}
to
B^{\circ}
seen as a coherent
sheaf on
Z^{\circ}
, also equal to
{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}^{\circ}/\mathcal{I}_{B^{\circ}\subset Z^{\circ}}{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}^{\circ}
. We have an
exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\circ}_{Z}\rightarrow g^{*}{\mathcal{V}}^{\circ}\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}_{B^{\circ}}\rightarrow 0

and
{\mathcal{V}}_{Z}
is the reflexive extension of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\circ}_{Z}

from
Z^{\circ}
to
Z
. The logarithmic Higgs field

\Phi
is induced from that of
g^{*}({\mathcal{V}})
.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the definition of a pullback of parabolic
structures.
∎

3.11
Pushforward statement

In order to calculate the Hecke transforms of the local systems that
we are going to construct, we need a Dolbeault method for calculating
higher direct images. This is provided by the theory of
[
DPS16
]
,
which is based in turn on the theory of twistor
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules of
Sabbah and Mochizuki
[
Moc07a
,
Moc07b
,
Sab05
]
.

We will need to extend the discussion of
[
DPS16
]
in order to
apply it to the specific situations encountered for the Hecke
transform. Luckily, the fibers of the Hecke correspondence are

1
-dimensional, since we are dealing with moduli spaces of rank
2

bundles in this paper. In order to identify the higher direct image
local systems, it suffices to restrict to curves in the target space,
and to make things easy we can even use lines. Thus, the direct image
calculations are for maps from a surface to a curve, the main setup of

[
DPS16
]
. The present situation differs in that the horizontal
divisor typically has simple ramification points, whereas the
hypothesis of
[
DPS16
]
was to have an etale horizontal
divisor. Thus, some work needs to be done in the direction of looking
at a ramified cover of the base.

The full discussion will be deferred to Chapter

12
. In this subsection we summarize
the basic knowledge to come out of that, as needed to treat the Hecke
transforms. The setup we discuss here is therefore closely tailored to
the situations that are encountered in the applications.

Suppose
f:X\rightarrow S
is a projective morphism from a smooth
surface to a smooth curve. Suppose
D\subset X
is a reduced divisor,
each of whose components dominates
S
. Classify the points of

(X,D)
among the following kinds:

3.11.1. Types of points of
(X,D)

(a)

Points in
X-D
where
f
is smooth;

(b)

Points in
X-D
where the fiber of
f
has a simple normal crossing;

(c)

Points on
D
where
D
is etale over
S
;

(d)

Points on
D
where
f
is smooth and
D
is smooth with simple
ramification over
S
;

(e)

Points on
D
where
f
is smooth and
D
has a normal crossing such
that both branches are etale over
S
;

(f)

Other points.

Suppose we are given a normal variety
\Sigma
that we will call
(under a small abuse of notation) the
spectral
variety
, with a map
\pi_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\rightarrow X
, together with a
line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
that we will call the
spectral line
bundle
and a
1
-form
\alpha
on the smooth locus of
\Sigma
that
we will call the
tautological form
.

Set
E_{0}:=\pi_{\Sigma*}({\mathcal{L}})
. We assume that
\alpha
leads to a map

\Sigma\rightarrow T^{\vee}(X,\log D)
, implying that the tautological form
provides
E_{0}
with a Higgs field
\theta:E_{0}\rightarrow E_{0}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{X}(\log D)
.

Notice here that the image of
\Sigma
in the logarithmic cotangent
bundle might not be normal, but we look at the normalization
\Sigma

and call that the spectral variety, whence the abuse of notation
mentioned above.

We will consider two cases: the
nilpotent case
, where
the parabolic structure is trivial and the Higgs field has a nilpotent
residue along
D
coming from the ramification of
\Sigma
; and the

parabolic case
where the parabolic weights are
0,1/2
. In this case, we suppose
E_{1/2}=\pi_{\Sigma*}({\mathcal{L}}(R))
where

R\subset\Sigma
is the part of the ramification divisor of
\Sigma\to X
that sits over
D
. Note that
\Sigma
will in general have
other ramification that is not included in
R
, the hypothesis is that
all the ramification over
D
contributes to the parabolic structure.

The Higgs field is given by the spectral
1
-form
\alpha\in H^{0}(\Sigma,\Omega^{1}_{\Sigma})
We assume that
\alpha
is nonzero on
normal vectors to
R
at general points. This implies, in the
nilpotent case, that the nilpotent residues of the Higgs field are
nonzero, giving a size
2
Jordan block at each point of
R
over a
general point of
D
.

Define the
upper critical locus

\widetilde{{\rm Crit}}(X/S,E_{\bullet},\theta)\subset\Sigma

to be the closure of the zero-locus of the projection of
\alpha

to a section of relative differentials on
X/S
, from the open subset
where
\Sigma
is smooth. The
lower critical locus

{\rm Crit}(X/S,E_{\bullet},\theta)
is its image in
X
.

Let
G
denote the normalization of
\widetilde{{\rm Crit}}(X/S,E_{\bullet},\theta)
, and let
g:G\rightarrow S
be the induced
morphism. Let
j:G\rightarrow X
denote the normalization of the inclusion map.

Hypothesis 3.16
.

We make the following genericity hypotheses, in addition to the basic assumptions above.

•

The lower critical locus
{\rm Crit}(X/S,E_{\bullet},\theta)

does not contain any points of type (
3.11.1(f)
);

•

The spectral variety
\Sigma
is smooth, except that each point above
a point of type (
3.11.1(e)
) is an ordinary double point;

•

If there are points of type (
3.11.1(e)
) then we are in the nilpotent
case;

•

The upper critical locus is smooth except over points of type
(
3.11.1(e)
), in particular its normalization
G
is a smooth curve.

•

The restriction of the spectral
1
-form
\alpha
to the vertical
direction in
\Sigma
over a point of type (
3.11.1(d)
) is nonzero.

•

At each ordinary double point over a point of type (
3.11.1(e)
), the
curve
G
has two smooth branches whose tangent vectors are distinct.

Let
Q:=(G\cap R)
be the divisor in
G
that is the intersection
with
R
. The hypothesis that
\alpha
is nonzero in the vertical
direction at points of type (
3.11.1(d)
) implies that
G
is transverse
to
R
at those points, so
Q
is reduced at such points (see Lemma

12.9
).

In practice,
G
will only meet
R
over points of type (
3.11.1(d)
)
or (
3.11.1(e)
): in the nilpotent case that comes from constancy of
the Jordan form of the residue of the Higgs field along
D
, while in
the parabolic case it is a property of our setup, see Lemma

9.6
. We can however state the pushforward property without
including such an hypothesis.

The basic philosophy of the pushforward statement,
whose proof will be given in Chapter
12
,
is that the cohomology along the
fibers is calculated by localizing at the zeros of the relative Higgs field, namely
the upper critical locus. Its normalization
G
is a smooth curve destined to be the
spectral curve of the higher direct image Higgs bundle.

The contribution at a local point will come from the spectral
line bundle, tensored with the relative differentials of
X/S
. A correction is needed at
the points where
D
is ramified over the base, so the expressions for the higher direct image spectral
line bundle will include correction
terms at the divisor
Q
in addition to
{\mathcal{L}}|_{G}\otimes j^{*}\omega_{X/S}
.

The points of type
(
3.11.1(e)
) introduce a difficulty since the spectral variety is not smooth and the
upper critical locus has normal crossings. Our statement identifying the higher direct image Higgs
bundle will require, in this case, a global degree calculation—that does work out in our cases of interest.

Theorem 3.17
.

Assume the basic hypotheses explained at the start, assume that we are
either in the parabolic or nilpotent cases as described above, and
assume the hypotheses
3.16
. Then, the parabolic
Higgs bundle
(F_{\bullet},\Phi)
on
S
corresponding to the middle direct
image of the perverse sheaf associated to
(E_{\bullet},\theta)
has the
following descriptions depending on the case.

—In the parabolic case,
F_{\bullet}
has trivial parabolic structure and

F_{0}=g_{*}\left({\mathcal{L}}|_{G}\otimes j^{*}\omega_{X/S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{G}(Q)\right).

—In the nilpotent case,
F_{\bullet}
has a parabolic structure with
weights
0,1/2
and contains a parabolic subsheaf

F^{\prime}_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow F_{\bullet}
defined in the following way:

F^{\prime}_{0}=g_{*}\left({\mathcal{L}}|_{G}\otimes j^{*}\omega_{X/S}\right)

and

F^{\prime}_{-1/2}=g_{*}\left({\mathcal{L}}|_{G}\otimes j^{*}\omega_{X/S}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{G}(-Q)\right).

We have
F^{\prime}_{\bullet}=F_{\bullet}

away from the images of points of type (
3.11.1(e)
), in particular if there are no such points
then this gives the expression for
F_{\bullet}
.

If the parabolic sheaf
F^{\prime}_{\bullet}
defined as above
has global parabolic degree
0
then

F^{\prime}_{\bullet}=F_{\bullet}
and these give expressions for

F_{\bullet}
.

In both the nilpotent and parabolic cases, the Higgs field on
F_{0}
comes from the
differential form on
G
obtained by restricting the tautological
differential
\alpha
from
\Sigma
.

3.12
Chern classes

For reference below, we recall here the required facts about Chern classes.
We’ll almost always be interested in what happens up to codimension
two, so by convention—unless otherwise specified—our formulas will be
truncated at
codimension two. Thus for example we write

{\rm ch}(E)=r(E)+c_{1}(E)+(c_{1}(E)^{2}/2-c_{2}(E).

In the other direction,

c_{2}=c_{1}^{2}/2-{\rm ch}_{2}.

An important invariant is Bogomolov’s discriminant

[
Bog78
,
Bog94
]

\Delta=\frac{1}{2r}c_{1}^{2}-ch_{2}=c_{2}-\frac{r-1}{2r}c_{1}^{2},

having the property that
\Delta(E\otimes L)=\Delta(E)

for a line bundle
E
.

The same hold for parabolic Chern classes.

Suppose
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
is a map. Then the
Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch formula says that for a coherent sheaf
L

on
Y
we have

{\rm ch}(\pi_{*}(L))=\pi_{*}({\rm td}(Y/X)\cdot{\rm ch}(L)),

where the relative Todd class is

{\rm td}(Y/X)={\rm td}(TY)\pi^{*}{\rm td}(TX)^{-1}

with

{\rm td}(TY)=1+\frac{c_{1}(TY)}{2}+\frac{c_{1}(TY)^{2}+c_{2}(TY)}{12}

and similarly for
{\rm td}(TX)
.

We will specialize to the main cases that arise in our study: projective
space, the intersection of two quadrics in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
, and blow-ups
of abelian varieties.

Projective spaces:
Let us consider the case of
projective space. Let
H
denote the
hyperplane class on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
, and as stated above by
convention we truncate to codimension
2
. The Euler exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1)^{n+1}\rightarrow T{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\rightarrow 0

gives

{\rm ch}(T{\mathbb{P}}^{n})=n+(n+1)H+\frac{n+1}{2}H^{2}

so

c_{1}(T{\mathbb{P}}^{n})=(n+1)H,\;\;\;\;c_{2}(T{\mathbb{P}}^{n})=\frac{n^{2}+n}{2}H^{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle{\rm td}(T{\mathbb{P}}^{n})

\displaystyle=1+c_{1}/2+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2})/12

\displaystyle=1+\frac{n+1}{2}H+\frac{3n^{2}+5n+2}{24}H^{2}.

For
X={\mathbb{P}}^{3}
this gives

{\rm td}(TX)=1+2H+\frac{11}{6}H^{2}.

Intersection of quadrics:
Suppose
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
a
complete intersection of two quadrics. Let
H
also denote the
restriction of the hyperplane class of
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
to
X
. The normal
bundle of
X
is
N_{X/{\mathbb{P}}^{5}}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(2)^{2}
so the exact sequence

0\rightarrow TX\rightarrow T{\mathbb{P}}^{5}|_{X}\rightarrow N_{X/{\mathbb{P}}^{5}}\rightarrow 0

gives

\displaystyle{\rm ch}(TX)

\displaystyle={\rm ch}(T{\mathbb{P}}^{5})-2{\rm exp}(2H)

\displaystyle=5+6H+3H^{2}-2-4H-4H^{2}

\displaystyle=3+2H-H^{2}.

We get

c_{1}(TX)=2H,\;\;\;\;c_{2}(TX)=3H^{2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle{\rm td}(TX)

\displaystyle=1+c_{1}/2+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2})/12

\displaystyle=1+H+7H^{2}/12.

Blow-ups of Pryms:
Suppose

{\mathcal{P}}
is a
3
-dimensional abelian variety, so its tangent
sheaf has trivial Chern classes, and we let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}:Y\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}
be
the blow-up along a smooth subvariety
A\subset{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
E\subset Y
denote the exceptional divisor. There are two cases of interest.

Suppose first
A
is a disjoint collection of
a
points.
Then
E
is a disjoint collection of
a
planes
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.
A point
y\in E
corresponds to a tangent direction
at the corresponding image point
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(y)\in{\mathcal{P}}
, and the
tangent vectors on
Y
at
y
are vectors that are constrained
to map into this tangent direction in
T_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(y)}{\mathcal{P}}
.
Over
E
there is the universal subbundle

{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1)={\mathcal{O}}_{E}(E)
,
fitting into an exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(E)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}}|_{E}\rightarrow R\rightarrow 0

where the middle is a trivial bundle.
Then, letting
R_{Y}
be
R
viewed
as a coherent sheaf on
Y
supported
on
E
, we then have an exact sequence

0\rightarrow TY\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow R_{Y}\rightarrow 0.

We have

{\rm ch}(R_{Y})={\rm ch}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}}){\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E})-{\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(E)){\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E}).

Now
{\rm ch}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}})=3
since the tangent sheaf of
{\mathcal{P}}

is trivial of rank
3
. The exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(-E)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{E}\rightarrow 0

gives

{\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E})=E-E^{2}/2,

so we get

\displaystyle{\rm ch}(R_{Y})

\displaystyle=(3-(1+E+E^{2}/2))(E-E^{2}/2)

\displaystyle=2E-2E^{2}.

Putting this into the other exact sequence we get

{\rm ch}(TY)=3-2E+2E^{2}.

We note that the shape of
this formula is independent of the number
a
of
points that were blown up (but of course the
full exceptional divisor
E
contains that information).

Turn now to the other case: when
A
is a smooth irreducible
curve. The normal bundle
N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}
is a rank
2
vector bundle
over
A
. Let
\delta
denote the degree of the normal bundle,
i.e. the degree of its determinant line bundle. A point
y\in E

corresponds to a normal direction to
A
at
b(y)
, so as before the
tangent vectors to
Y
at
y
are constrained to have image in

T_{b(y)}{\mathcal{P}}
that maps into this normal direction under the map

T{\mathcal{P}}|_{A}\rightarrow N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1)
denote the
tautological sub-bundle over
E
. It fits into an exact sequence

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1)\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}\rightarrow R\rightarrow 0

and as before, if
R_{Y}
denotes the corresponding coherent
sheaf on
Y
supported over
E
, then we have
the exact sequence

0\rightarrow TY\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}}\rightarrow R_{Y}\rightarrow 0.

The tautological subbundle is also the normal bundle
to
E
in
Y
, thus
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(-1)={\mathcal{O}}_{E}(E)
as in the
previous case.

The rank
2
bundle
N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}
over the curve

A
is determined, up to rational numerical equivalence,
by its degree that we’ll call
\delta
.

Suppose
G
is an ample divisor class on
{\mathcal{P}}
, then

G\cdot A
is the degree of
G
restricted to
A
. Working
numerically, we can write

{\rm det}N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}\sim\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G|_{A}

so
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}}
, considered as a
coherent sheaf on
Y
, is rationally
numerically equivalent to the expression
(with a fractional divisor)

(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{Y})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
. This gives

{\rm ch}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}N_{A/{\mathcal{P}}})=\left(2+\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G\right){\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E}).

Here we can truncate the expression on the left at codimension

1
because it is multiplied by

{\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E})
of rank
0
.

Recall as above that
{\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E})=E-E^{2}/2
.
Similarly
{\rm ch}({\mathcal{O}}_{E}(E))=E+E^{2}/2
and from
the exact sequence,

\displaystyle{\rm ch}(R_{Y})

\displaystyle=\left(2+\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G\right)\cdot(E-E^{2}/2)-E-E^{2}/2

\displaystyle=E-\frac{3}{2}E^{2}+\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G\cdot E.

From the other exact sequence and recalling that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^{*}T{\mathcal{P}}

is trivial of rank
3
we get

{\rm ch}(TY)=3-E+\frac{3}{2}E^{2}-\frac{\delta}{G\cdot A}G\cdot E.

The last term could perhaps more easily be understood
as a sum of fibers: let
\mathsf{fib}
denote the numerical
class of a fiber of the projection
E\rightarrow A
, viewed
as a codimension
2
class on
Y
. We have
(writing also
G
for its pullback to
Y
)

\mathsf{fib}=\frac{1}{G\cdot A}G\cdot E

so we can also write

{\rm ch}(TY)=3-E+\frac{3}{2}E^{2}-\delta\cdot\mathsf{fib}.

we get

\displaystyle c_{1}(TY)

\displaystyle=-E,

\displaystyle c_{2}(TY)

\displaystyle=\delta\cdot\mathsf{fib}-E^{2},

and

\displaystyle{\rm td}(TY)

\displaystyle=1+c_{1}/2+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2})/12

\displaystyle=1-E/2+(\delta/12)\cdot{\rm fib}.

4
The degree one moduli space

For odd degree we have fixed the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
to be the
determinant, for the chosen Weierstrass point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\in C
. For
purposes of the present section,
X:=X_{1}
is the moduli space of
stable bundles
E
with
{\rm det}(E)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Similarly,

{\rm Wob}:={\rm Wob}_{1}
denotes the wobbly locus. Recall that
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}

denotes the moduli space of Higgs bundles with determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
and trace of the Higgs field equal to zero.

Recall from Proposition
3.1
that the
2:1
spectral
covering
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow C
has four branch points

x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in C
such that
x
and
x^{\prime}
are opposite under the
hyperelliptic involution, and
y
and
y^{\prime}
are opposite under the
hyperelliptic involution. For a general point in the Hitchin base, the
resulting two points of the hyperelliptic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
are general with
respect to the choice of
C
.

In this chapter, various notations such as
X,Y,{\rm Wob}
etc. are used
without the subscript
1
since we are talking about the degree
1

moduli space. For insertion into the rest of the paper one should read

X_{1},Y_{1},{\rm Wob}_{1}
and so on.

4.1
Geometry of the wobbly locus in degree one

We use the notations of subsections
3.1
and

3.7
. In particular
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}
denotes
the fixed point locus of the
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-action on
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}
and

{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}
denotes the unstable locus in
{\mathcal{P}}
which by Theorem
3.6
coincides with the intersection
of
\mathfrak{Q}\cap{\mathcal{P}}
of
{\mathcal{P}}
with the incoming variety
\mathfrak{Q}
defined
in subsection
3.7
. The geometric description of these loci
was already given in subsections
3.1
and
3.7

but we record it again in the following Lemma for ease of reference.

Lemma 4.1
.

•

\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}
is equal to the curve
{\overline{C}}\subset\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}
, the
16
-sheeted etale covering of
C
defined in
subsection
3.1
.

•

If
{\mathcal{P}}
is a generic Hitchin fiber corresponding to spectral curve

{\widetilde{C}}
, then
{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}=\mathfrak{Q}\cap{\mathcal{P}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}
is
a smooth curve
{\widehat{C}}
that can be expressed as a fiber product

{\widehat{C}}={\widetilde{C}}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}.

In particular,
{\widehat{C}}
is a
16
-sheeted étale
covering of
{\widetilde{C}}
.

These curves have genera respectively

g_{C}=2,\;\;\;g_{{\widetilde{C}}}=5,\;\;\;g_{{\overline{C}}}=17,\;\;\;g_{{\widehat{C}}}=65.

Proof.

The spectral curve
{\widetilde{C}}
has genus
5
and is a smooth double
cover
{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow C
ramified over
4
points in two
conjugate pairs. This follows from our running genericity assumption
requiring that
{\widetilde{C}}
is not ramified over any of the Weierstrass
points of
C
. The Hitchin fiber
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}

corresponding to such
{\widetilde{C}}
is the Prym variety
{\mathcal{P}}={\mathcal{P}}_{3}
of line
bundles on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose direct image down to
C
is a rank
2

bundle of determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

The statement that
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}={\overline{C}}
was already
proven in Proposition
3.3
but we recall the argument
here for completeness. A fixed point that is in

\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}
(i.e. such that the underlying vector
bundle is not stable) is a Higgs bundle of the form
\left(A\oplus A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),\theta\right)
, such that
\theta:A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow A\otimes\omega_{C}
. Stability of this Higgs bundle means

\deg(A)\leq 0
and existence of the map implies that
\deg(A)=0
.
As
\omega_{C}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
this means that
A^{\otimes 2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for the point
t\in C
where
\theta
vanishes. This
description provides an isomorphism
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}\cong{\overline{C}}
.

The incoming variety
\mathfrak{Q}\subset\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}
consists of Higgs bundles

(E,\theta)
such that
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}(E,t\theta)\in\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}^{{\mathbb{C}}^{\times},{\rm nu}}\cong{\overline{C}}
. In subsection
3.6

we saw that the limiting map
\mathfrak{Q}\rightarrow{\overline{C}}
is well defined
and has fibers isomorphic to
{\mathbb{A}}^{3}
. We claim that

{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}=\mathfrak{Q}\cap{\mathcal{P}}

has the structure

{\widehat{C}}={\widetilde{C}}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}.

In particular, it is seen to be a genus
65
curve.

For a general Hitchin fiber the intersection
{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}
is
smooth of dimension
1
. A point of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}
is by
definition a line bundle
U
of degree
3
on
{\widetilde{C}}
such that
\pi_{*}U
has determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
but is unstable. Instability
means that there is a sub-line bundle of degree
1
that we will
denote
A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\hookrightarrow\pi_{*}U
. By adjunction we
have
\pi^{*}(A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\hookrightarrow U
, and

\pi^{*}(A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))
has degree
2
. Thus, there is a unique point

\tilde{t}\in{\widetilde{C}}
such that
U=\pi^{*}(A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))(\tilde{t})
. If
t
denotes the image of

\tilde{t}
in
C
, the determinant condition says that

A^{\otimes-2}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}+t)\otimes\omega_{C}^{-1}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

Thus,
A
solves
A^{\otimes 2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
and
(A,t)

is the corresponding point of
{\overline{C}}
. This identifies
{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}
with the image of
{\widehat{C}}={\widetilde{C}}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}
under the
map (
4
). But by Lemma
3.2
this map
is a closed embedding, and so we have
{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm unss}={\widehat{C}}
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

One can notice that the genera of the curves involved
here are of the form
4^{k}+1
. We do not know if that is
significant or not.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
D
denote the

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle over
{\overline{C}}
projectivization of the
bundle of outgoing directions. Then the map sending
a point on
{\overline{C}}
together with an outgoing direction,
to the limit in
X
of the resulting
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-orbit,
provides a map
D\rightarrow{\rm Wob}
that is finite and generically injective. Thus,
D
is the
normalization of
{\rm Wob}
.

Proof.

Given a
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
-fixed Higgs bundle
(A\oplus A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),\theta)

corresponding to
(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
, the space of outgoing directions is

{\mathbb{P}}{\rm Ext}^{1}(A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),A)
which is by definition the fiber
of
D
over
(A,t)
. As we saw in
Lemma
3.9
(b)
and we will see again by a
different argument in Proposition
4.5
below,
D\cong{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Each
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
fiber of
D\to{\overline{C}}
maps to the
space of extensions which is the general form of a line in
X=X_{1}
. Both the lines and the horizontal
{\overline{C}}
’s map to positive
degree curves, and an effective divisor on the product
D
is a
positive sum of vertical and horizontal ones, so any effective divisor
on
D
maps to a positive degree curve in
X
, in particular it is not
contracted. For generic injectivity, we note that a general wobbly
bundle
E
has a one dimensional space of nilpotent Higgs fields and
if we
\theta
is any non-zero nilpotent Higgs field, we get a well
defined line subbundle
A=\ker\theta E
and a realization of
E
as
an extension of
A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
by
A
. This determines a unque point
in
D
which shows that the map
D\to{\rm Wob}
has a rational
section. Since
D
is irreducible this implies that
D\to{\rm Wob}
is a
birational morphism. Alternatively, we can see the generic injectivity
from the geometry of the quadric line complex elucidated in
section
2.5
. Recall that at a general point
w
of

{\rm Wob}
the four lines in
X
through that point include one with
multiplicity
2
. That line corresponds to the point
A\in{\overline{C}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
, and the position of
w
on it corresponds to
the extension class, so we can recover the data of a point of
D
. It
follows that
D
is the normalization of
{\rm Wob}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The divisor class of the wobbly locus is
[{\rm Wob}]=8H
.

Proof.

We will consider lines
\ell\subset X
and show that

\ell
intersects
W
in
8
points. In the moduli point of view on

X
a line corresponds to fixing a line bundle
L
of degree
0
and
looking at the set of bundles
E
fitting into an extension

0\rightarrow L\rightarrow E\rightarrow L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow 0.

Such an extension is wobbly if there is
A\rightarrow E
and
t\in C
with
A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Assuming that
L
does not
satisfy this condition, it means that
A=L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-y)
, so

L^{\otimes 2}(2y-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})={\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)
in other words
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t-2y)
, and the pullback of the extension to
A

splits. For a choice of
(y,t)
there will be a unique extension up to
scalars, i.e. a unique point on the line, such that the pullback
extension splits, so we need to look at the number of solutions of the
equation
L^{\otimes-2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})={\mathcal{O}}(2y+t)
. This is the number of
branch points of a trigonal map
C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, which is

8
. Thus
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}
has
8
points for a general line

\ell
. We will discuss the trigonal covers more in subsection

4.5
below.
∎

The basic structure of
{\rm Wob}
is captured by the following
proposition.

Proposition 4.5
.

We have
D\cong{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and the
factor
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is naturally identified with the base
of the hyperelliptic covering
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=C/\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
. The map

D\rightarrow{\rm Wob}
identifies together pairs of points on

6
curves of the form
{\overline{C}}\times\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\}
,
yielding a nodal locus in
{\rm Wob}
. Furthermore, on the
graph of the natural projection
{\overline{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

the map
D\rightarrow X
is non-immersive, yielding a
cuspidal locus in
{\rm Wob}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{6}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
denoted the branch points
for the hyperelliptic map
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, i.e. the images
of the Weierstrass points
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{6}\in C
; also we chose

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}:=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{1}
to fix the determinant
\det(E)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
of
our stable vector bundles.

The statement that the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-fiber of
D
is naturally identified
with the hyperelliptic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
was already checked in
Lemma
3.9
(b)
but we
recall the argument here for convenience. A point in
{\overline{C}}
is a
line bundle
L
such that
L^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
is effective, or
equivalently such that
L^{\otimes 2}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for some
point
t\in C
. Thus
L
defines uniquely the point
t
.

A point in
D
is a pair
(L,\eta)
where

L\in{\overline{C}}
and
\eta\in{\mathbb{P}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)
,
defining the extension

0\rightarrow L\rightarrow E\rightarrow L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow 0.

We note that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)=H^{1}(L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))

where
t^{\prime}
is the image of
t
under the
hyperelliptic involution.
The map
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{C}

induces an isomorphism on
H^{1}
so we can write

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)\cong H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}).

The scalar multiplying this isomorphism is not canonical,
it depends on something and indeed that leads (as we
have discussed) to the degree
128
of the normal
bundle of
{\widehat{C}}
in
{\mathcal{P}}
. However, it
does give a canonical isomorphism

{\mathbb{P}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)\cong{\mathbb{P}}H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})={\mathbb{P}}^{1}.

We note that if
y\in C
then the map

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(y))

is a quotient of rank
1
corresponding to the inage
\mathsf{h}_{C}(y)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
of
y
. This is the identification between
the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
factor of
D
, and the base of the
hyperelliptic projection of
C
.

We would like to understand when two points of
D
correspond
to the same bundle. Suppose given
(L,\eta)\in D
,
corresponding to an extension as above. We look for a
new line subbundle
L_{1}\subset E
such that
L_{1}\in{\overline{C}}
. We note first of all that if
L\neq L_{1}
as subbundles in
E
,
then
there is an inclusion

L_{1}\hookrightarrow L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),

so we may write

L_{1}=L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-y).

We have

L_{1}^{\otimes 2}=L^{\otimes-2}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2y)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2y)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t-2y)

so the condition
L_{1}\in{\overline{C}}

becomes

{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t-2y)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

for some point
t_{1}\in C
. In other words,

4\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\sim t+t_{1}+2y.

One may notice that for our hyperelliptic curve,
any time that
a+b+c+d\sim 2\omega_{C}=4\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
,
we have
a+b+c+d=x+x^{\prime}+y+y^{\prime}
such that
x,x^{\prime}
(respectively
y,y^{\prime}
)
are paired under the hyperelliptic involution
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
.

Given the form
t+t_{1}+2y
, there are only two possibilities:

(i)

either
t=t_{1}
, and
t
is
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
-paired with
y
,

(ii)

or
(t,t_{1})
are
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
-paired, and
y=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}
is a Weierstrass
point on
C
.

Case (i):
If we are in the first case we get

L_{1}=L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-y)=L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t))=L^{\vee}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

and so we must have
L_{1}\cong L
, that is
L
and
L_{1}

correspond to two different embedings of the same line bundle in

E
. However, for
L_{1}\cong L
the space
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L_{1},L)
of
extensions of
L_{1}
by
L
has dimension
2
(it is
H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})
. Furthermore, the map on extension sapces

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L_{1},L)

induced by the inclusion
L_{1}\hookrightarrow L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
,
is identified with the map on cohomology

H^{1}(L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C}(t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))\to H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C})

induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}
, which is an isomorphism. Hence if
L_{1}\cong L
,
then no choice of bundle
E
admits a lifting from
L_{1}
into

E
. Therefore, case (i) does not lead to any
identification.

As we will see below, it will however lead to an identification
infinitesimally, yielding the cuspidal locus. We ignore case (i) for now.

Case (ii):
In this case, we have
y=p_{i}
and
t_{1}=t^{\prime}
.
This gives a linear equivalence
2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\sim 2y
and we get

L_{1}=L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-p_{i})=L^{\vee}\otimes\mathfrak{a}_{i}

where
\mathfrak{a}_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i})
is a
2
-torsion point. There are
six possibilities in this case. Let us look at what
is the extension
\eta
such that
L_{1}
lifts into
E
.
The extension
\eta
should be in the kernel of
the map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)\rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L_{1},L)

i.e. the map

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}(-t^{\prime}))\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}(-t^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i})).

We note that this map is isomorphic to the map

H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}_{i}))

so it means that
\eta
should be the extension
corresponding to the branch point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

We obtain
6
glueings, one for each point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}
, with the point

(L,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i})
being glued to
(L\otimes\mathfrak{a},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i})
. In
other words the surface
D
is glued to itself along each of the
6

curves
{\overline{C}}\times\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\}
,
i=1,\ldots,6
. On each curve

{\overline{C}}\times\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\}
, the glueing map is the automorphism of
{\overline{C}}

given by tensoring with the
2
-torsion point
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. This
is the first part of the statement in the proposition.

The analysis of the cuspidal locus, to conclude the proof of the
proposition, will be done later in subsection
4.4
.
∎

4.2
Computations in degree one

By convention all answers to Chern calculations are truncated to the
terms of degree
\leq 2
.

By Lemma
3.2
) and Theorem
3.6
the curve

{\widehat{C}}
embeds in
{\mathcal{P}}
and the blow-up
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}:Y\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}

of
{\mathcal{P}}
along
{\widehat{C}}
resolves the natural rational map
\pi_{*}(-):{\mathcal{P}}\dashrightarrow X
producing a finite morphism
f:Y\to X
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\subset Y
denote the exceptional locus. It is a

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle over
{\widehat{C}}
, namely
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}(N)
(this being
the projective bundle of subspaces), where
N:=N_{{\widehat{C}}/{\mathcal{P}}}
. In
fact by Lemma
3.9
(b)

we have
N=H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\otimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\pi^{*}\omega_{C}
and so
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}={\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
where
the second factor is the hyperelliptic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
for
C
.

Remark 4.6
.

The images in
X
of the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s that are fibers of
b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}:\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\rightarrow{\widehat{C}}
are lines in
X
. Indeed, these fibers are
isomorphic to the projectivized bundle of outgoing directions along

{\overline{C}}
which map to lines in
{\rm Wob}
, see Lemma
4.3
.

Let
H
denote the hyperplane class on
X
. Since
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}

is a degree
4
subvariety of dimension
3
, we get
H^{3}=4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}=f^{*}H
be the inverse image of the class
H
on
Y
.
We know that for a generic Hitchin fiber the Neron-Severi group of

{\mathcal{P}}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}
, so the Neron-Severi group of
Y
is
generated rationally by the classes
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The Todd classes for the degree
1
moduli spaces are:

{\rm td}(TX)=1+H+7H^{2}/12,

\pi^{*}{\rm td}(TX)^{-1}=1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12,

{\rm td}(TY)=1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9,

{\rm td}(Y/X)=(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9).

Proof.

Recall from Section
3.12
, the Chern class calculations for
the intersection of two quadrics
X
say

c_{1}(TX)=2H,\;\;\;\;c_{2}(TX)=3H^{2},

and

{\rm td}(TX)=1+c_{1}/2+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2})/12

=1+H+7H^{2}/12.

A computation shows that

(1+H+7H^{2}/12)(1-H+5H^{2}/12)=1,

thus

{\rm td}(TX)^{-1}=1-H+5H^{2}/12

which pulls back to the same formula with
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}
on
Y
.

For the blow-up
Y
of an abelian threefold
{\mathcal{P}}
along
the curve
{\widehat{C}}
we need to know
the degree of the normal bundle. This degree is

2g_{{\widehat{C}}}-2
for the embedding of a curve
in an abelian variety, thus by either the explicit description of
N
in
Lemma
3.9
(b)
or by the genus calculation
of Lemma
4.1
we have

\delta={\rm deg}(N)=128.

This could also be seen from the intersection number
calculations above, one can see that it is the same as
-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}
.

Again from the calculations
of Section
3.12
we have

c_{1}(TY)=-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}},

c_{2}(TY)=\delta\mathsf{fib}-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2},

and

{\rm td}(TY)=1+c_{1}/2+(c_{1}^{2}+c_{2})/12

=1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+\delta\mathsf{fib}/12.

We note that
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})|_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}
is a divisor on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
whose
intersection with a fiber is zero, indeed a fiber intersect
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
is

-1
and it intersects
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}
in
1
point since the image of a fiber
is a line contained in
X
(Remark
4.6
). Therefore
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
is a sum of fibers. The number may be calculated
as
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}
again from Remark
4.6
,
this gives

(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}=96\mathsf{fib}.

Therefore
\delta{\rm fib}=(128/96)(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}=4(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/3
.
Our formulas become

c_{2}(TY)=4(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/3-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}=(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+4\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/3,

and

{\rm td}(TY)=1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9

which completes the calculation.
∎

Next we compute the triple intersections of divisor classes on
Y
.

Proposition 4.8
.

The triple intersections of divisor classes on the degree
1
modular
spectral
covering
Y
are:

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}=32,\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}=64,\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}=32,\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}=-128.

Recall that
H
denotes the divisor class of the hyperplane section
on
X
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}:=f^{*}(H)
.
We have

\displaystyle H^{1,1}(Y)\cap H^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}},\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\rangle,\text{ and }

\displaystyle H^{2,2}(Y)\cap H^{4}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}},\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\rangle.

The corresponding groups on
X
are generated
(over
{\mathbb{Q}}
) by

H
and
H^{2}
respectively.

Lemma 4.9
.

The map
f:Y\rightarrow X
has degree
8
.

Proof.

Since over the very stable locus the map
\pi_{*}(-):{\mathcal{P}}\dashrightarrow X
is a proper morphism (see
e.g.
[
PP21
,
Zel20
,
PN20
]
it suffices to compute
the number of preimages of a very stable point in
X
under
f:Y\to X
. Choose a general hence very stable point
E\in X
. The fiber of
the cotangent bundle
T^{\vee}_{E}X
is the space

H^{0}(\operatorname{End}_{0}(E)\otimes\omega_{C})
of
\omega_{C}
-twisted
endomorphisms. The Hitchin base is the
3
-dimensional space

H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}^{\otimes 2})\cong{\mathbb{A}}^{3}
of quadratic
differentials on
C
. The map
T^{\vee}_{E}X\rightarrow{\mathbb{A}}^{3}
is the
restriction of the Hitchin map and is thus given by three quadratic
forms, so the inverse image of a general point
\mathsf{b}\in{\mathbb{A}}^{3}

is the intersection of three quadrics: it has
8
points. As
{\mathcal{P}}=\boldsymbol{h}^{-1}(\mathsf{b})
, and the exceptional divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
maps to
the wobbly locus, we have that the intersection of
Y\cap T^{\vee}_{E}X

is equal to
{\mathcal{P}}\cap T^{\vee}_{E}X
and so is this set of
8

points. Thus for a very stable
E
and a generic
{\widetilde{C}}
the inverse
image
f^{-1}(E)\subset Y
consists of
8
points.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Note that the proof
of Lemma
4.9
repeats verbatim to show that in the degree

0
case the map
f_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
has degree
8
as well.

We have
H^{3}=4
since
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
is a degree
4

subvariety. Hence,

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}=32.

We also checked that

{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})|_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}
is a sum
of
96
fibers of the map
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\to{\widehat{C}}
. In particular,
the divisor
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})
restricted to

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
, has trivial self-intersection. We get the formula

(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})=0,

or

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}=0.

Lemma 4.11
.

The wobbly locus is the image

{\rm Wob}=f(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\subset X
. The intersection of
{\rm Wob}
with a line

\ell
in
X
(in other words a subvariety
that is a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
) has
8
points.
The map
f_{|\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}:\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\rightarrow{\rm Wob}
is generically

2
to
1
, and if
D
denotes the normalization of
{\rm Wob}

(see Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.5
above)
this map factors through a
2:1
map

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\rightarrow D
.

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
4.3
that
D
can be described as the
space of downward or outgoing directions to the curve
{\overline{C}}\subset\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}
. As we saw in subsections
3.6
and
3.7

the incoming flow gives a
2:1
map
{\widehat{C}}\rightarrow{\overline{C}}
and a
normal direction to a general point of
{\widehat{C}}
maps to a downward
direction at the image point in
{\overline{C}}
. This gives the map
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\rightarrow D
. In the present case, downward flow lines are broken
only once and the broken flow line depends only on the first normal
derivative to the point of
{\widehat{C}}
in the Hitchin fiber, so the
closure of the downward flow map restricts on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
to the
composition
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\rightarrow D\rightarrow{\rm Wob}\subset X_{1}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

We saw above that
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}=32
.

A line
\ell
lying on
X
satisfies
\ell\cap H=1
, but also
(H^{2})\cap H=4
; hence in the group
H^{2,2}(Y)\cap H^{4}(Y,{\mathbb{Q}})
we have

H^{2}=4\ell.

This gives
H^{2}\cap{\rm Wob}=32
.

From the
2:1
covering property we get

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}=H^{2}\cap 2{\rm Wob}

so

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}=64.

Let us now calculate
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}
. It is the self-intersection of the
divisor class
c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))
on the surface
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
.
By the usual blowup picture the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})

is the universal subbundle for the projectivization
\mathbb{P}(N)=\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}

so the universal quotient bundle is

UQ=b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}^{*}(N)/{\mathcal{O}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}).

In terms of divisor classes on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
this gives

c_{1}(UQ)=b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}^{*}c_{1}(N)-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}).

The relative tangent bundle
T(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/{\widehat{C}})
of the map
b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}:\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\to{\widehat{C}}
is given by

T(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/{\widehat{C}})={\rm Hom}({\mathcal{O}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}),UQ),

which gives

c_{1}(T(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/{\widehat{C}}))=c_{1}(UQ)-c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))=b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}^{*}c_{1}(N)-2c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))

on the level of divisor classes.

Since
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\cong{\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the Neron-Severi group of

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
is freely generated by two classes
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}
and

\mathsf{fib}
, where
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}
is the class of

{\widehat{C}}\times\operatorname{pt}
and as before
\mathsf{fib}
is the class of

\operatorname{pt}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. In terms of these classes we have

b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}^{*}c_{1}(N)=128\mathsf{fib}\qquad\text{and}\qquad c_{1}(T(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/{\widehat{C}}))=2\hat{\mathfrak{c}}.

Therefore we get

c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))=\frac{1}{2}\left(b_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}^{*}c_{1}(N)-c_{1}(T(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/{\widehat{C}}))\right)=64\mathsf{fib}-\hat{\mathfrak{c}},

and so taking into account that
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}^{2}=\mathsf{fib}^{2}=0
and
\hat{\mathfrak{c}}\cdot\mathsf{fib}=1
we
get

c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))\cdot c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}))=(64\mathsf{fib}-\hat{\mathfrak{c}})^{2}=-128.

That was the self-intersection on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
, so it gives
on
Y
,
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}=-128
.

Now from the formula
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}=0

we get

(-128)+2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(64)=0,\mbox{ so }\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}=(128-64)/2=32.

This completes our list of intersection numbers on
Y
.
∎

4.3
Main construction

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
be the pullback to
Y
of a degree zero line bundle on

{\mathcal{P}}
, and set

{\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}:={\mathcal{L}}_{0}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\ \text{and}\ {\mathcal{V}}_{a,b}:=f_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}).

Following the notation we adopted in the definition of crude
parabolic structures in section
3.10
we will also
set

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{a,b}:={\mathcal{L}}_{0}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\ \subset\ {\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}\ \text{and}\ {\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{a,b}=f_{*}({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{a,b})\ \subset\ {\mathcal{V}}_{a,b}.

The notations are chosen so that the spectral line bundle defined
in section
3.10
is precisely
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})
. Notice that
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{a,b}={\mathcal{V}}_{a,b-1}
.

The next step is to look at the calculations of the Chern characters
of the direct image
{\mathcal{V}}_{a,b}=f_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{a,b})
. The
Chern character of
{\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}
is

{\rm ch}({\mathcal{L}}_{a,b})=1+(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})+(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2.

Putting the Todd class formula of Proposition
4.7
into the
Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch formula gives

\displaystyle{\rm ch}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})

\displaystyle={\rm ch}(f_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}))

\displaystyle=f_{*}(1+(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})

\displaystyle+(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9)

\displaystyle=f_{*}\left[1+((a-1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1/2)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})+(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2\right.

\displaystyle\left.+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}/2-(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2)(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\right].

The rational cohomology of
X_{1}
is generated by
H
in degree
2

and
H^{2}
in degree
4
, so if
a_{1}\in H^{2}(Y)
and
a_{2}\in H^{4}(Y)
are classes on
Y
we can write

f_{*}(1+a_{1}+a_{2})=8+b_{1}H+b_{2}H^{2},

for some rational numbers
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then, using
H^{3}=4
we get

4b_{1}=H^{2}\cdot f_{*}(a_{1})=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}a_{1}
and

4b_{2}=H\cdot f_{*}(a_{2})=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}a_{2}
.

We get, using the calculations of Proposition
4.8
:

H^{2}\cdot{\rm ch}_{1}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}((a-1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1/2)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})=32(a-1)+64(b+1/2)=32a+64b

so

{\rm ch}_{1}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})=(8a+16b)H.

And

\displaystyle H\cdot{\rm ch}_{2}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\left[(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2\right.

\displaystyle\left.+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}/2-(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2)(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=(a^{2}/2+5/12-a)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}+(a(b+1)+1/9+1/2-(b+1+a/2))\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle+((b+1)^{2}/2+1/9-(b+1)/2)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}

\displaystyle=16a^{2}-32a+32a+64ab+16b^{2}+-64b+16b+40/3+64/9-32+32/9

\displaystyle=16a^{2}+16b^{2}+64ab-48b-8.

We get

{\rm ch}_{2}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})=4a^{2}+16ab+4b^{2}-12b-2.

Together these prove the following:

Proposition 4.12
.

For
{\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}
in the numerical class of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})

the direct image
{\mathcal{V}}_{a,b}=\pi_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{a,b})

has Chern character (truncated to codimension
2
as usual)

{\rm ch}({\mathcal{V}}_{a,b})=8+(8a+16b)H+(4a^{2}+16ab+4b^{2}-12b-2)H^{2}.

Later we will consider a parabolic modification of
{\mathcal{L}}_{a,b}
over
the divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
, giving a new smooth parabolic structure defined
locally upstairs over the modular spectral cover or somewhat
equivalently over the projectivization of the bundle.

4.4
The cusp locus of the wobbly divisor

In this subsection we prove the part of Proposition
4.5

about the cuspidal locus. Specifically we compute the locus
D_{\rm cusp}\subset D
on which the map
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}:D\to X
is not
immersive. Recall that this map is induced from the map
f:Y\to X
. More precisely, the restriction
f_{|\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}}:\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\to{\rm Wob}\subset X
factors through the double cover
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}={\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to{\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=D
and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}:D\to X
is just the induced
map.

Let

(L,\eta)\in D={\overline{C}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a point, and
let
E=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}(L,\eta)\in X
be the corresponding rank two bundle. Our
goal is to understand when the differential

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)}:T_{D,(L,\eta)}\to T_{X,E}

has a non-trivial kernel.

Recall that
L\in{\overline{C}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
means that

L^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
is effective, i.e. that
L^{\otimes 2}\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for some point
t\in C
. Thus
L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}(t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})=\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})
,
where
t^{\prime}\in C
is the point corresponding to
t
under the
hyperelliptic involution.

Hence we have

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)=H^{1}(C,L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})).

From the long exact sequence in cohomology associated with the short
exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathcal{O}_{t^{\prime}}\to 0
it follows that the natural map

\textstyle{\iota_{t^{\prime}}:\hskip-6.0pt}
\textstyle{H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})}

is an isomorphism,
and hence we can view
\eta
as an element in
\iota_{t^{\prime}}^{-1}(\eta)\in\text{Ext}^{1}(L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),L)
. The rank two vector bundle
E=f(L,\eta)
is the extension

0\to L\to E\to L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0

given by the extension class
\iota_{t^{\prime}}^{-1}(\eta)
.

We have
T_{D,(L,\eta)}=T_{{\overline{C}},L}\oplus T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}
and so to understand the map
df_{(L,\eta)}

it is enough to understand its restrictions to the two coordinate
lines
T_{{\overline{C}},L}\oplus\{0\}
and
\{0\}\oplus T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}
in
T_{D,(L,\eta)}
.
These restrictions which will abbreviate as

\textstyle{d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{{\overline{C}},L}}:\hskip-6.0pt}
\textstyle{T_{{\overline{C}},L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{X,E}}
\textstyle{d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}:\hskip-6.0pt}
\textstyle{T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{X,E}}

have a natural modular interpretation.

4.4.1
Interpretation of

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}

Fix
L\in{\overline{C}}
and hence the points
t,t^{\prime}\in C
. The
extensions of
L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
by
L
corresponding to a varying point

\eta\in\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}))
fit in a natural
universal family parametrized by
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Indeed, consider
the surface
C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By Künneth we have

\displaystyle\text{Ext}^{1}_{C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(p_{C}^{*}L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),p_{C}^{*}L\otimes p_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1))

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C\times\mathbb{P}^{1},p_{C}^{*}\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})\otimes p_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1))

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))\otimes H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}(1))

\displaystyle\cong H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})^{\vee}.

Here in the last step we used
\iota_{t^{\prime}}
to identify

H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))
with
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})
and we used

\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))
to identify

H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}(1))
with
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})^{\vee}
.

The extension

0\to p_{C}^{*}L\otimes p_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathscr{E}\to p_{C}^{*}L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0

corresponding to the identity element in
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})^{\vee}
is a rank two bundle
\mathscr{E}
on
C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
whose restrictiion to
C\times\{\xi\}
is the extension

0\to L\to\mathscr{E}_{\xi}\to L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0

given by the class
\iota_{t^{\prime}}^{-1}(\xi)
. In particular we have

\mathscr{E}_{\xi}=E
.

Therefore
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{|\{L\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to X
can
be viewed as the classifying map for the bundle
\mathscr{E}\to C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
\text{End}_{0}(E)
denote the bundle
of traceless endomorphisms of
E
. By deformation theory we have

T_{X,E}=H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(E))
and we can identify the
restricted map

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}:T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}\to H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(E))

with the Kodaira-Spencer class of the family
\mathscr{E}\to C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at
\eta
. But tensoring a family with a fixed line
bundle does not change the Kodaira-Spencer class and so equivalently
we can compute the Kodaira-Spencer class for the family
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{E}\otimes p_{C}^{*}L(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By
definition
\mathscr{F}
is the extension

0\to\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(1)\to\mathscr{F}\to\mathcal{O}_{C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to 0

(19)

corresponding to the element

\iota_{t^{\prime}}^{-1}\in H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))\otimes H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))^{\vee}=\text{Ext}^{1}_{C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(1)),

and so is the universal family of extensions

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\to\mathscr{F}_{\xi}\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to 0,

corresponding to points
\xi\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. To simplify notation
we will denote
\mathscr{F}_{\eta}
by
F
.

Now from the exact sequence (
19
) it follows that

\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{F})=\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E})
maps naturally onto

\text{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(1),\mathscr{F})
and so we have an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(1)\to\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{F})\to\mathscr{F}\otimes(\mathcal{O}(t^{\prime})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}(-1))\to 0

which for each
\xi\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
specializes to a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\to\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi})\to\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime})\to 0.

(20)

Since the family
\mathscr{E}
encodes the variation of the bundle

\mathscr{E}_{\xi}
as the extension class
\xi\in H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))
varies, the Kodaira-Spencer class

\mathsf{ks}^{\mathscr{E}}_{\xi}:T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\xi}\to H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi}))
is captured by the maps
between cohomology of the terms in (
20
). Indeed, taking
into account that
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))=0
and

H^{0}(C,\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi}))=0
, the long exact
sequence of cohomology associated with (
20
) reads

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{0}(C,\mathscr{F}(t^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(21)

Also, in the long exact sequence associated with

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime})\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(t^{\prime})\to 0

the first edge homomorphism
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}(t^{\prime}))\to H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})
is given simply by cup product with

\iota_{t^{\prime}}^{-1}(\xi)\in H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))
. Since
\xi
is
not zero and
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}(t^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{C}
this implies
that this edge homomorphism is injective and so the natural map

H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O})\to H^{0}(C,\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime}))
is an
isomorphism. If we identify
H^{0}(C,\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime}))
with

H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O})
via this isomorphism, then the first edge
homomorphism in (
21
) becomes the cup product

\iota_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}(\xi)\cup\ :H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O})\to H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime})).

Using
\iota_{t^{\prime}}
to identify
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-t^{\prime}))
with

H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})
we then see that the long exact
sequence (
21
)
reduces to a short exact sequence

0\to H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{C}\cdot\xi\to{H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi}))}\to{H^{1}(C,\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime}))}\to 0.

But
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\mathbb{C}\cdot\xi=T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\xi}

and the first map in this sequence by construction sends an
infinitesimal deformation of the extension
\xi
to an infinitesimal
deformation of
\mathscr{E}_{\xi}
. Therefore this map is the
Kodaira-Spencer map and we can rewrite the previous sequence as

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\xi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ks}^{\mathscr{E}}_{\xi}}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(\mathscr{E}_{\xi}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\mathscr{F}_{\xi}(t^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

or in the special case
\xi=\eta
as

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(E))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,F(t^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

This gives the desired modular interpretation of

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}
, and confirms that
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}
restricted to
any ruling
\{L\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an immersion.

4.4.2
Interpretation of

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{{\overline{C}},L}}

Fix
\eta\in\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))
. The
extensions of
L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
by
L
corresponding to the fixed
extension class
\eta
and a variable
L\in{\overline{C}}
fit in a natural
family parametrized by
{\overline{C}}
. Indeed, consider the surface
C\times C
with its diagonal divisor
\Delta\subset C\times C
. Pushing down
the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)\to\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\to 0

via the projection
p_{2}:C\times C\to C
onto the second factor
gives a long exact sequence of direct images:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{p_{2*}\mathcal{O}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{p_{2*}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

We have
p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)=0
since
\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)

has negative degree along the fibers of
p_{2}
. Since
p_{2}:\Delta\to C
is an isomorphism we also have

p_{2*}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}=\mathcal{O}_{C}
and

R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}=0
. Finally by Künneth we
have
p_{2*}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{C}
and

R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}
. Substituting these in the long exact sequence
gives

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}_{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}_{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Therefore we have

p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)=0,\qquad\qquad R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}

With this in mind we compute

\displaystyle\text{Ext}^{1}_{C\times C}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(-\Delta))

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C\times C,\mathcal{O}(-\Delta))

\displaystyle=H^{0}(C,R^{1}p_{1*}\mathcal{O}(-\Delta))

\displaystyle=H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O})

where at the second step we used the Leray spectral sequence for

p_{2}
.

Therefore the class

\eta\otimes 1\in H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\otimes H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O})

corresponds to a rank two bundle
\mathbb{F}
on
C\times C
which is
an extension

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C\times C}(-\Delta)\to\mathbb{F}\to\mathcal{O}_{C\times C}\to 0.

By construction, for every point
x\in C
, the restriction

\mathbb{F}_{x}=\mathbb{F}_{|C\times\{x\}}
is the extension

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(-x)\to\mathbb{F}_{x}\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to 0

given by the class
\iota_{x}^{-1}(\eta)\in H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}(-x))
. In particular we have
\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}=F
.

Now consider the degree
16
map
C\times{\overline{C}}\to C\times C

which is the identity on the first factor and on the second factor is
the composition of the natural map
{\overline{C}}\to C
with the
hyperelliptic involution
5
5

5

The canonical map from

{\overline{C}}
to
C
assigns to each
L
the unique point
t\in C

such that
L^{\otimes 2}=\mathcal{O}_{C}(t)
. The map we consider
here sends
L
not to
t
but to its image under the hyperelliptic
involution
t^{\prime}
. Let

\mathscr{F}\to C\times{\overline{C}}
be the pull back of

\mathbb{F}
by this map, and let
\mathscr{L}\to C\times{\overline{C}}

be the Poincaré line bundle. Then the bundle

\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{\vee}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\}\times{\overline{C}})
is an extension

0\to\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{L}^{\vee}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\}\times{\overline{C}})\to 0.

For each
M\in{\overline{C}}
restricts to the bundle

\mathscr{E}_{M}:=\mathscr{E}_{|C\times\{M\}}
which is the
extension

0\to M\to\mathscr{E}_{M}\to M^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0

corresponding to the class
\eta
. In particular
\mathscr{E}_{L}=E

and the map
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{|{\overline{C}}\times\{\eta\}}:{\overline{C}}\to X
is
the classifying map for the family
\mathscr{E}\to C\times{\overline{C}}
.

This identifies the restricted differential
d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{{\overline{C}},L}}

with the Kodaira-Spencer class

\mathsf{ks}^{\mathscr{E}}_{L}:T_{{\overline{C}},L}\to H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(E))

of the family
\mathscr{E}
computed
L\in{\overline{C}}
.

The family
\mathscr{E}
differs from the family of bundles

\mathscr{F}
by a tensoring with a family of line bundles. So, even
though
\mathscr{F}
is a family of bundles with varying determinant,
the traceless parts of the Kodaira-Spencer classes for
\mathscr{E}

and
\mathscr{F}
are equal. Given a family of rank two bundles

\mathcal{F}
on
C
let us write

\kappa^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathsf{ks}^{\mathcal{F}}-(\text{tr}(\mathsf{ks}^{\mathcal{F}})/2)\cdot\text{id}
for the
traceless part of the Kodaira-Spencer class of
\mathcal{F}
. With
this notation we then have

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{{\overline{C}},L}}=\mathsf{ks}^{\mathscr{E}}_{L}=\kappa^{\mathscr{E}}_{L}=\kappa^{\mathscr{F}}_{L}.

As a last simplification, note that the map
{\overline{C}}\to C
,

L\mapsto t^{\prime}
is étale and so its differential is an isomorphism

T_{{\overline{C}},L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}T_{C,t^{\prime}}
of tangent spaces. Taking
into account that the family
\mathscr{F}
is the pullback of the family

\mathbb{F}
we conclude that modulo the isomorphism

T_{{\overline{C}},L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}T_{C,t^{\prime}}
the traceless
Kodaira-Spencer class
\kappa^{\mathscr{F}}_{L}
equals the traceless
Kodaira-Spencer class
\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}_{t^{\prime}}
.

4.4.3
The kernel of the differential

After these preliminaries we are now ready to understand where the map

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}:D\to X
is not immersive.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
(L,\eta)\in D={\overline{C}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a point, and let
E=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}(L,\eta)\in X
be the corresponding rank two bundle.
Then the differential

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)}:T_{D,(L,\eta)}\to T_{X,E}

has a non-trivial kernel if and only if
L\in{\overline{C}}
maps to

\eta\in\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}))
under
the natural degree
32
map
{\overline{C}}\to C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.
Since
d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)}
is
determined by its restricions to
T_{{\overline{C}},L}
and

T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}
and since we saw that

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)|T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta}}
is always injective, it
suffices to characterize all points
(L,\eta)\in D
for which the
image
d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)}(T_{{\overline{C}},L})
is contained in the line

d\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}_{(L,\eta)}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\eta})\subset T_{X,E}=H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(E))=H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(F))
.

In view of the modular interpretations of the restricted differentials
in sections
4.4.1
and
4.4.2
this question is
equivalent to the problem of characterizing all points
(t^{\prime},\eta)\in C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that

\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}_{t^{\prime}}(T_{C,t^{\prime}})\subset\ker\left[H^{1}(C,\text{End}_{0}(F))\to H^{1}(C,F(t^{\prime}))\right].

(22)

Here
as before
\mathbb{F}
is the extension

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{C\times C}(-\Delta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{C\times C}\to 0,}

(23)

corresponding to
\eta
and
F=\mathbb{F}_{t^{\prime}}:=\mathbb{F}_{|C\times\{t^{\prime}\}}
.

More invariantly the condition (
22
) can be
rewritten as follows. The short exact sequence
(
23
) induces a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}(-\Delta))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a\circ-\circ b}
\textstyle{\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\circ a}
\textstyle{\text{Hom}(\mathcal{O}(-\Delta),\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}(\Delta)}

(24)

Pushing this down the map
-\circ a:\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\to\mathbb{F}(\Delta)
via the second projection
p_{2}:C\times C\to C
gives us a map of coherent sheaves

R^{1}p_{2*}(-\circ a):R^{1}p_{2*}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\to R^{1}p_{2*}\mathbb{F}(\Delta).

We also have the universal traceless Kodaira-Spencer class of
\mathbb{F}

\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}:T_{C}\to R^{1}p_{2*}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F}).

Composing these two maps we get a map

R^{1}p_{2*}(-\circ a)\circ\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}:T_{C}\to R^{1}p_{2*}\mathbb{F}(\Delta),

or equivalently a global section

R^{1}p_{2*}(-\circ a)\circ\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}\in H^{0}\left(C,\Omega^{1}_{C}\otimes R^{1}p_{2*}\mathbb{F}(\Delta)\right).

(25)

Note that the bundle
\mathbb{F}
and the global section
(
25
) depend only on the class
\eta\in\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}))
. The condition
(
22
) is simply the condition that the point
t^{\prime}

belongs to the zero locus of this section. Therefore we need to
describe all pairs
(t^{\prime},\eta)\in C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
for which
the section (
25
) defined by
\eta

vanishes at the point
t^{\prime}
.

To understand these pairs observe that
\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)\subset\mathbb{F}
can be viewed as the family of filtered bundles

\mathcal{O}(-x)\subset\mathbb{F}_{x}
parametrized by
x\in C
. In
particular, the traceless Kodaira-Spencer class
\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}

will preserve the filtration. In other words if
{}^{\rm filt}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})
denotes the bundle of traceless
endomorphisms of
\mathbb{F}
preserving the subbundle

\mathcal{O}(-\Delta)
, then the global traceless Kodaira-Spencer
class
\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}
is induced from the traceless part of the
Kodaira-Spencer class

{}^{\rm filt}\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}:T_{C}\to R^{1}p_{2*}{}^{\rm filt}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})

classifying filtered deformations. This means that

\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}
factors as

\textstyle{T_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{\rm filt}\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}}
\textstyle{R^{1}p_{2*}{}^{\rm filt}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R^{1}p_{2*}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F}).}

By definition

{}^{\rm filt}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})=\left\{\varphi\in\text{End}(F)\,\left|\,b\circ\varphi\circ a=0\ \text{and}\ \text{tr}(\varphi)=0\right.\right\},

and hence

{}^{\rm filt}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})=\ker\left[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.35422pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.9826pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{b\circ-\circ a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\Delta)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right].

This implies that
R^{1}p_{2*}(-\circ a)\circ\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}

maps
T_{C}
into the subsheaf

\ker\left[R^{1}p_{2*}\mathbb{F}(\Delta)\to R^{1}p_{2*}\mathcal{O}(\Delta)\right]\subset R^{1}p_{2*}\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F}.

But from the long exact sequence of
p_{2}
direct images of the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{F}(\Delta)\to\mathcal{O}(\Delta)\to 0

it follows immediately that

\ker\left[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.35422pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{End}_{0}(\mathbb{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.9826pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{b\circ-\circ a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.35422pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\Delta)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right]=(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}.

This imlplies that the section
R^{1}p_{2*}(-\circ a)\circ\kappa^{\mathbb{F}}
in

\omega_{C}\otimes R^{1}p_{2*}\mathbb{F}(\Delta)
is in fact the tautological
section in the natural subbundle

\omega_{C}\otimes\left((H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C})\right)=(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta)\otimes\omega_{C},

i.e. is the element in

(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta)\otimes H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})=(H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta)\otimes H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})^{\vee}

corresponding to the graph of the quotient map
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})\to H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O})/\eta
. This is precisely the section of

\omega_{C}
that vanishes at the preimage of
\eta
under the
hyperelliptic map
\mathsf{h}_{C}:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Therefore the set of pairs

(t^{\prime},\eta)
we were seeking is just the graph of the hyperelliptic map
and so the set of points
(L,\eta)
at which
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{f}}
is not immersive is
the graph of the projection
{\overline{C}}\to C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
\Box

4.5
The trigonal cover of a general line

Recall from
[
New68
, Theorem 2]
,
[
NR69
, Theorem 5]
that the
variety of lines on
X
is isomorphic to the Jacobian

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
of
C
. Taking up the theme used in the proof of
Lemma
4.4
in more detail, consider a general line
\ell\subset X
. It corresponds to a degree
0
line bundle
A\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
, and in terms of vector bundles the line is the set of
isomorphism classes of nonzero extensions of the form

0\rightarrow A\rightarrow V\rightarrow A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow 0.

A point corresponding to a given extension, will lie on a
different line
\ell^{\prime}
corresponding to
A^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)

distinct from
A
,
if and only if there is a map
A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
such that the
pullback extension splits. Such a map corresponds to a point
t\in C

with
A^{\prime}=A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)
.

The splitting of the extension may be analyzed as follows. We have

{\rm Ext}^{1}(A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),A)\cong H^{1}(C,A^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))

which is dual to
H^{0}(C,A^{\otimes-2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))
. These spaces have
dimension
2
, so a nonzero element of
H^{1}(C,A^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))

modulo scalars also corresponds to a nonzero element of

H^{0}(C,A^{\otimes-2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))
modulo scalars, the unique element that
pairs with it to zero by the duality.

The extension splits when pulled back to
