theta group
of authomorphisms of the total space of
the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(2\Theta)\in\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C))
that lift the translation action of

\mathsf{J}[2]
on
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
.

In particular
\mathsf{Heisen}
acts on the vector space
H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),{\mathcal{O}}(2\Theta))\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
and this action can be
identified
[
Mum66
,
Mum08
]
with the
Schrödinger representation, i.e. with the unique irreducible
representation of
\mathsf{Heisen}
with a tautological central character. The
projectivisation of
H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),{\mathcal{O}}(2\Theta))

is the Narasimhan-Ramanan model of the moduli space of rank two
bundles with trivial determinant giving an identification
X_{0}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{3}

[
NR69
]
. The center of
\mathsf{Heisen}
acts by scalars, so the action
descends to an action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
X_{0}
.

The resulting action of
\mathsf{Heisen}
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{6}=\bigwedge^{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{4})
is
also an action for which the center of
\mathsf{Heisen}
acts by scalars and
thus again provides an action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
,
preserving and hence acting on
X_{1}
. If we view
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
as
being the group of bundles on
C
with structure group the center of

SL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
then these actions are the actions obtained by tensoring
semistable bundles with finite order line bundles that we described in
section
6.2
. It is worth noting that the action of

({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
X_{1}
can be naturally linearized on the
hyperplane bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{5}}(1)_{|X_{1}}
while the action of

({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
X_{0}
is only projective, i.e. it does not
linearize on the hyperplane bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(1)={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{3}}(1)
. Indeed, the group
\mathsf{Heisen}
and hence its subgroup

\mathsf{Heisen}^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathsf{Heisen}
both act linearly on
{\mathbb{C}}^{4}

and on
{\mathbb{C}}^{6}=\bigwedge^{2}{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
. Since the center of

\mathsf{Heisen}^{\operatorname{fin}}
acts by multiplication by
\pm 1
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
it
follows that this center acts trivially on
{\mathbb{C}}^{6}=\bigwedge^{2}{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
. In particular the action of
\mathsf{Heisen}^{\operatorname{fin}}

factrors through a linear action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{6}
. In
contrast, since
{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
is the Schrödinger representation of

\mathsf{Heisen}^{\operatorname{fin}}
, and this representation determines the
non-trivial extension class defining
\mathsf{Heisen}^{\operatorname{fin}}
, it follows
that the projective action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
can not be
lifted to a linear action on
{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
.

The natural family of moduli spaces
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}\rightarrow C
whose
fiber over
t\in C
is the moduli space
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}(t)
of stable bundles
of determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t)
, is étale locally trivial but not
globally trivial. It is obtained by dividing
X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
by
the action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
, where a
2
-torsion line bundle

\mathfrak{a}\in({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}=\mathsf{J}[2]
acts by sending a
piar
(E,(A,t))\in X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
to the pair
(E\otimes\mathfrak{a},(A\otimes\mathfrak{a},t))
.

This is similar to the family
{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\rightarrow C
of moduli spaces,
whose fiber over
t\in C
we described before, where the fiber over
t\in C
is the space
{\mathscr{X}}_{0}(t)
of bundles determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Note that while
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}
depends only on the curve
C
, the
space
{\mathscr{X}}_{0}
depends on having fixed the Weierstrass point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
.

As mentioned above, pulling back to the covering
{\overline{C}}\rightarrow C

gives a trivialization

{\mathscr{X}}_{1}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}\cong X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}.

This yields the isomorphism between
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}(t)
and
X_{1}
, through
which we pass to obtain the Hecke correspondence between
X_{0}
and

X_{1}
depending on the point
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
. We similarly have a
trivialization

{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\times_{C}{\overline{C}}\cong X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}.

If one wants to work with structure group
G={\mathbb{P}}GL(2)
, the moduli of

G
-bundles on
C
is a disjoint union
\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{M}_{0}\sqcup\mathsf{M}_{1}
. Each
\mathsf{M}_{i}
is a quotient of
X_{i}
by
the action of
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})^{4}
. The action on
X_{1}
flips the sign
of an even subset of the 6 coordinates. As the point
t
varies,

\mathsf{M}_{1}
is constant, while
X_{1}
is fixed only up to this
subgroup of its finite group of symmetries. The action on
X_{0}
is
the Heisenberg action on
\mathbb{P}^{3}=\mathbb{P}H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\mathcal{O}(2\Theta))
. Neither
X_{0}
nor
\mathsf{M}_{0}

depend on
t\in C
. In this viewpoint, the Hecke correspondence is a

5
-dimensional subvariety of
C\times\mathsf{M}_{0}\times\mathsf{M}_{1}
. Fixing
t\in C
and lifting from
\mathsf{M}_{i}
to

X_{i}
gives the Hecke correspondence between
X_{0}
and
{\mathscr{X}}_{1}(t)
,
and then fixing a lifting of
t
to
a\in{\overline{C}}
yields the Hecke
correspondence between
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
.

6.6
Hecke fibers over the nodes

The Hecke fibers over nodes of
\mathsf{Kum}
are lines in
X_{1}
counted with
multiplicity two. In this subsection we indicate their locations, in
particular they will be lines in the wobbly locus. For comparison, we
look also at the special lines on the wobbly locus that correspond to
the trope planes.

For the latter question, suppose given a line in the wobbly locus
parametrizing all non-split extensions

0\to L\to E\to L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0,

for some line bundle
L
for which
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. If

a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
is given, then the corresponding Hecke
transforms of such an
E
are of the form
E^{\prime}\otimes A
where
E^{\prime}
is
the kernel of some map
E\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{t}
. Now, for all
Hecke transforms except a special one that we will ignore here, the
bundle
E^{\prime}
will be an extension

0\to L(-t)\to E^{\prime}\to L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0.

Thus the Hecke transform contains as subbundle
U=L\otimes A(-t)
.
The equation saying that this collection is equal to a trope plane is
the equation
U^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. For
U=L\otimes A(-t)
we have

U^{\otimes 2}=L^{\otimes 2}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}(-2t)={\mathcal{O}}(q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}+t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2t)={\mathcal{O}}(q-t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

The condition that we are on a trope plane is that this bundle is
{\mathcal{O}}(-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, i.e. the equation
{\mathcal{O}}(q-t)={\mathcal{O}}
in other words,
q=t
.
Thus, we conclude that the
16
lines on the wobbly locus that
correspond to bundles whose Hecke transforms are trope planes, are the

16
lines corresponding to solutions of
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
with
q=t
.

Now to the main question: suppose given a node of
\mathsf{Kum}\subset X_{0}
,
and let us do the Hecke transformation. The Hecke transformation is
unstable if we take the polystable representative to start with, so
let us look at a bundle
E
that is an extension of
V
by itself with

V^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}
. Then the subsheaf
E^{\prime}
has as line subbundle

V(-t)
, so the Hecke transform
E^{\prime}\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
has as line
subbundle
L=V\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)
. Let us verify that this is on the
wobbly locus: we have

L^{\otimes 2}=V^{\otimes 2}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2t)={\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2t)={\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t).

We may also set
q=t^{\prime}
to be the conjugate point of
t
, and note that

{\mathcal{O}}(t+q)={\mathcal{O}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
so we can write

L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2t)={\mathcal{O}}(t+q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)={\mathcal{O}}(q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

Thus the bundle
E^{\prime}\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
being an extension containing
L

as subbundle, is on the wobbly locus. It corresponds to the line over
the point
(L,q)
of
{\overline{C}}
with
L=V\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-t)
and
q=t^{\prime}

is the conjugate point of
t
.

We now have a description of the
16
lines, that are on the wobbly
locus, that are Hecke transforms of the nodes. They are the
16
lines
corresponding to solutions of
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}(q-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
with

q=t^{\prime}
being the conjugate point of
t
.

Putting together these two collections of
16
lines, we get
32

lines on the wobbly locus that correspond to the
32
points in

{\overline{C}}
whose images in
C
are either
t
or
t^{\prime}
.

It looks like these should be the
32
lines under discussion on

[
GH94
, pp 775-77]
. In particular, the
32
lines of
[
GH94
]

should have the property that they are ‘special’ in the sense of the
definition of page 792 (i.e. being lines of the wobbly locus). This is
certainly known in the classical theory but does not seem to have been
mentioned in
[
GH94
]
.

One may also ask to describe the planes in
X_{0}
that correspond to
the second collection of
16
lines i.e. the Hecke fibers over
nodes. We think that the plane corresponding to the Hecke fiber over a
node will contain that node, and will have tangent cone that is the
tangent line to the conic (tangent cone of
\mathsf{Kum}
) at the point of the
hyperelliptic line given by the image of
t
. We do not have a proof of
that; it should follow from a closer look at the theory of the
relationship between the Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}
and its dual

[
Keu97
,
GH94
]
, but that goes beyond our present scope.

6.7
Pullbacks of wobbly divisors and ramification

Let

{\overline{W}}_{0}:=d^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{0})\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}},\;\;\;{\overline{W}}_{1}:=p^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{1})\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}

be the inverse images of the wobbly divisors in the big Hecke correspondence.

Fix a point
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
. Let

{\overline{W}}_{0}(a)
and
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
denote the fibers of

{\overline{W}}_{0}\to{\overline{C}}
and
{\overline{W}}_{1}\to{\overline{C}}
over
a\in{\overline{C}}
. We have a diagram

\textstyle{{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{0}}

Recall that the map
p{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)\rightarrow X_{1}
is a

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle. Therefore, the map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\rightarrow{\rm Wob}_{1}
is
smooth. It follows that the singularities of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
are the same
as those of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
pulled back. Namely, we have a locus of cusps and
a locus of nodes in codimension
1
.

Let
{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\mathsf{n}}\rightarrow{\rm Wob}_{1}
denote the normalization,
so
{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\mathsf{n}}={\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Similarly denote by

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{n}}
the normalization of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
, which
maps by a smooth
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-fibration to
{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\mathsf{n}}
.

We give here some statements about the wobbly from
X_{1}
pulled back
and ramifying over
X_{0}
.

6.7.1.
The map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\rightarrow X_{0}
is a proper
morphism which is a finite
16
-sheeted cover away from a codimension

2
subset of
X_{0}
.

Proof:
We have computed that the class of the
divisor
{\rm Wob}_{1}\in X_{1}
is
8H
, where
H
is the hyperplane
class in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
. This means that
{\rm Wob}_{1}
intersects each line in

X_{1}
at
8
points and each conic in
X_{1}
at
16
points. Since
the fibers of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)\to X_{0}
are the conics in
X_{1}
this
shows that
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\rightarrow X_{0}
is a map of degree

16
.
\Box

6.7.2.
Let
J=\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C))
denote the Jacobian of
C
viewed as
the moduli of lines
\ell\subset X_{1}
. Let

\Gamma=\{(x,\ell)\in X_{1}\times J\ |\ x\in\ell\}

be the incidence correspondence.
\Gamma
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle over

J
. In the interpretation of
X_{1}
as the moduli of vector bundles
of determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
a point of the Jacobian
A\in J

corresponds to the line
\ell_{A}\subset X_{1}
parametrizing all
non-trivial extensions

0\to A\to E\to A^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\to 0.

Thus the fiber
\ell_{A}
of
\Gamma\to J
over
A
is identified
canonically with the line
{\mathbb{P}}(H^{1}(C,A^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})))
. In
particular if
A\in{\overline{C}}\subset J
, then
A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
for some point
t\in C
and so we have
H^{1}(C,A^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))=H^{1}(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-t^{\prime}))
where
t^{\prime}
is the hyperelliptic
conjugate of
t
. This space in turn is equal to
H^{1}(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})

under the natural embedding
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-t^{\prime})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
. In other
words the restriction of the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle
\Gamma\to J
to the
curve
{\overline{C}}\subset J
is trivial, i.e.
\Gamma_{|{\overline{C}}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\overline{C}}
- the product of the hyperelliptic

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
{\overline{C}}
.
The projection map
\Gamma\to X_{1}
is known
[
New68
]

to be a finite morphism
of degree
4
.

Claim 6.7.3
.

(a)

For every line
\ell\subset X_{1}
there is natural line
bundle
\gamma(\ell)
of degree three giving a rational map
C\dashrightarrow\ell
. The map has a base point if and only if
\ell\subset{\rm Wob}_{1}
(
\ell
“wobbles”).

(b)

The branch divisor of the map
\Gamma\to X_{1}
contains

{\rm Wob}_{1}
, and the ramification divisor above
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is equal
to

\Gamma_{|{\overline{C}}}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\overline{C}}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times({\overline{C}}\times{\overline{C}})=({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\overline{C}})\times{\overline{C}}\to{\rm Wob}_{1}\times J\subset X_{1}\times J,

where the maps between products are the natural maps on the
components. Also,
\Gamma\to X_{1}
is simply ramified at the general
point of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\overline{C}}\subset\Gamma
.

(c)

If
\ell\subset{\rm Wob}_{1}
does not wobble, then the
intersection points
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
are the
8
branch points
for the cover
C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Proof.

We identify the curve
C
with the family of rulings of the pencil of
quadrics. A point
t\in C
Corresponds to a ruling
R_{t}
, which is a
family (whose parameter space is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
) of planes

{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
contained in quadric
Q_{\text{\sf h}(t)}
, where
\text{\sf h}:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

is the hyperelliptic map. We get a morphism
i:C\times J\to J
: if

\ell
is a line in
X_{1}
, it is contained in a unique plane of ruling

R_{t}
. The intersection of this plane with
X_{1}
equals its
intersection with the quadric
Q_{\lambda}
for any
\lambda\neq\text{\sf h}(t)
, so it consists of
\ell
plus another line
i(t,\ell)
. If
we use the Weierstrass point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
to embed
C
in
J=\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
, then this morphism becomes
i(t,\ell_{A})=\ell_{A^{\vee}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}
. For each
t\in C
, the restriction
i_{t}:J\to J
is an involution, so it has 16 fixed points, namely
\{\mathsf{a}\ |\ 2\mathsf{a}={\mathcal{O}}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}
. We see that
{\overline{C}}
is the union of these fixed loci
as the point
t
varies over
C
. On the other hand, for each
\ell\in X_{1}
, the restriction
i_{\ell}
identifies
C
with the family of
lines in
X_{1}
intersecting
\ell
. So we get a morphism
j:\Gamma\times_{X_{1}}\Gamma\to\Gamma\times C.

The fiber product
\Gamma\times_{X_{1}}\Gamma
is reducible,
consisting of the diagonal plus a 3-sheeted cover
\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma
. The image
j(\Gamma^{\prime})\subset\Gamma\times C
gives a
morphism
\Gamma\to{\rm Sym}^{3}C
which induces the desired
\gamma:J\to\operatorname{Jac}^{3}C.
For a given
\ell
, the sections of
\gamma(\ell)

map
C
to
\ell
itself. A point
x\in C
is a base point of

\gamma(\ell)
iff
\ell
is a fixed point of the involution
i_{x}
. So
as we noted above,
\gamma(\ell)
has a base point iff
\ell
is a
line contained in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. More generally, for a line
\ell\subset X_{1}
, the points where
\ell
meets
{\rm Wob}_{1}
are the points above
which two of the three points of the corresponding divisor in

|\gamma(\ell)|
come together .
∎

Corollary 6.7.4
.

The general line
\ell\subset X_{1}
intersects
{\rm Wob}_{1}
at
8

distinct points and therefore
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\to X_{0}
is
unramified over the general point of the Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}
.

The non-smooth divisor of the map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\rightarrow X_{0}

decomposes into three pieces that we will denote as

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif},\;\;\;{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm node},\;\;\;{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}

where
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm node}
is the nodal locus of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
,

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}
is the cuspidal locus,
and
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}
is the remainder of the ramification locus.

We note that the ramification locus of the map from the normalization

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{n}}\rightarrow X_{0}
will consist of the
pieces mapping to
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}
and
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}
, the latter because a cusp gives ramification. Furthermore,
the three pieces are not disjoint since they intersect in codimension

2
of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
but we ignore this aspect.

As
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
is a
3
-dimensional variety these pieces are
two-dimensional, so they map to divisors in
X_{0}
. The previous
Corollary
6.7.4
tells us that none of these images meet
the Kummer surface.

Claim 6.7.5
.

The images of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm node}

and
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}
do not contain any trope planes.

Proof.

The locus
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}
is irreducible and the locus

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm node}
has
6
pieces. On the other hand, when all
the data moves around, the trope planes are permuted in an orbit of
size
16
, so a monodromy argument in terms of our general parameters
implies that these divisors can not contain trope planes.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

Fix a point
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
.
Away from the inverse image of a codimension
2
subset of
X_{0}
,

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathsf{J}[2]}{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}_{\mathfrak{a}}

is a union of
16
divisor pieces

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}_{\mathfrak{a}}\subset{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)
each of which is a ruled surface
\mathbb{F}_{1}

mapping to the corresponding trope plane

\mathsf{Trope}_{\mathfrak{a}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
.

The map back to
{\rm Wob}_{1}
decomposes as a sum of maps (ruled surfaces)

{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm ramif}_{\mathfrak{a}}\rightarrow({\rm line})_{\mathfrak{a}}(a)\subset{\rm Wob}_{1}

where
({\rm line})_{\mathfrak{a}}(a)
is one of the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s
in the expression

{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\mathsf{n}}={\overline{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\rm Wob}_{1}

and it corresponds to the point
b=(A\otimes\xi,t)\in{\overline{C}}
.

Proof.

If
b=(B,u)\in{\overline{C}}
then the line
\{b\}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset{\rm Wob}_{1}
pulls back to an
{\mathbb{F}}_{1}
-surface in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
. Such a surface then contracts to a plane that we will denote by

{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)\subset X_{0}
. The contraction has an exceptional
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

that maps to a point we will denote by
x(b)\subset X_{0}
.

The line corresponds to bundles fitting into an extension

0\rightarrow B\rightarrow E\rightarrow B^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow 0

and the Hecke transformed bundle will be of the form
E^{\prime}\otimes A

where
E^{\prime}
is the kernel of a length
1
quotient of
E
supported on

t\in C
. In particular, the Hecke transformed bundle contains the
line bundle
B\otimes A(-t)
, and this determines the plane
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
as being the plane of semistable bundles in
X_{0}
that contain
the degree
-1
line bundle
B\otimes A(-t)
.

Let
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}
denote the resulting family of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
’s.
It is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
-bundle over
{\overline{C}}
with a dominant map to
X_{0}={\mathbb{P}}^{3}
.

The map
b\mapsto x(b)
has image a curve being the subset of
codimension
2
appearing in the statement of the proposition.
Outside of this curve,
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}
and
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{n}}
coincide. Therefore it is enough to locate the
ramification of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow X_{0}
.

The map
b\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
may be viewed as a map to the dual projective
space that we will denote by
X_{0}^{\vee}
. It factors as

{\overline{C}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{-1}(C)\rightarrow\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}\rightarrow X_{0}^{\vee}

where
\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}
is the
dual Kummer surface

appearing in the classical synthetic theory
[
Keu97
,
GH94
]
. It is the
dual surface of
\mathsf{Kum}
. The birational map sending a point to its
tangent plane blows up the nodes of
\mathsf{Kum}
and blows down the trope
conics to get the dual Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}
. We remark that an
important element of the classical theory says that
\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}
is
projectively isomorphic to
\mathsf{Kum}
although not by this birational map.

The map
\operatorname{Jac}^{-1}(C)\rightarrow\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}\subset X_{0}^{\vee}

is not an immersion, exactly over the nodes of the dual surface, and
there the tangent map vanishes. Any translation of the map
{\overline{C}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{-1}(C)
is an immersion. Thus, the map
{\overline{C}}\rightarrow X_{0}^{\vee}
is non-immersive exactly at points that go to
nodes of
\mathsf{Kum}^{\vee}
.

This implies that the family of
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
’s
becomes stationary exactly whenever the plane in question is a trope plane.
In particular,
the map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow X_{0}
is ramified along the planes in
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}

that map to trope planes.

We claim that there are
16
of these, and then we will see by a
characteristic class calculation that this accounts for all of the
ramification.

The condition that the plane
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
of vector bundles containing
the line bundle
B\otimes A(-t)
should be a trope plane, is
equivalent to saying that
B\otimes A(-t)
is the dual of a square
root of the canonical bundle. We calculate

\left[B\otimes A(-t)\right]^{\otimes 2}=B^{\otimes 2}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-2t)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(u-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}+t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}-2t)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(u-t-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

This is isomorphic to the dual of the canonical bundle exactly when

u=t
. Thus the set of choices of
b
making
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
into a trope
plane is the set of choices of square-root
B
such that
B^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. There are
16
of these.

To complete the proof, we need to see that we have accounted for all
of the ramification. Notice that there is another reason for
ramification, namely the fact that the map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{n}}\rightarrow X_{0}
factors through
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)\rightarrow X_{0}
that has a cuspidal locus. This provides a
divisor of ramification that is the inverse image of the cuspidal
locus in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
and consists of a line in each of the planes

{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
.

Let
\omega
denote here the canonical class of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}
. Its restriction to each fiber
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
is
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2}}(-3)
. Therefore, the relative canonical class of
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}
over
X_{0}
, which is
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(4)\otimes\omega
, restricts to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{2}}(1)
on each plane
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
.

We already know a divisor, the ramification coming from the cuspidal
locus, that is a line in each
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
. This therefore accounts for
any ramification that restricts to a divisor in the
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)
. The
leftover possibility is of ramification consisting of a union of
fibers. But, if the map were ramified along a fiber then the family of
planes
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}(b)\subset X_{0}
will be stationary at that fiber. Above
we saw that the cases where the planes are stationary correspond
exactly to the cases where they are trope planes. We conclude
that the ramification locus of the map
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\mathsf{p}}\rightarrow X_{0}
consists of the
16
planes corresponding to lines in

{\rm Wob}_{1}
as are described in the statement of the proposition, plus
the divisor that maps to
{\overline{W}}_{1}(a)^{\rm cusp}
.
∎

Similar analysis proves the following

Corollary 6.7.6
.

The ramification along the planes given in the proposition is simple.

7
Abelianized Hecke

7.1
Setup for abelianization of the Hecke property

Let as before
C
be our smooth genus
2
curve, and let
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\in C

be our fixed Weierstrass point. Let
(E,\theta)
be an eigenvalue

SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle for our Hecke eigensheaf
problem. That is

•

E
is a rank two algebraic vector bundle on
C

with trivial determinant.

•

\theta:E\to E\otimes\omega_{C}
is a traceless Higgs field
satisfyig the genericity condition that the spectral curve

{\widetilde{C}}\,:\,\det(\lambda\cdot\operatorname{id}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\theta)=0\ \subset\ T^{\vee}C

is smooth.

Note that this genericity condition automatically implies that the
Higgs bundle
(E,\theta)
is stable, i.e. corresponds to a flat bundle
and a polarized twistor
{\mathscr{D}}
-module on
C
by the non-abelian Hodge
correspondence
[
Sim92
]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\in\operatorname{Pic}({\widetilde{C}})
be the spectral line bundle
corresponding to
(E,\theta)
. That is
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
is a line bundle on

{\widetilde{C}}
such that

E=\pi_{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}},\quad\theta=\pi_{*}(\lambda\otimes(-)).

The spectral data
({\widetilde{C}}\subset T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}})
is the

abelianization
of the Higgs bundle
(E,\theta)
. We
used this spectral data to construct modular spectral data defininig a
tame parabolic rank
8
Higgs bundle on the moduli of semistable

\mathbb{P}SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-bundles on
C
. We already checked that
this rank
8
Higgs bundle is stabe and has vanishing first and second
parabolic Chern class, hence by Mochizuki’s theorem

[
Moc07a
,
Moc07b
]
it gives rise to a polarized twistor

{\mathscr{D}}
-module and a tame parabolic flat bundle. Now we will use the
modular spectral data to rewrite the Hecke eigensheaf property on this
rank
8
Higgs bundle in terms of abelianized information and the
abelianized Hecke correspondence. We will then explain how to use the
abelianized picture to prove that the rank
8
Higgs bundle is indeed
a Hecke eigensheaf satisfying the conditions in
Problem
6.1
.

Our strategy in constructing
(\mathscr{F}_{i,\bullet},\Phi_{i})
and
checking the eigensheaf property is to use abelianization. To
abelianize the problem we look at the
{\widetilde{C}}
-Hitchin fibers inside
the moduli of Higgs bundles
\mathsf{Higgs}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Higgs}_{1}

respectively. These are the degree
2
and degree
3
Prym varieties

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
of the cover
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.M\in\operatorname{Jac}^{2}({\widetilde{C}})\ \right|\ \mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(M)=\omega_{C}\,\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.M\in\operatorname{Jac}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\ \right|\ \mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(M)=\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\,\right\}.

We have natural rational maps

\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{0},}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{*}M}

and

\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1},}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{*}M}

(30)

which are surjective, quasifinite, and finite of degree
8
over the
very stable loci in
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
. These maps fit in the following
commutative diagrams of correspondences

\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{sum}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}\times\widehat{\pi}}
\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{diff}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}\times\widehat{\pi}}
\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}

\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}}

(31)

where
{\widehat{C}}
is the curve defined by the fiber product

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 11.77779pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-11.77779pt\raise the maps
\mathsf{sum}/\mathsf{diff}
are the sum/difference maps

\textstyle{\mathsf{sum}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3},}
\textstyle{(L,(A,\tilde{t}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t})}

\textstyle{\mathsf{diff}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2},}
\textstyle{(L,(A,\tilde{t}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\otimes\pi^{*}A(-\tilde{t})}

while the rational maps

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
and

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}

are defined by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}

\displaystyle\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}},\qquad(L,(A,\tilde{t}))\mapsto((E,E^{\prime},\beta),(A,t)),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}

\displaystyle\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}},\qquad(M,(A,\tilde{t}))\mapsto((V,V^{\prime},\gamma),(A,t))

where

\left|\ \begin{aligned} &E=\pi_{*}\left((L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1})(\tilde{t})\right),\\[-2.39996pt]
&E^{\prime}=\pi_{*}L,\\[-2.39996pt]
&\beta=\pi_{*}\left[L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1}\hookrightarrow(L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1})(\tilde{t})\right],\\[-2.39996pt]
&t=\pi(\tilde{t}).\end{aligned}\right.

and

\left|\ \begin{aligned} &V=\pi_{*}M,\\[-2.39996pt]
&V^{\prime}=\pi_{*}\left((M\otimes\pi^{*}A(-\tilde{t})\right),\\[-2.39996pt]
&\gamma=\pi_{*}\left[M(-\tilde{t})\hookrightarrow M\right],\\[-2.39996pt]
&t=\pi(\tilde{t}).\end{aligned}\right.

(32)

The modular spectral covers

Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
corresponding to
{\widetilde{C}}
are minimal resolutions

\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{o}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and

\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

of the rational maps in (
30
).
We will recall the explicit construction of these resolutions below.
The resulting covering maps
f_{i}:Y_{i}\to X_{i}
are finite of
degree
8
and we will use them to construct the Hecke eigensheaf.

Let
({\widetilde{C}}\subset T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}})
be the spectral data
abelianizing the Higgs bundle
(E,\theta)
on
C
. Our approach to
solving Problem
6.1
is to use the Fourier-Mukai
transform on the Jacobian of
{\widetilde{C}}
to convert the skyscraper sheaf

\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}}
to line bundles on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}

respectively. After that we pull back these line bundles to
Y_{0}
,

Y_{1}
, modify them appropriately along the exceptional divisors of
the blow-up maps
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
and push them forward via
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
to
get the eigensheaf: a pair of parabolic Higgs bundles

(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})

(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})

on
X_{)}
and
X_{1}
respectively, which are stable and satisfy the
vanishing Chern class conditions from Problem
6.1
.

Once the parabolic Higgs bundles
(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})

and
(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})
are constructed via this
procedure, we can use the diagram (
31
) to rewrite
the eigensheaf equation from Problem
6.1
as an
equation of line bundles. Specifically, the
(X_{1}\text{ to }X_{0})
part of the eigensheaf condition becomes an equation of line
bundles on
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
while the
(X_{0}\text{ to }X_{1})

part of eigensheaf condition becomes an equation of line bundles on

Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
. To carry this out and to show that our
Fourier-Mukai constructions satisfy the equations on
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
and
Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
we will need the abelianized Hecke
correspondence
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{ab}
which can be described either as
the minimal blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
resolving the rational
map
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
or as the
minimal blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}
resolving the rational map

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. In the next
section we will construct and analyze this minimal resolution in
detail.

7.2
The abelianized Hecke correspondence in context

Recall that the (big) abelianized Hecke space
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}

depends on the choice of a fixed spectral curve
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C

and can be viewed either as a correspondence between
Y_{1}
and

Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
or as a correspondence between
Y_{0}
and

Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
:

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}}

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}}

(33)

Here

•

Y_{0}
is the blow-up of the Prym variety

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}=\left\{\left.M\in\operatorname{Jac}^{2}({\widetilde{C}})\ \right|\ \mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(M)=\omega_{C}\,\right\},

at the
16
points
\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
,
where
\mathsf{Spin}(C)=\{\kappa\in\operatorname{Jac}^{1}(C)\,|\,\kappa^{\otimes 2}=\omega_{C}\}
denotes the set of theta
characteristics on
C
. We will write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
for the blow up morphism and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}=\sqcup_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}
for the corresponding exceptional divisor.

•

Y_{1}
is the blow-up of the Prym variety

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}=\left\{\left.M\in\operatorname{Jac}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\ \right|\ \mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(M)=\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\,\right\},

at the image of the map (
4
) which is explicitly given by

\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3},\quad(A,\tilde{t})\mapsto\pi^{*}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}).

We will write
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
for the blow up
morphism and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
for the corresponding exceptional divisor.

Note that
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
is disconnected with
16
connected components

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}
each isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. In contrast the
divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
is irreducible. In fact, as we saw in
Lemma
3.2
and Lemma
3.9
(b)
it is smooth and isomorphic to
{\widehat{C}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

We are now ready to describe the space
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
as a
common blow-up of
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
and
Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
. Namely

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is the blow up of
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}

centered at the strict transform of the surface
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
, where the inclusion
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
is given by

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}}
\textstyle{((A_{1},\tilde{t}_{1}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(\pi^{*}\left(A_{2}\otimes A_{1}^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)(\tilde{t}_{1}-\tilde{t}_{2}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2})\right).}

(34)

The fact that (
34
) is a closed embedding follows from
Lemma
3.2
since the restriction of the map
(
34
) to
{\widehat{C}}\times\{(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2})\}

is a translate of the map (
4
).

Note next that for each theta characteristic
\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)
,
the curve
\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}

is contained in the image of (
34
). Indeed, if

(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
, then
(A\otimes\kappa^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),\tilde{t})
is also in
{\widehat{C}}
, and the image of the pair
((A\otimes\kappa^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),\tilde{t}),(A,\tilde{t}))
under the map
(
34
) is precisely
(\pi^{*}\kappa,(A,\tilde{t}))\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
. This implies that the strict transform of

{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
in

Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}=\mathsf{Bl}_{\hskip-8.39996pt\underset{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\bigsqcup}\hskip-3.60004pt\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\right)

is still equal to
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
. Hence we can describe
the abelianized Hecke correspondence
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}

as the iterated blow up

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}=\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\left(Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}\right)=\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\left(\mathsf{Bl}_{\hskip-8.39996pt\underset{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\bigsqcup}\hskip-3.60004pt\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}.

Furthermore, since the centers of these succesive blowups
are smooth and nested in each other

\underset{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\bigsqcup}\hskip-3.60004pt\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}

we can also describe

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
by performing the blow ups in the opposite
order, i.e. first blow up
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
along
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
, and then blow up along the strict transforms of the curves

\pi^{*}\kappa\times{\widehat{C}}
. In other words, we also have

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}=\mathsf{Bl}_{\hskip-8.39996pt\underset{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\bigsqcup}\hskip-3.60004pt\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}.

For ease of reference we introduce notation for the exceptional
divisors of these blow ups. We let

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\subset{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
denote the exceptional
divisor of the blow up map

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
, and let

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}=\sqcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}\subset{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
denote the strict transform of the divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
. Equivalently

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}
is the exceptional divisor of the blow up map

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
is the strict transform of
the exceptional divisor of the map

\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
.

The map
q^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
is just
the blow up morphism, while the map
p^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{1}
lifts the Abel-Jacobi sum map

\mathsf{sum}:{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3},\quad\mathsf{sum}(L,(A,\tilde{t}))=L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t})

to the blow ups of the source and target. Recall that
Y_{1}=\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
is the blow up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}

centered at the curve
{\widehat{C}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
,

(A,\tilde{t})\to\pi^{*}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))(\tilde{t})
.

The preimage

\mathsf{sum}^{-1}({\widehat{C}})
of this curve under
\mathsf{sum}
is exactly
the surface
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
given by
the image of (
34
). So by the universal property of
blow ups we see that the map
\mathsf{sum}
lifts to a morphism

\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\to\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}}{\mathcal{P}}_{3}=Y_{1}

which we can further precompose with the second blow up map

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}=\mathsf{Bl}_{\hskip-8.39996pt\underset{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\bigsqcup}\hskip-3.60004pt\{\pi^{*}\kappa\}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}

to obtain the morphism
p^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{1}
.
This exhibits
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
as a blow-up of
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
and gives the first correspondence diagram in (
33
).

To realize
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
as a blow-up of
Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}

and describe the second correspondence diagram in (
33
)
note that the products
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}
are naturally isomorphic via the pair of mutually inverse maps

\textstyle{(\mathsf{diff},\operatorname{id}):\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}},}
\textstyle{(M,(A,\tilde{t}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(M\otimes\pi^{*}A(-\tilde{t}),(A,\tilde{t}))}

and

\textstyle{(\mathsf{sum},\operatorname{id}):\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}.}
\textstyle{(L,(A,\tilde{t}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(L\otimes\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t}),(A,\tilde{t}))}

But postcomposing the inclusion (
34
) with the map

\mathsf{sum}\times\operatorname{id}
gives the map

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}}\times\operatorname{id}:\hskip-42.0pt}
\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}},}
\textstyle{((A_{1},\tilde{t}_{1}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\pi^{*}(A_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))(\tilde{t}),(A_{2},\tilde{t}_{2}))}

gives the product of
{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
with
{\widehat{C}}
.
Therefore the composition

\textstyle{\operatorname{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{sum},\operatorname{id})}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}}

is simply the blow up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}
along the product surface

\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\widehat{C}}}({\widehat{C}})\times{\widehat{C}}
. Thus

\operatorname{Bl}_{{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}}\,({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}})=Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}},

which in turn identifies
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
with a blow up of

Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
.

The morphism
b^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
is again the blow-up morphism, while the morphism
d^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{0}
now lifts the difference map

\mathsf{diff}:{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
to the blow-ups of the
source and target. This describes the second correspondence in diagram
(
33
).

Observe also that the two maps
(\mathsf{diff},\operatorname{id})
and

(\mathsf{sum},\operatorname{id})
not only identify
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}
and

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
but also identify the two diagrams of
correspondences in (
31
)
when the maps between all of the other
corresponding nodes in the two diagrams are taken to be identities.

Recall next that the blown up Prym varieties
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
are
equipped with finite degree
8
morphisms
f_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0}

and
f_{1}:Y_{1}\to X_{1}
which are the minimal resolutions of the
rational maps
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}
both given by
L\mapsto\pi_{*}L
, for
L
in
either
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
or
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
. These maps realize
Y_{0}
and

Y_{1}
as the modular spectral covers of
X_{9}
and
X_{1}
which
are used to define the putative Hecke eigen Higgs bundle on

X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}
.

Following our general strategy, we will check the Hecke eigensheaf
property for this prarbolic Higgs bundle by reducing the question to
checking an abelianized Hecke eigensheaf property for the
corresponding modular spectral data. This is facilitated by the
observation that the Pryms
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, the moduli

X_{0}
,
X_{1}
of bundles, their modular spectral covers
Y_{0}
,

Y_{1}
, the classical Hecke correspondence
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
, and the
abelianized Hecke correspondence
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
can all be
organized in a single geometric context, which, for the
(X_{1}\text{
to }X_{0})
direction of the Hecke property, is most compactly
recorded in the following commutative diagram

(35)

We used
f_{0}
,
f_{1}
, and
g
to denote the resolutions of the
rational maps from the Pryms to the moduli of bundles and the Hecke
correspondence to maps from the modular spectral covers, and the
abelianized Hecke correspondence. Note that the map
g:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
is indeed a morphism because
the the maps
f_{1}:Y_{1}\to X_{1}
and
f_{0}\times\widehat{\pi}:Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}\to X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
are morphisms, and also the
map
p\times q:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times(X_{0}\times{\overline{C}})
is
a closed embedding.

Finally note that we also have a companion diagram which compactly
records all spaces and maps needed to abelianize and check the

(X_{0}\text{
to }X_{1})
direction of the Hecke property, namely

(36)

7.3
The pullback of the relative dualizing sheaf

For the verification of the Hecke property we need to understand the
line bundles
g^{*}\omega_{q}
and
g^{*}\omega_{b}
on

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
, where
\omega_{q}=\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes q^{*}\omega_{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}}^{-1}

and
\omega_{b}=\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes b^{*}\omega_{X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}}^{-1}
are the relative dualizing sheaf of
the maps
q
and
b
respectively. The key thing is to understand
the pullbacks of
\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}
,
\omega_{q}
, and

\omega_{b}
under the rational map
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. Note that the base locus of this map
has codimension at least two, and so the pullback of line bundles is
well defined.

First we have the following

Proposition 7.1
.

The Picard group of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
is

\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{\mathcal{H}}})=\operatorname{Pic}(X_{1})\times\operatorname{Pic}(X_{0})\times\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{C}})\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{C}}).

In particular there is a unique line bundle
M\in\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{C}})
so
that

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}=p^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\otimes q^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\boxtimes M).

Proof.

Recall that the map
p\times(p_{{\overline{C}}}\circ q):{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
bundle. In
moduli terms this
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle is described as follows.

The moduli space
X_{1}
has a universal bundle
\mathscr{E}\to X_{1}\times C
, satisfying
\mathscr{E}_{|\{E\}\times C}\cong E
for
all
E\in X_{1}

[
New68
,
NR69
]
. The universal bundle
\mathscr{E}

is not quite unique
6
6

6

In fact in our setting there the
universal sheaf can be normalized by further requiring that

\det(\mathscr{E})_{|X_{1}\times\{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}\}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)
. One can show that such a normalized universal
bundle is unique but we will not need this fact.
but is well defined
up to tensoring with a line bundle of the form

p_{X_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(k)
for some
k
. We also have the
normalized Poincaré line bundle
\mathsf{Poinc}\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times C
, i.e. the unique line bundle satisfying

\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{A\}\times C}\cong A
for all
A
, and

\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times\{p\}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{|\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)}
. Since the curve

{\overline{C}}=\{(A,t)\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times C\,|\,A^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}(t)\}

is embedded in
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times C
, we can consider the pulled
back line bundle
\mathscr{A}:=\mathsf{Poinc}_{|{\overline{C}}}
. Then we have

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}=\mathbb{P}\left((\operatorname{id}\times{\mathsf{sq}})^{*}\mathscr{E}\otimes p_{{\overline{C}}}^{*}\mathscr{A}\right)\longrightarrow X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}.

(37)

Also, recall that the fibers of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
map to straight lines in
X_{0}

under the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}=p_{X_{0}}\circ q:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{0}
, and so the line bundle

\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(1)
has degree one on the fibers
of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
.

Suppose
\xi
is a line bundle
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. If
\xi
has degree
k

on the fibers of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
, then

\xi\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-k)
is trivial on the
fibers of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
, and so by cohomology
and base change

({\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}})_{*}(\xi\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-k))

(38)

is a line bundle on
X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
. Since
\operatorname{Pic}(X_{1})=\mathbb{Z}
and is generated by
\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)
it follows
that the restriction of the line bundle (
38
) to any
slice
X_{1}\times\{\bar{t}\}
,
\bar{t}\in{\overline{C}}
is isomorphic to

\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(r)
for some fixed integer
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
independent of
\bar{t}
. Again by cohomology and base change we
conclude that the further pushforward of
({\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}})_{*}(\xi\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-k))\otimes p^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-r)
under the map
p_{{\overline{C}}}:X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}\to{\overline{C}}
is some line bundle
\alpha
on
{\overline{C}}
. But now the
projection formula implies that

\xi=\operatorname{pr}_{X_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(r)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(k)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}^{*}\alpha,

where
\operatorname{pr}_{X_{1}}=p
,
\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}=p_{X_{0}}\circ q
, and

\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}=p_{{\overline{C}}}\circ q
.

We can now apply this reasoning to
\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}
.
We will have

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}=\operatorname{pr}_{X_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(r)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(k)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}^{*}M.

Since the restriction of the dualizing sheaf to a smooth fiber of a
map is the dualizing sheaf of that fiber, and since the fibers of

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
map to lines in
X_{0}
we
conclude that
k=-2
. Also, a smooth fiber of the map

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
maps to a conic inside
X_{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}
, and so we conclude that
r=-1
. Therefore

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}=\operatorname{pr}_{X_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{{\overline{C}}}^{*}M,

for some line bundle
M\in\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{C}})
. This completes the proof
of the proposition
∎

Let now

\omega_{q}=\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes q^{*}\omega_{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}}^{-1}
denote the relative dualizing sheaf for the map
q
.
Then the previous proposition gives

\omega_{q}=p^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\otimes q^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\boxtimes\left(M\otimes\omega_{{\overline{C}}}^{-1}\right)\right),

and therefore

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{*}\omega_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{sum}^{*}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\right)\otimes\left(\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}\left(M\otimes\omega_{{\overline{C}}}^{-1}\right)\right).

(39)

Similarly, if
\omega_{b}=\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes b^{*}\omega_{X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}}^{-1}
denotes the relative dualizing
sheaf for the map
b
, then the formula for
\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}}
implies

\omega_{b}=d^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\otimes b^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\boxtimes\left(M\otimes\omega_{{\overline{C}}}^{-1}\right)\right),

and therefore

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{*}\omega_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{diff}^{*}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\right)\otimes\left(\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}\left(M\otimes\omega_{{\overline{C}}}^{-1}\right)\right).

(40)

Next we will compute the line bundle
M\in\operatorname{Pic}({\overline{C}})
appearing
in Proposition
7.1
and in the formulas
(
39
) and (
40
).

Lemma 7.2
.

M={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

Proof.

Let
E\in X_{1}
be a general point. Consider the
surface
P_{E}:=p^{-1}(E)\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. Then
P_{E}
is
a smooth geometrically ruled surface over
{\overline{C}}
and in fact from the

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle description (
37
) of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
we get

P_{E}=\mathbb{P}\left((\operatorname{id}\times{\mathsf{sq}})^{*}\mathscr{E}_{|\{E\}\times{\overline{C}}}\otimes\mathscr{A}\right)=\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{sq}}^{*}E\otimes\mathscr{A})\cong\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{sq}}^{*}E).

The map

\textstyle{P_{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{|P_{E}}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}

embeds
P_{E}
into
X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
by embedding each fiber of

P_{E}\to{\overline{C}}
as a straight line in
X_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Also, since
P_{E}=p^{-1}(E)
is a fiber of a map,
we have
\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|P_{E}}\cong\omega_{P_{E}}
and so
by Proposition
7.1
we have

\omega_{P_{E}}\cong\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|P_{E}}=\left(q_{|P_{E}}\right)^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\boxtimes M\right).

So we can compute
M
by computing the restrictions of

\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)
and
\omega_{P_{E}}
to some
section
\zeta:{\overline{C}}\to P_{E}
of the ruled surface
P_{E}\to{\overline{C}}
.

Any line subundle
L\subset E
of degree zero will give rise to such
a section. There are four such sububundles for a generic
E
and we
can choose any one of them. Let
L\subset E
be a line subbundle of
degree zero. Then we have a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

and
L
gives a section of
\mathbb{P}(E)\to C
which pulls back to a
section
\zeta:{\overline{C}}\to P_{E}
of
P_{E}\to{\overline{C}}
. Note that for
any point
(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
the fiber of
P_{E}\to{\overline{C}}
over

(A,t)
is canonically
\mathbb{P}(E_{t}\otimes A_{t})
and the value
of the section
\zeta
at
(A,t)
is given by the line
L_{t}\otimes A_{t}
, i.e.

\zeta(A,t)=\left[L_{t}\otimes A_{t}\right]\in\mathbb{P}(E_{t}\otimes A_{t}).

The bundle
E\otimes A
fits in a short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\vee}\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}
\textstyle{(L\otimes A)^{\vee}(t)}

By definition the map
P_{E}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{0}
sends the point

\zeta(A,t)
to the down Hecke transform of
E\otimes A
centered at
the line
(L\otimes A)_{t}\subset(E\otimes A)_{t}
, i.e. to the
locally free sheaf
\widetilde{E}
defined by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{((L\otimes A)^{\vee}(t))_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{((L\otimes A)^{\vee}(t))_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(L\otimes A)^{\vee}(t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(L\otimes A)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The third row of this diagram exhibits
\widetilde{E}
as an extension
of the degree zero line bundle
(L\otimes A)^{\vee}
by
L\otimes A

and so the bundle
\widetilde{E}
goes to the point
corresponding to the
S
-equivalence class
\left[(L\otimes A)\oplus(L\otimes A)^{\vee}\right]\in\mathsf{Kum}\subset X_{0}
.

Hence the map
\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}\circ\zeta:{\overline{C}}\to X_{0}
factors as

\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}\circ\zeta}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Kum}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(41)

Here the left vertical map
{\overline{C}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)

is given by
(A,t)\mapsto A\otimes L
, i.e. is the translation by

L
of the defining embedding of
{\overline{C}}
in
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
. The
bottom horizontal map
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\to\mathsf{Kum}
is the quotient of

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
by
(-1)
, and the composition
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\to\mathsf{Kum}\to X_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
is given by the linear
system

|2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}|
, with
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
being the theta divisor

\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}=\left\{\left.\alpha\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\ \right|\ h^{0}(C,\alpha(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\geq 1\,\right\}.

Remark 7.3
.

Strictly speaking the moduli
space
X_{0}
is isomorphic to

\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\mathcal{O}(2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})))
,
rather than

\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\mathcal{O}(2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}))^{\vee})
. Indeed,
by
[
NR69
]
the identification

X_{0}=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C),\mathcal{O}(2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})))

is
given by the map
V\mapsto D_{V}\in|2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}|
, where

D_{V}=\{\alpha\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\ |\ h^{0}(C,V\otimes\alpha(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\,\}
. To conclude that the map

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\to X_{0}
is indeed given by the linear system

|2\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}|
we use the classical fact

[
Hud05
,
Dol20
,
Keu97
]

that the embedding of the Kummer
surface in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
is projectively self-dual.

Suppose
x\in C
is a fixed point, and

\mathsf{AJ}_{x}:C\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
,

t\mapsto\mathcal{O}(t-x)
is the
x
-based Abel-Jacobi
map. Suppose
\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)
is a theta characteristic
and let
\Theta_{\kappa}=\{\alpha\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\,|\,h^{0}(C,\alpha\otimes\kappa)\geq 1\,\}
be the associated theta
divisor on
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
. By Rieman’s theorem
[
GH94
]
we have
that
\mathsf{AJ}_{x}^{*}\mathcal{O}(\Theta_{\kappa})=\kappa(x)
.
In particular, since
\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
is a theta
characteristic we get that

\mathsf{AJ}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})=\omega_{C}.

If we denote the natural embedding of
{\overline{C}}
in
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
by

\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}}:{\overline{C}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
,

(A,t)\mapsto A
, then we can write the left vertical map in the
diagram (
41
) as the composition
\boldsymbol{t}_{L}\circ\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}}:{\overline{C}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
, where for a line bundle
L
on
C
we use

\boldsymbol{t}_{L}:=L\otimes(-):\operatorname{Jac}^{k}(C)\to\operatorname{Jac}^{k+\deg L}(C)

to denote the map of tensoring by
L
. With this notation we
now have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\overline{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{sq}}}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{t}_{L}\circ\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{t}_{L^{\otimes 2}}\circ\mathsf{AJ}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{mult}_{2}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)}

(42)

But
\mathsf{mult}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})=\mathcal{O}(4\Theta_{p})
, and so we get

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{t}_{L}\circ\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}})^{*}\mathcal{O}(4\Theta_{p})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\circ\mathsf{AJ}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}^{*}\circ\boldsymbol{t}_{L^{\otimes 2}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes-2}\otimes\mathsf{AJ}_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes-2}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

But by (
41
) we have

(\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}\circ\zeta)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)=(\boldsymbol{t}_{L}\circ\boldsymbol{\imath}_{{\overline{C}}})^{*}\mathcal{O}(-4\Theta_{p}),

and hence

(\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}\circ\zeta)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

(43)

Next we need to compute
\zeta^{*}\omega_{P_{E}}
. By adjunction we have

\zeta^{*}\omega_{P_{E}}=\omega_{{\overline{C}}}\otimes\zeta^{*}\mathcal{O}_{P_{E}}(-\zeta).

Using the fact that
\zeta:{\overline{C}}\to P_{E}=\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{sq}}^{*}E)
corresponds to the
subbundle
{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}L
we compute

\displaystyle\zeta^{*}\mathcal{O}_{P_{E}}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=N_{\zeta/P_{E}}

\displaystyle=\underline{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\overline{C}}}}\left({\mathsf{sq}}^{*}L,{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}(E/L)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}(L^{\vee}\otimes L^{\vee}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))

\displaystyle={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes-2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

Hence

\zeta^{*}\omega_{P_{E}}=\omega_{{\overline{C}}}\otimes{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)=\left({\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)\otimes{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

(44)

Substituting (
43
) and (
44
) in the
the identity

\zeta^{*}\omega_{P_{E}}=(\operatorname{pr}_{X_{0}}\circ\zeta)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\otimes M

we get

{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\left(L^{\otimes 2}(-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)\otimes M,

that is
M={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.
∎

Since
\omega_{{\overline{C}}}={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\omega_{C}
we therefore get
that

\omega_{q}=p^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\otimes q^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)

and so we get a slightly simpler version of (
39
), namely:

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{*}\omega_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{sum}^{*}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\right)\otimes\left(\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

(45)

Finally, pulling (
45
) by
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
and using the commutativity of
the main diagram (
35
) we get

\boxed{g^{*}\omega_{q}=\left(p^{\rm ab}\right)^{*}f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(-1)\otimes\left(q^{\rm ab}\right)^{*}\left(f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)}

(46)

This is exactly what we will need to abelianize and check the
(X_{1}\text{ to }X_{0})
direction of the Hecke property. For
understanding the abelianization of the
(X_{0}\text{ to }X_{1})

direction we also need to rewrite (
46
) in terms of
the maps in the second correspondence diagram in (
33
). This is
straighforward. Substituting
M={\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\omega_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
in
(
40
) gives the simplified identity

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}\dashrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\right)^{*}\omega_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{diff}^{*}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow X_{0}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\right)\otimes\left(\left({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow X_{1}\right)^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right).

(47)

Finally, pulling back (
47
) via the map

(\mathsf{sum},\operatorname{id})\circ\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}

and using the commutativity of
the companion diagram (
36
) we get

\boxed{g^{*}\omega_{b}=\left(d^{\rm ab}\right)^{*}f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(-2)\otimes\left(b^{\rm ab}\right)^{*}\left(f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\boxtimes\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)}

(48)

7.4
The Hecke property via abelianization

Throughout this section we will fix a base point
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\in{\widetilde{C}}
such that
\pi(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
. Now suppose
({\widetilde{C}}\subset T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}})
is the spectral data for
(E,\theta)
,
i.e.
(E,\theta)=(\pi_{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}},\pi_{*}(\lambda\otimes(-)))
. Then
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and it determines two natural
line bundles

\mathfrak{L}_{0}\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{L}_{1}\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{3}),

where
\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\boldsymbol{t}_{-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}
, and

\mathfrak{L}_{0}
is the appropriately defined Fourier-Mukai transform
of the skyscraper sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}}
on

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
. Explicitly, consider the abelian subvariety
{\mathcal{P}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
defined by

{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{\,L\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\,\left|\,\mathsf{Nm}_{\pi}(L)=\mathcal{O}_{C}\,\right.\right\}.

This abelian subvariety comes with a natural polarization
\boldsymbol{\xi}\to{\mathcal{P}}
defined by pulling back the canonical theta line bundle
on

\operatorname{Jac}^{4}({\widetilde{C}})
via the natural map

{\mathcal{P}}\subset\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\to\operatorname{Jac}^{4}({\widetilde{C}}),\quad L\mapsto L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

Explicitly
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is defined as

\boldsymbol{\xi}=\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\Xi_{2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\right)
,
where
\Xi_{2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
is the Prym theta divisor
\Xi_{2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}:=\left\{L\in{\mathcal{P}}\ \left|\ h^{0}({\widetilde{C}},L(2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\geq 1\,\right.\right\}
.
The line bundle
\boldsymbol{\xi}
has polarization type
(1,2,2)
and the
kernel of the associated polarization homomorphism is isomorphic to

\mathsf{J}[2]

[
BNR89
]
. Concretely, the polarization homomorphism

\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
to the dual abelain variety

{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}=\operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}})
is defined by

\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee},\quad L\mapsto\boldsymbol{t}_{L}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}.

It is a surjective homomorphism of abelian varieties and
\ker\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\pi^{*}\mathsf{J}[2]
.

Since
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
are dual abelian varieties we have a
canonical normalized Poincaré line bundle

{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}

characterized by the conditions

\displaystyle{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|P\times\{\mathscr{L}\}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathscr{L},\ \text{for all }\mathscr{L}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee},\ \text{and}

\displaystyle{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}.

Let
\mathsf{FM}:D^{b}({\mathcal{P}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}D^{b}({\mathcal{P}}^{\vee})
,
F\mapsto p_{2*}\left(p_{1}^{*}F\otimes{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}\right)
denote the Fourier-Mukai transform
with kernel
{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}
. For any
L\in{\mathcal{P}}
we
have

\mathsf{FM}(\mathcal{O}_{L})={}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{L\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}

which is a line bundle of degree zero on
{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
.

With this notation we can now define the line bundles on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
,
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
which become part of the modular spectral data for the
Hecke eigensheaf.

Definition 7.4
.

For any
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
define degree zero line bundles on

{\mathcal{P}}
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
, and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
by setting

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}

\displaystyle:=\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{*}\mathsf{FM}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}\right)\ \in\ \operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{t}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}^{*}\mathfrak{L}\ \in\ \operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{t}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}^{*}\mathfrak{L}\ \in\ \operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}}_{3}).

Note:
By construction The line bundles
\mathfrak{L}
,

\mathfrak{L}_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
all depend on the Higgs bundle

(E,\theta)
or more precisely on the corresponding spectral data

({\widetilde{C}}\subset T^{\vee}C,\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}})
. We suppress the explicit
dependence on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
in the labeling of these line bundles to
avoid cluttering the notation.

We can describe these line bundles explicitly in terms of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

\mathfrak{L}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}
.

Proof.

The Poincaré line bundle
{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}
is built explicitly
starting from the bi-extension line bundle

\mathcal{Q}\to{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}},\qquad\mathcal{Q}=\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1},

where
\boldsymbol{m}:{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathcal{P}}
is the group
operation on the abelian variety
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Indeed, the translation action of
\mathsf{J}[2]
on the second copy
of
{\mathcal{P}}
in
{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
lifts to an action on

\mathcal{Q}
. Indeed, since
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is invariant under translation
by elements in
\mathsf{J}[2]
, it follows that
\mathcal{Q}
is
invariant under the translation action by elements of

\mathsf{J}[2]\times\mathsf{J}[2]\subset{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
. But the
extension class defining the theta group of
\mathcal{Q}
is given by

[
Mum08
,
BL04
]
a pairing
e^{\mathcal{Q}}

which is the exponentiation of the first Chern class of

\mathcal{Q}
. By definition the first Chern class of
\mathcal{Q}

restricts to zero on each of the two summands

H_{1}({\mathcal{P}},\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{1}({\mathcal{P}},\mathbb{Z})
in the
Künneth decomposition of the first homology of
{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
. Hence
e^{\mathcal{Q}}
restricts to the trivial character on
each of the two factors in
\mathsf{J}[2]\times\mathsf{J}[2]
. Thus the theta group of
\mathcal{Q}
splits over

\mathsf{J}[2]
which implies that
\mathcal{Q}
is equivariant
under the the translation action of

\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times\mathsf{J}[2]
, as claimed. Note
that by definition the restriction of
\mathcal{Q}
to

\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}
is canonically trivial
and we can use this trivializatio to normalize the

\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times\mathsf{J}[2]
-action on

\mathcal{Q}
by requiring that
\mathsf{J}[2]
acts
tautologically on
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}}=\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Using this normalized equivariant structure on
\mathcal{Q}
we can descend
\mathcal{Q}
to a biextension line bundle

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}\to{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}

on the quotient
{\mathcal{P}}\times({\mathcal{P}}/\mathsf{J}[2])\cong{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
. If
\mathscr{L}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
we have that

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}_{|{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\mathscr{L}\}}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{|{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\alpha\}},

where
\alpha\in{\mathcal{P}}
is any point, s.t.
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\alpha)=\mathscr{L}
. But then by the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
we have

\mathcal{Q}_{{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\alpha\}}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\alpha}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}=\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\alpha)=\mathscr{L}.

Hence

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}_{|{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\mathscr{L}\}}\cong\mathscr{L},\quad\text{for all }\mathscr{L}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}.

Also, by our normalization of the equivariant structure,

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\mathcal{O}_{Ctilde}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}
is the descent of
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}}=\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}
with respect to
the tautological
\mathsf{J}[2]
-action. Hence

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}
. This shows that

\underline{\mathcal{Q}}\cong{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc},

and so

\mathcal{Q}\cong\left(\operatorname{id}\times\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}\right).

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\operatorname{id}\times\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right)^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}\right)\right)_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{t}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}\boldsymbol{\xi}\times\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎
