Recall next that our candidate Hecke eigensheaf for the eigenvalue

(E,\theta)
was a tame parabolic Higgs bundle

(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})\sqcup(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})
on
X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}
which was
constructed from the modular spectral covers

f_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0},\quad\text{and}\quad f_{1}:Y_{1}\to X_{1}

by setting

\displaystyle\left(\mathscr{F}_{0,0},\Phi_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f_{0*}\mathscr{L}_{0},f_{0*}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\otimes(-)\right)\right),

\displaystyle\left(\mathscr{F}_{1,0},\Phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(f_{1*}\mathscr{L}_{1},f_{1*}\left(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\otimes(-)\right)\right).

Here

•

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}:Y_{0}\to T^{\vee}_{X_{0}}(\log{\rm Wob}_{0})
and

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}:Y_{1}\to T^{\vee}_{X_{1}}(\log{\rm Wob}_{1})
are the
tautological maps (defined away from the preimage of codimension two
loci in
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
) from the modular spectral covers

Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
to the logarithmic cotangent bundles of
X_{0}

and
X_{1}
with poles along the (normal crossings part of the)
wobbly divisors.

•

The modular spectral line bundles
\mathscr{L}_{0}
and
\mathscr{L}_{1}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0})(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\otimes f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\otimes f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)

and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
denote the
exceptional divisors of the blowup maps
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
.

•

\left(\mathscr{F}_{0,0},\Phi_{0}\right)
and

\left(\mathscr{F}_{1,0},\Phi_{1}\right)
are equipped with parabolic
structures along
{\rm Wob}_{0}
and
{\rm Wob}_{1}
respectively.

In chapters
9
and
8
we
used these constructions for
\left(\mathscr{F}_{i,0},\Phi_{i}\right)

together with the formula for the
L^{2}
Dolbeault pushforward of
parabolic Higgs bundles from
[
DPS16
]
and its refinements proven
in Proposition
12.7
to rewrite the Hecke condition

\displaystyle q_{*}p^{*}\left(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0}\right)\boxtimes{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}(E,\theta),

(49)

\displaystyle b_{*}d^{*}\left(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1}\right)\boxtimes{\mathsf{sq}}^{*}(E,\theta)

as a condition on the modular spectral data
(Y_{0},\mathscr{L}_{0})
and

(Y_{1},\mathscr{L}_{1})
. Namely, chasing the maps in the basic diagram
(
35
) we get that the
(X_{1}\text{ to }X_{0})

direction of the Hecke eigensheaf property (
49
) is
equivalent to the
(Y_{1}\text{ to }Y_{0})
abelianized Hecke
condition

\boxed{\begin{aligned} (p^{\rm ab})^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\otimes&f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\big)\otimes\left(g^{*}\omega_{q}\right)(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1})\\
&=(q^{\rm ab})^{*}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\otimes f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\right)\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\right],\end{aligned}}

(50)

where (
50
) is understood as an isomorphism of line
bundles on
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
which holds away from the
q^{\rm ab}
-pullback of any codimension two subvariety in
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
. In particular it suffices to prove that
(
50
) holds modulo multiples of the divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
.

Similarly, by Proposition
12.7
, the analysis of the
contribution from the singularities of the horizontal divisor to be carried
out in
section
10.2
, and the companion diagram
(
36
) we get that the
(X_{0}\text{ to }X_{1})
direction of the Hecke eigensheaf property
(
49
) is equivalent to the
(Y_{0}\text{ to }Y_{1})
abelianized Hecke condition

\boxed{\begin{aligned} (d^{\rm ab})^{*}\big(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\otimes&f_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}(2)\big)\otimes g^{*}\omega_{b}\\
&=(b^{\rm ab})^{*}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\otimes f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)\right)\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\right].\end{aligned}}

(51)

Again (
51
) should be understood as an equality of
line bundles on
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
away from the
b^{\rm ab}
-pullback of any codimension two subvariety in
Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
. In particular, it suffices to check that
(
51
) holds modulo integral combinations of the
components of the divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}
.

Substituting the formula (
46
) into
(
50
) and (
48
) we see that
the
(Y_{1}\text{ to }Y_{0})
abelianized Hecke condition
(
50
) reduces to checking that

\left((p^{\rm ab})^{*}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\right)(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1})=(q^{\rm ab})^{*}\left[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\right],\quad\text{modulo }\mathbb{Z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}.

(52)

Similarly the
(Y_{0}\text{ to }Y_{1})
abelianized Hecke condition
(
48
) reduces to showing that

(d^{\rm ab})^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\right)=(b^{\rm ab})^{*}\left[\left(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\right)\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\right],\quad\text{modulo }\sum_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\mathbb{Z}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}.

(53)

But in section
7.2
we saw that

(q^{\rm ab})^{*}\left[\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{{\widehat{C}}}\right]=(q^{\rm ab})^{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}\times{\widehat{C}})=\mathcal{O}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}),

and that

(d^{\rm ab})^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})=\mathcal{O}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}).

This shows that full exceptional divisor content cancels in both equations
(
52
) and (
53
) and so
these become equations on line bundles on
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}
and

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}
respectively. Concretely we are reduced to
checking that

\vskip-12.0pt\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\text{ in }\operatorname{Pic}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}).

(54a)

\mathsf{diff}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}=\mathfrak{L}_{1}\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\text{ in }\operatorname{Pic}({\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\widehat{C}}).

(54b)

Since
(\mathsf{sum},\operatorname{id})
and
(\mathsf{diff},\operatorname{id})
are inverse isomorphisms,
(
54a
) and (
54b
) are clearly
equaivalent. Therefore, to verify both the
(Y_{1}\text{ to }Y_{0})
and
(Y_{0}\text{ to }Y_{1})
abelianized Hecke conditions
we only need the following

Proposition 7.6
.

For
any
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
the corresponding modular spectral
line bundles
\mathfrak{L}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
satisfy the identity

\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))
in
\operatorname{Pic}({\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}})
.

Proof.

Let us first compute
\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}
. For this it will be
convenient to express
\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}
as a pullback of a
line bundle on
{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
. We have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{sum}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{t}_{-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{m}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}}

where
\boldsymbol{\jmath}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}
is given by
(A,\tilde{t})\mapsto\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})
, and
\boldsymbol{m}:{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}\to{\mathcal{P}}
is the group law on the Prym, i.e.

(L_{1},L_{2})\mapsto L_{1}\otimes L_{2}
.

Since by definition
\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\boldsymbol{t}_{-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}^{*}\mathfrak{L}
we get that

\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\left(\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}\right)^{*}\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\mathfrak{L}.

Also

\mathfrak{L}_{0}=\boldsymbol{t}_{-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}^{*}\mathfrak{L}=\left(\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}\right)^{*}p_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L},

and so it suffices to understand the line bundle

\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\mathfrak{L}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}^{-1}

on the abelian variety
{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
. By Lemma
7.5
we
have
\mathfrak{L}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}
. To simplify notation write
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\in{\mathcal{P}}
. Thus we would like to understand
the line bundle

\boldsymbol{m}^{*}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1})\otimes p_{1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}).

To simplify notation consider the
\boldsymbol{a}
-translated
biextension line bundle
{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}
on

{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}
defined by

{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}=\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}.

With this notation we now have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}

\displaystyle={}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}^{-1}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}.

In particular we get

\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\mathfrak{L}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}^{-1}=\left({}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\right)\otimes p_{2}^{*}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\theta^{-1})=\left({}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\right)\otimes p_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{L}.

On the other hand, for any
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathcal{P}}
we have

{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\boldsymbol{b}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}\right).

But
\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}})
, and so is translation invariant.
Hence

{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\boldsymbol{b}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}\cong\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}=\mathcal{Q}_{|\{\boldsymbol{b}\}\times{\mathcal{P}}}.

Similarly we have that
{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}_{|{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\boldsymbol{b}\}}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{|{\mathcal{P}}\times\{\boldsymbol{b}\}}
. So by the see-saw principle we have

{}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathcal{Q}\cong\mathcal{Q}
and hence

\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\mathfrak{L}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}^{-1}=p_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{L}.

This implies that

\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{-1}=\left(\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}\right)\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}\right)^{*}p_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{L}=\operatorname{pr}_{{\widehat{C}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\mathfrak{L}

(55)

and the proposition reduced to the following

Lemma 7.7
.

\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\mathfrak{L}=\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\right)
.

Proof.

First observe that
\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\mathfrak{L}
is a pullback of a line bundle on

{\widetilde{C}}
via the map
\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}
. Indeed, by
definition

\mathfrak{L}=\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}pw)\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right).

On the other hand, the map

\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath}:{\widehat{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}

is given by

(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath})(A,\tilde{t})=\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})).

But
\boldsymbol{\jmath}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}
is
\mathsf{J}[2]
equivariant for the
Galois action of
\mathsf{J}[2]
on the source
{\widehat{C}}
and the
translation action of
\mathsf{J}[2]=\pi^{*}\mathsf{J}[2]=\ker\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}
. Indeed we have

\boldsymbol{\jmath}\left(\mathfrak{a}\cdot(A,\tilde{t})\right)=\boldsymbol{\jmath}(A\otimes\mathfrak{a},\tilde{t})=\pi^{*}(A\otimes\mathfrak{a})^{-1}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})=\pi^{*}A^{-1}\otimes\pi^{*}\mathfrak{a}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})=\pi^{*}\mathfrak{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{\jmath}((A,\tilde{t})).

Hence

(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath})\left(\mathfrak{a}\cdot(A,\tilde{t})\right)=(\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath})(A,\tilde{t}),

and so the map
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath}
factors through

{\widehat{C}}/\mathsf{J}[2]={\widetilde{C}}
. In other words we have a comutative
triangle

\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}

for a well defiined map
\psi:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
.

In particular

\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\mathfrak{L}=\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)=\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\psi^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right).

So the question reduces to computing

\psi^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)
. This calls for a better understanding of
the map
\psi
which is a version of the Abel-Prym map.

We have an addition map

\mathbf{add}:\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times{\mathcal{P}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}}),\quad(A,L)\mapsto\pi^{*}A\otimes L,

which is a surjective homomorphism of abelian varieties with kernel
isomorphic to
\mathsf{J}[2]
, embedded in

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times{\mathcal{P}}
by the map

\mathfrak{a}\mapsto(\mathfrak{a},\pi^{*}\mathfrak{a})
. Next note
that we have a natural map

\widehat{\pi}\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}:{\widehat{C}}\longrightarrow,\quad(A,\tilde{t})\mapsto\left(A,\pi^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})\right).

This map is clearly
\mathsf{J}[2]
-equivariant and so the composition

{\widehat{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{\pi}\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times{\mathcal{P}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{add}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
will factor as

\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{add}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}}

where
\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}
is the
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
-based Abel-Jacobi map

\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}:{\widetilde{C}}\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
,
\tilde{t}\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}})
.

Consider the theta line bundle
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
on
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})

defined by
the theta characteristic

\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\in\mathsf{Spin}({\widetilde{C}})
. In
other words

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}),\quad\text{where}\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}=\left\{L\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\ \left|\ h^{0}({\widetilde{C}},L(2\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))\geq 1\,\right.\right\}.

Recall that we used
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
to define the line bundle

\boldsymbol{\xi}
on
{\mathcal{P}}
, i.e. we had
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{|{\mathcal{P}}}
. Also,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is a principal polarization
on
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
which defines a surjective group homomorphism

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}:\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee},\quad L\mapsto\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{L}^{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\otimes\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}\right)_{|{\mathcal{P}}},

which fits in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)\times{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}_{{\mathcal{P}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{sum}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}}
\textstyle{{\widehat{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\jmath}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}

Therefore

\phi_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\circ\boldsymbol{\jmath}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\circ\mathsf{sum}\circ\left(\widehat{\pi}\times\boldsymbol{\jmath}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\circ\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}\circ\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}.

This implies that the map
\psi:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
factors as

\textstyle{{\widetilde{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}}

and so we need to compute pullbacks by
\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}
and

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
.

But for any
\alpha\in{\mathcal{P}}
and any
\mathscr{A}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
we have
that the fiber of the Poincare line bundle
{}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}

at the point
(\alpha,\mathscr{A})\in{\mathcal{P}}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
is
canonically isomorphic to the fiber of the line bundle
\mathscr{A}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}=\operatorname{Pic}^{0}({\mathcal{P}})
at
\alpha\in{\mathcal{P}}
. Hence for any
L\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
we have equality of fibers

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\alpha\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)\right))_{L}

\displaystyle={}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(L))}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}(L)\right)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{L}^{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\otimes\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}\right)_{\alpha}.

By the see-saw principle this equality of fibers implies that
we have an isomorphism

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\alpha\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\alpha\}\times\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}

of line bundles on
\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
, where

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})\times\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
is the biextension line bundle given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}=\boldsymbol{m}^{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}\otimes p_{2}^{*}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}.

By definition, the pullback of

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\alpha\}\times\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}
by
the Abel-Jacobi map
\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}:{\widetilde{C}}\to\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
is the line bundle on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose fiber at
a point
\tilde{t}\in{\widetilde{C}}
is equal to the line

\alpha_{\tilde{t}}\otimes\alpha_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{-1}
. Thus

\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{*}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\alpha\}\times\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}\right)=\alpha\otimes\alpha_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{-1}\cong\alpha.

All together this gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle\psi^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{*}\left({}^{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{Poinc}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{AJ}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}}^{*}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{|\{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\}\times\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})}\right)\cong\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),

and hence
\boldsymbol{\jmath}^{*}\mathfrak{L}=\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

as claimed. This proves the lemma.
∎

The previous lemma together with the identity (
55
) now
implies that
\mathsf{sum}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}\boxtimes\widehat{{\mathsf{sq}}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
which completes the proof of the
proposition.
∎

7.5
Disjointness statements

Lemma 7.8
.

The projection map
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\rightarrow X_{0}\times X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
is an isomorphism onto its image, which is smooth.

Proof.

If
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
and a stable bundle
E_{1}\in X_{1}
are fixed, the
fiber of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
over
(a,E_{1})
is the Hecke line consisting of
all the
E_{0}
obtained by Hecke transformations along
1
-dimensional
quotients of
(E_{1}\otimes A)(t)
. This is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
mapping to a line
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. We get a morphism
X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}\rightarrow{\rm Grass}(2,4)
to the Grassmanian of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. Since the
source is smooth, this map is a regular function. The pullback of the
universal fibration over
{\rm Grass}(2,4)
is a smooth
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle
over
X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}
. A point on one of the lines determines
uniquely the sheaf
E_{0}
and the map
\zeta
, so
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
is
isomorphic to this
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle.
∎

Recall that
{\mathcal{P}}_{m}
denotes the Prym variety of degree
m
line bundles

L
on
{\widetilde{C}}
such that the norm
\mathsf{Nm}_{{\widetilde{C}}/C}(L)
of
the associated divisor down to
C
is linearly equivalent to
m\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
. This
condition is equivalent to stating that
\det(\pi_{*}L)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}((m-2)\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
since
\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}})=\omega_{C}^{-1}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.
In particular,
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
is the Prym variety of degree
2
line
bundles
L
on
{\widetilde{C}}
such that
{\rm det}(\pi_{*}L)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
, and
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
is the Prym variety of degree
3
line bundles
L

on
{\widetilde{C}}
such that
{\rm det}(\pi_{*}L)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.

We have the maps
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, that are respectively blowing up of
16

points or a curve
{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
.
We also have
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
the Prym variety of degree
1
line bundles on

{\widetilde{C}}
whose norm is
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Subtraction gives a map

{\bf m}_{3,2}:{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{1}.

On the other hand, we have a shifted Abel-Jacobi map

{\bf j}:{\widehat{C}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{1}

defined by
{\bf j}(A,\tilde{t}):={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t})\otimes\pi^{*}(A^{-1})
. Note that if
L={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t})\otimes\pi^{*}(A^{-1})
and
\pi(\tilde{t})=t
, then

\mathsf{Nm}_{{\widetilde{C}}/C}(L)=A^{\otimes-2}(t)={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

as required.

Using the product of the blowup maps and
{\bf m}_{3,2}

gives a composed map

\textstyle{Y_{1}\times Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}\times\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\bf m}_{3,2}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{1}.}

The abelianized Hecke maps to the fiber product

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\rightarrow(Y_{1}\times Y_{0})\times_{{\mathcal{P}}_{1}}{\widehat{C}}.

If we fix a point
\widehat{a}\in{\widehat{C}}
, and consider the
restriction of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
to the subvariety
(Y_{1}\times Y_{0})\times_{{\mathcal{P}}_{1}}\{\widehat{a}\}

we get a correspondence

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}

The map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow Y_{1}
is the
blow-up of
Y_{1}
along
16
lines contained in the exceptional divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\cong{\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
of the map
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
. Similarly the map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow Y_{0}
is the blow-up of
Y_{0}
along a curve
isomorphic to
{\widehat{C}}
which intersects transversally at a single point
each of the
16
plane components

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
of the exceptional divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}=\sqcup_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}
.

Consider the maps
r_{0}=\operatorname{id}\times f_{0}:Y_{1}\times Y_{0}\rightarrow Y_{1}\times X_{0}
and
r_{1}=f_{1}\times\operatorname{id}:Y_{1}\times Y_{0}\rightarrow X_{1}\times Y_{0}
. With this notation we have

Theorem 7.9
.

Fix a point
\widehat{a}=(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
and let
\tau\widehat{a}=(A,\tau(\tilde{t}))
denote its conjugate under the covering
involution of the double cover
\widehat{\pi}:{\widehat{C}}\to{\overline{C}}
. Assume that

\widehat{a}
is general in
{\widehat{C}}
. Then the image in
X_{0}
of the
intersection

r_{0}\left({\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\right)\cap r_{0}\left({\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})\right)\subset Y_{1}\times X_{0}

has dimension at most
1
.

Proof.

Suppose we are given two points in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
that map
to the points
(y_{0},y_{1},\widehat{a})
and
(y^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{1},\tau\widehat{a})

in
(Y_{0}\times Y_{1})\times_{{\mathcal{P}}_{1}}{\widehat{C}}
. We assume that
these points further map to the same point
(x_{0},x_{1},a)
of

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
, and that
y_{1}=y_{1}^{\prime}
. We would
like to conclude
x_{0}
lies in a dimension
\leq 1
subset of

X_{0}
. Here this dimension is measured for a given fixed
\widehat{a}
.

Suppose first that the point
y_{1}=y^{\prime}_{1}
lies in the
exceptional divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\subset Y_{1}
. This part of the
exceptional locus in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
projects to a
curve in
Y_{0}
and so to a curve in
X_{0}
, so we may ignore this case.

We may therefore assume that
y_{1}=y^{\prime}_{1}
corresponds to a line bundle

L
on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose direct image is a stable vector bundle
V=\pi_{*}L
of determinant
\det V=\mathcal{O}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The Hecke
transformations corresponding to the abelianized Hecke at
\widehat{a}

and
\tau\widehat{a}
are along the lines
L_{\tilde{t}}
and

L_{\tau(\tilde{t})}
in
V_{t}
corresponding to the two different
points
\tilde{t}
and
\tau(\tilde{t})
of the spectral
curve
{\widetilde{C}}
. Thus
L_{\tilde{t}},L_{\tau(\tilde{t}}\subset V_{t}

correspond to distinct points of the Hecke line
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}={\mathbb{P}}((V\otimes A)_{t})
, so they are distinct points in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
and hence
distinct points in
X_{0}
. This contradicts the hypothesis, which
completes the proof starting with
y_{1}=y^{\prime}_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 7.10
.

Suppose
\ell
is a general line in
X_{0}
and let
H_{\ell}=q^{-1}(\ell)\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
be the restriction of

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
to
\ell
. Then the restrictions
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
over
\ell\subset X_{0}
are disjoint in the spectral
surface
\Sigma\rightarrow H_{\ell}
that is the pullback

\Sigma:=H_{\ell}\times_{X_{1}}Y_{1}.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.9
, the image in
X_{0}
of the intersection
of the two abelianized Hecke spaces inside
Y_{1}\times X_{0}
, is at most a
curve. Thus, a general line does not meet it, and we get the required
disjointness property.
∎

For the Hecke transform in the direction from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
, the
following lemma is used.

Lemma 7.11
.

For a general point
x_{0}\in\mathsf{Kum}
of the Kummer in
X_{0}
, the image

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)

intersects the Hecke fiber over
x_{0}
only at the point on the K3
surface where the two lines intersect, corresponding to the Hecke
transform of a polystable bundle.

Proof.

Choose one of the four points
y_{0}\in Y_{0}
over
x_{0}
, and let
L
be
the corresponding line bundle on
{\widetilde{C}}
. Let
E:=\pi_{*}(L)
. We
claim that
E
is polystable. Since
x_{0}\in\mathsf{Kum}
we know that
E

is strictly semistable, i.e. there is a degree
0
line bundle
U
with

U\hookrightarrow E
. We would like to show that there is also a
nonzero map
U^{\vee}\rightarrow E
.

By adjunction the inclusion
U\subset E
on
C
corresponds to a map

\pi^{*}U\hookrightarrow L
of rank one locally free sheaves on

{\widetilde{C}}
. The latter map will have cokernel which is a torsion sheaf
of length two since
L
has degree
2
. We can therefore write

L=\pi^{*}U\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}).

Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in C
be the image points of
\tilde{t}_{1},\tilde{t}_{2}
.
Let
t^{\prime}_{1}
and
t^{\prime}_{2}

be their conjugates by the hyperelliptic involution
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
.
The norm of
L
down to
C
is
\omega_{C}
, yielding the formula

U^{\otimes 2}\cong\omega_{C}(-t_{1}-t_{2}).

In particular,

U^{\vee}\cong U\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t_{1}+t_{2}-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}).

Thus

\pi^{*}(U^{\vee})\cong\pi^{*}(U)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}+\tau\tilde{t}_{1}+\tau\tilde{t}_{2}-2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-2\tau\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}).

To get a map
\pi^{*}(U^{\vee})\rightarrow L
we therefore need a
section of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}+2\tau\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\tau\tilde{t}_{1}-\tau\tilde{t}_{2})
.

Recall that
{\widetilde{C}}
is also a hyperelliptic curve, whose
hyperelliptic involution
\sigma
is a lift of the hyperelliptic
involution
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
of
C
. The fixed points of
\sigma
are the
12

inverse image points of the Weierstrass points of
C
. In particular,

2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
and
2\tau\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}
are linearly equivalent divisors
coming from
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
by pullback along the hyperelliptic map
{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Therefore

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\tau\tilde{t}_{i})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\sigma\tau\tilde{t}_{i})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\tau\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\tau\tilde{t}_{i}).

Thus

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(2\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}+2\tau\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\tau\tilde{t}_{1}-\tau\tilde{t}_{2})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\sigma\tau\tilde{t}_{1}+\sigma\tau\tilde{t}_{2})

is effective. This gives the required nonzero section so there is a
nonzero map
\pi^{*}(U^{\vee})\rightarrow L
corresponding by adjunction
to
U^{\vee}\rightarrow E
. We get a map
U\oplus U^{\vee}\rightarrow E

that, for general
U
, has to be an isomorphism. Thus
E
is
polystable. Now, a Hecke transformation of
E
that becomes a stable
bundle has to be by a diagonal line, corresponding to the stated point
of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
.
∎

Theorem 7.12
.

Fix a point
\widehat{a}=(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
and its conjugate
\tau\widehat{a}=(A,\tau(\tilde{t}))
. Assume that
\widehat{a}
is general in

{\widehat{C}}
. Then, up to a subset whose image in
X_{1}
has dimension
\leq 1
, the intersection

r_{1}\left({\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\right)\cap r_{1}\left({\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})\right)\subset X_{1}\times Y_{0}

consists of the set of points of the form
(x_{1},y_{0})
such that

y_{0}
is a point of
Y_{0}
lying over the Kummer variety, and
x_{1}
is
a point in
X_{1}
that is in the image of the K3 surface in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
.

Proof.

This will follow the same lines as the proof of Theorem

7.9
and we keep the same notations. However, instead
of supposing that
y_{1}=y^{\prime}_{1}
, we suppose now that
y_{0}=y^{\prime}_{0}
. If

y_{0}
is a point of one of the
16
exceptional divisors

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}\subset Y_{0}
lying over the trope planes, these
project down to lines in the wobbly locus of
X_{1}
so as before we can
ignore this case.

If
y_{0}
corresponds to a line bundle
L
on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose direct
image
E=\pi_{*}L
is a stable bundle, then by the same argument as
in the proof of Theorem
7.9
, the two points of the abelianized
Hecke correspond to Hecke transformations at different points of the
spectral curve, so they are distinct in the Hecke curve
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}={\mathbb{P}}((E\otimes A)_{t})
that maps to a conic in
X_{1}
. Thus, they map
to distinct points in
X_{1}
and so this case can not happen.

We are left with the case that the point
x_{0}=f_{0}(y_{0})
is a
point
x_{0}\in\mathsf{Kum}
of the Kummer variety. We may assume that
x_{0}

is general in the Kummer variety, as points on a divisor of the Kummer
will lead to subsets of
X_{1}
of dimension
\leq 1
. By Lemma

7.11
, the point
(x_{1},x_{0})
on
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
lies on the
Kummer K3 surface, so
x_{1}
is in the image of the K3 surface in

X_{1}
. We get the stated divisor as the image of the intersection of
the two abelianized Hecke pieces.
∎

Corollary 7.13
.

Suppose
\ell
is a general line in
X_{1}
and let
H_{\ell}=p^{-1}(\ell)\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
be the restriction of

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
to
\ell
. Let

\Sigma:=H_{\ell}\times_{X_{1}}Y_{1}

be the spectral surface of the pullback Higgs sheaf over
H_{\ell}
.
Then the restrictions

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tilde{a})_{\ell}
and

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\tilde{a})_{\ell}
over
\ell\subset X_{1}

are curves in
\Sigma
that
meet at each of the double points of
\Sigma
lying over one of the
two nodes of the curve to be denoted
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
in
Section
8
.

Proof.

Consider the image
\boldsymbol{\mathit{K}}(a)\subset X_{1}
of the Kummer K3 surface inside

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
. The line
\ell
intersects
\boldsymbol{\mathit{K}}(a)
in two
points. This may be seen from the discussion of Section

8
where the two points are the images in
\ell

of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
, or alternatively in Remark

11.3
where
K(a)
is viewed as the intersection of
X_{1}

with an additional quadric that depends on
a
.

Suppose
(x_{1},y_{0})
is an intersection point in
\Sigma
of the two
curves, that means that
x_{1}\in\ell
and this point is in the
intersection of the two abelianized Hecke pieces. We have seen that
this means that
y_{0}
lies over a point of the Kummer surface, and
indeed that
y_{0}
corresponds to a line bundle
L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{@}
such
that
\pi_{*}L\cong U\oplus U^{\vee}
for some line bundle
U\in\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
with

L=\pi^{*}U\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}).

But
\widehat{a}=(A,\tilde{t})
with
\pi(\tilde{t})=t
and

A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-p)
, and so
the point
x_{1}
corresponds to a bundle
V
that fits in an exact sequence

0\rightarrow U\otimes A^{-1}\rightarrow V\rightarrow U^{\vee}\otimes A^{-1}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t)\rightarrow 0.

Assuming that
x_{0}
is a general point of the Kummer surface, then we
are given two sub-line bundles of degree
0
in the same Hecke
transformation, namely
U\otimes A^{-1}
and
U^{-1}\otimes A^{-1}
. It means that these two agree as sub-lines in the fiber

V_{t}
.

Suppose we now vary the point in a family
x_{1}(z)
and thus the bundle

V(z)
. Starting with a line that is not multiple as one of the four
lines through
x_{1}(0)
, and extend it to a family of lines locally at

x_{1}(z)
; follow this line, making the Hecke transformation at the
resulting sub-line of
V(z)_{t}
. This gives a locally well-defined
section of the Hecke correspondence that maps into the Kummer surface
in
X_{0}
. We see in this way that if two distinct lines through
x_{1}

(neither of which is doubled in the set of four) have the property
that they agree in
V_{t}
, then the family of intersections of the
Hecke lines with the Kummer surface has two distinct branches.

This applies to our previous situation. In Section
8.1
we
will see that the family of intersections of the Hecke lines with the
Kummer surface is a reducible curve
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
inside
H_{\ell}
. We have
said that at our intersection point
(x_{1},y_{0})
we are given two
distinct sub-lines of the bundle
V
(the point
x_{1}
) such that the
Hecke transformations at
a
agree. Our argument then says that

(x_{1},x_{0})
is a point in
H_{\ell}
that is a double point of the
curve
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
. This shows that it is one of the two points we have
identified, over which
\Sigma
has four ordinary double points.

We notice that there are four points
y_{0}
over
x_{0}
, and this will
give intersection points in the four ordinary double points of

\Sigma
lying over the given point of
H_{\ell}
.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

In the Hecke transformation from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
, the restriction of the
resulting rank

16
Higgs bundle on a general line
\ell\subset X_{1}
is the direct
sum of two rank
8
Higgs bundles isomorphic to the restrictions of
our constructed Higgs bundle to
\ell
. The discussion of Section

8
will show that the contributions from the two
branches of the critical locus became equivalent to direct images of
line bundles on the the disjoint unions of the two branches, via the
mechanism of the blow-up of the double points of
\Sigma
and the
correction due to a line bundle on the exceptional locus.

7.6
Pullbacks of the wobbly locus in the abelianized Hecke

We have two composed maps

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\rm ab}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{1}

and

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{\rm ab}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{0}.

We would like to describe the inverse images of the wobbly loci

{\rm Wob}_{1}
,
{\rm Wob}_{0}
under these.

For
f_{1}:Y_{1}\to X_{1}
recall that

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\subset Y_{1}
is the exceptional divisor of the blowup

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}:Y_{1}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
(see
section
3.2
). Then

(f_{1})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{1})=2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}+{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{1}.

Here
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{1}
is a 4-sheeted cover of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
, because
f_{1}

has degree 8, while
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
is a double cover of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. By
definition
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{1}
parametrizes line bundles in the Prym
that push forward to wobbly bundles.

Look at the map
p^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{1}
. Birationally
this is the Abel-Jacobi map:
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\times{\widehat{C}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, sending a
pair
(L,\hat{y}=(\tilde{y},(A,y)))
to
L\otimes{\pi}^{*}A^{-1}(\tilde{y})
. The generic fiber of
p^{\rm ab}
is
{\widehat{C}}
. Over
the generic point of the exceptional divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}={\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the fiber becomes the union of
{\widehat{C}}
with
16
surfaces, each
an
\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

We want to pull back this divisor by the Abel-Jacobi map. Recall from
the discussion in Section
7.2
that the
map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\rightarrow Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
is a
blowing-up along
16
disjoint subvarieties
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
in

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
. In other words, the variety
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is the blowup of
Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
along the
16
surfaces,
all contained in
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
. The
16
surfaces are

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\widehat{C}}\times_{{\widetilde{C}}}{\widehat{C}}={\sqcup}_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}.

When we pull back our divisor
(f_{1})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{1})=2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}+{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{1}
by
p^{\rm ab}
, we may first just take its product with

{\widehat{C}}
and then do the blowing-up. Not much happens over
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
(we get its strict transform
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}
), while
the pullback of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}
is
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
plus
16
new
components
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}
. This proves the following:

Lemma 7.15
.

The pullback of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
by the composed map

f_{1}\circ p^{\rm ab}
is

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\cup(\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathsf{Spin}(C)}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa})\cup\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}.

Turn now to
the pullback of
{\rm Wob}_{0}
in
Y_{0}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
.
For the map

f_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
and the exceptional divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}\subset Y_{0}
we get

(f_{0})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{0})=2\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}+{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{0}+K

Here
K
is the full inverse image of the Kummer surface
\mathsf{Kum}\subset Y_{0}
. It is irreducible, in fact it is dominated by the second
symmetric product of
{\widehat{C}}
.

Left over,
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{0}
is a
6
-sheeted cover of
\cup_{\kappa}\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
, because
f_{0}
has degree
8
, while
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
is birational
to
\cup_{\kappa}\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
. In particular,

{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{0}
is a union of
16
pieces corresponding to the
16

trope planes (we are not saying here that these pieces are necessarily
irreducible but of course that is strongly suspected).

Consider
d^{\rm ab}:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to Y_{0}
.
Here
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is
the blowup of
Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
along the surface
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
. The exceptional divisor for this blowup is

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
. The pullback of
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{0}
is the strict transform

\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}
of the divisor
{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
. For

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}={\sqcup}_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0,\kappa}
, the pullback of each
component consists of the strict transform of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}
.

Lemma 7.16
.

The pullback of
{\rm Wob}_{0}
by the composed map

f_{0}\circ p^{\rm ab}
is

\left(\bigcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}\right)\cup\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\cup\widehat{K}\cup\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}.

Proof.

We claim that the surface
{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}
is
contained in the extra component
K\times{\widehat{C}}
.
We have a commutative diagram

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}&\rightarrow&Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}&\rightarrow&X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}\end{array}.

If we take a general point
(V,\hat{y})\in{\widehat{C}}\times{\widehat{C}}\subset Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}
then the inverse image of
(V,\hat{y})
in

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We want to say that
this
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
maps to a positive dimensional subvariety in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. By construction the image of this
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
to
Y_{1}
is one of the rulings of the divisor

{\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\subset Y_{1}
. In particular if we
map the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
all the way down to

X_{1}
, then the image is one of the lines in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
, i.e. is
positive dimensional. But the map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\to X_{1}

factors through
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
. Hence the image of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
will be a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
as well.
Note also that the image
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
is contained in a
fiber of the map
q:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\to X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
. But the
only components of fibers of
q
that map to lines in
X_{1}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
are components of fiber over points of the Kummer (note that
by assumption our
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
maps to a general point in
{\overline{C}}
in
particular not a preimage of a Weierstrass point of
C
).

This shows that the locus to be blown up is contained in
K\times{\widehat{C}}
. Therefore, the pullback of
K
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
is
the union of the strict transform
\widehat{K}
, and the exceptional
divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
. The pullbacks of the other pieces of

(f_{0})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{0})
yield the other pieces of the stated
decomposition.
∎

Theorem 7.17
.

The intersection of the two pullbacks of the wobbly loci
up to codimension
1
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}

consists of just the
exceptional pieces

(f_{0}\circ p^{\rm ab})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{0})\cap(f_{1}\circ p^{\rm ab})^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{1})=\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\cup(\bigcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}).

Proof.

Using the previous lemmas, it means we need to look at the intersection

\left[(\bigcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa})\cup\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\cup\widehat{K}\cup\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}\right]\;\cap\;\left[\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}\cup(\bigcup_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa})\cup\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}\right].

We need to show that
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}
does not have any irreducible
components in common with
\widehat{K}\cup\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}
.

Choose
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
with a lifting
\widehat{a}=(A,\tilde{t})

and look at the fiber over
\widehat{a}
. Here, the subvarieties have
dimension
2
and we want to show (for general
a
) that they do not
share any
2
-dimensional components.

In view of the decomposition of the fiber
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}(a)
into

16
pieces that move around under the monodromy operation when we
move
a
, whereas on the other hand
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}(a)
can’t have
more than
4
irreducible components since it is a
4
-sheeted
covering of the irreducible
{\rm Wob}_{1}
, we see that
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{0}

and
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}
can not share irreducible components. Thus, we are
reduced to the question of showing that
\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}
and

\widehat{K}
do not share any components.

Now, by Lemma
7.11
, the image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tilde{a})
in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
only intersects the Hecke fibers
over the Kummer
\mathsf{Kum}
, in the intersection points of the two lines.
But this collection of points maps to the Kummer K3 surface inside

X_{1}
that isn’t the same as
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. Thus, the intersection

\widehat{{\operatorname{{\sf Residual}}}}_{1}\cap\widehat{K}
does not have any
2
-dimensional
pieces. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

Inside the big Hecke correspondence

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}
, the intersection of the two pullbacks of the wobbly loci with
the abelianized Hecke is, as for its
3
-dimensional pieces:

p^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{1})\cap d^{-1}({\rm Wob}_{0})\cap g({\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab})=g(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}).

Proof.

This is the image by
g:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}

of the statement in the previous theorem.
∎

Recall that for each
a\in{\widehat{C}}
, the piece
g(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0})(a)

in the fiber
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
contracts to a lower-dimensional
subvariety (in this case the union of
16
lines in the wobbly locus)
under the projection to
X_{1}
. And similarly,
the piece
g(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1})(a)
contracts to a lower-dimensional
subvariety (a birational copy of the curve
{\overline{C}}
in
\mathsf{Kum}
)
under the projection to
X_{0}
.

The piece
g(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0})(a)
mapping to
X_{0}
consists of
16
planes
over the trope planes, which are the ramification of the horizontal
divisor in the Hecke correspondence that contribute to the
singularities of the Hecke transformed system from
X_{1}
to
X_{0}
. The
singularities over the Kummer surface come from the degenerations of
Hecke conics to pairs of lines.

On the other hand, the piece
g(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1})(a)
mapping to
X_{1}
is the
ramification of the inverse image of the Kummer surface in the Hecke
correspondence, giving the singularities of the Hecke transformed
system from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
.

In the next chapters we will be testing the Hecke transforms by
restricting over lines in the target spaces, so the ramification
locations contributing to singularities will show up as intersected
with the inverse images of the lines in the Hecke correspondence.

8
Hecke transformation from
X_{0}
to

X_{1}

We recall the standard diagrams. The big Hecke correspondence fits
into a diagram of the form

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\times{\overline{C}}.}

Fix a point
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
where
A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
and consider the Hecke correspondence
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\overline{a})
fitting into the diagram

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{X_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}.}

The objective is to pull-back the constructed Higgs bundle from
X_{0}

and take the higher direct image along
b
to
X_{1}
.

In order to simplify the measurement of the result, fix a general line

\ell\subset X_{1}
. Let
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
be the
inverse image of
\ell
in the Hecke variety
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
. This is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{1}
, which
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle over
\ell\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Let
P\subset X_{0}
be the image of the map from

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
to
X_{0}
. This is a plane
P\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. The map
d:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\rightarrow P

is blowing up a point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}\in P
. This point is a general point
contained in the Kummer
\mathsf{Kum}\subset X_{0}
. There is also a point

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}\in P\cap\mathsf{Kum}
where
P
is tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
. In particular

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
are not on the trope planes.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
be the strict transform of

\mathsf{Kum}\cap P
, and let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
be the
exceptional divisor. It is the unique section of the
\mathbb{F}_{1}

surface that has self-intersection
-1
.

We have that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cap\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
is a single point in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. The point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
corresponds to a unique
point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\in{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
where the curve
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
has a
node. The distinction in notation here is that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}\in P
is a point
of the plane inside
X_{0}
, while
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
is one of the points in the
exceptional divisor above
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
, namely the intersection point of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
; it also corresponds to the tangent direction of the
Hecke line at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
or equivalently the tangent direction of
P\cap\mathsf{Kum}
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
. The points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are basically identical
since there is no blowing-up, we just use the notation
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
rather
than
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
for uniformity.

Figure 1:
The Hirzebruch surface
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}

Let
Y_{1,\ell}\subset Y_{1}
be the inverse image of
\ell
. This is a
smooth curve in view of the moving properties of the family of lines

\ell
, indeed, locally this family looks like the family of complete
intersections of two moving divisors in
X_{1}
.

8.1
The abelianized Hecke as a critical locus

The big abelianized Hecke fits into a diagram of the form

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\times{\widehat{C}}.}

The inverse image in
{\widehat{C}}
of
a\in{\overline{C}}
consists of two points
that we will note
\widehat{a}
and
\tau\widehat{a}
. We obtain two
abelianized Hecke varieties
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})
. Denote their disjoint union by

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a}):={\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\sqcup{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a}).

The map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow Y_{1}
is the
blow-up along
16
lines contained in the wobbly locus, as was
discussed in Section
7.2
.

A general line
\ell\subset X_{1}
does not meet those lines. Thus,
if we let
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
denote the inverse
image of
\ell
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
, the projection
induces an isomorphism

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}Y_{1,\ell}.

The same holds for the other piece
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

The curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has degree
8
over
\ell
.
The full curve of degree
16
over

\ell
is

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}={\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\sqcup{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}.

The
16
exceptional divisors in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
contract to the
16
exceptional planes in

Y_{0}
. It follows that the image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
in
Y_{0}
does not meet those.

Let
Y_{0,P}:=Y_{0}\times_{X_{0}}P
, and set

\Sigma:=Y_{0,P}\times_{P}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}.

This is the spectral variety for the Higgs bundle

(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}}
which is

(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})
pulled
back to
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
via the map
d:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\to X_{0}
. In particular
\Sigma
has degree
8

over
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. We have a lifting

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\rightarrow\Sigma.

Proposition 8.1
.

The map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\rightarrow\Sigma
identifies the abelianized
Hecke as the upper critical locus (see Proposition
12.1
)

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\cong\widetilde{{\rm Crit}}\left({\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell,(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}}\right)

away from the points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose we are given a point of
\Sigma
. It corresponds to a point of

Y_{0}
together with a Hecke operation leading to a Hecke transformed
bundle which is a point of
\ell\subset X_{1}
.

If the point of
Y_{0}
is on an exceptional divisor, it means that the
point of
\Sigma
is a ramification point of
\Sigma
over the
horizontal divisor in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. The residues of the
Higgs field
\Phi_{0}
are nilpotent, so their Jordan types must be
constant along the parts of the horizontal divisor that are etale over

\ell
, and this implies that ramification points of
\Sigma
over
this subset of the horizontal divisor can not be in the relative
critical locus (we will see this argument again in the next
subsection). The points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, as well as the
ramification points of the horizontal divisor, are contained in the
image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
and
also in the relative critical locus.

We claim that the upper critical locus is smooth at the ramification
points of the horizontal divisor, i.e. at points where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
ramifies
over
\ell
. In local coordinates
x,t
where the map to
\ell
is
given by
t
, we can write
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
as
x^{2}-t=0
, and
\Sigma
is
x^{2}-t=w^{2}
. Thus
x,w
are coordinates on
\Sigma
and
t=x^{2}-w^{2}
. Write
\alpha
as
adx+bdw
. The vertical direction over the
ramification point is the
w
-axis, and the fact that the Jordan form
of the residue of the Higgs field is constant along smooth points of

{\rm Wob}_{1}
(our ramification is such a smooth point) implies that

\alpha
is nonzero on the vertical direction, as was pointed out in
Lemma
8.3
. This says that
b(0)\neq 0
. Now, the
relative differentials are obtained by setting
dt=0
so
xdx=wdw
. Thus
\alpha^{\rm rel}
is written as

\alpha^{\rm rel}=(a+(x/w)b)dx

and the condition
\alpha^{\rm rel}=0
may be written as
wa+xb=0
. As
b(0)\neq 0
, the linear term
wa(0)+xb(0)
is nonzero, so
this defines a smooth curve which is the local branch of the relative
critical locus at this point of
\Sigma
.

This shows that the relative critical locus is smooth at ramification
points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}/\ell
, so a set-theoretical identification between the
two subvarieties (neither of which has embedded points) is an
isomorphism.

Over the points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, we will see that the relative
critical locus has two branches in each of the four nodes of
\Sigma

over one of these points, and the abelianized Hecke is isomorphic to
the normalization.

In view of the previous paragraphs, we can now assume that the point
of
Y_{0}
is not on an exceptional divisor, so it corresponds to a line
bundle
L
of degree
2
on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose norm to
C
is
\omega_{C}
. The corresponding vector bundle on
C
is
E=\pi_{*}L
. Our
Hecke transformation is given by a pair
(A,x)
where
x\in C
and

A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(x-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The Hecke transformation is centered at
a line
V\subset E_{x}
and we let
E^{\prime}
be the kernel of the map

E\rightarrow E_{x}/V
. This is a bundle of degree
-1
with
determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(-x)
. Then
E^{\prime}\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
has degree
1
and
determinant
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
so it is a point in
X_{1}
.

In turn we have a line
V^{\prime}\subset E^{\prime}(x)_{x}
and the kernel of
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}
is
E
. The line
V^{\prime}
corresponds to a line
in
E^{\prime}\otimes A(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
and the opposite Hecke transform involving
tensoring again with a square-root, gets us back to
E
. The Hecke
line through the point
[E]
consists of all the bundles obtained as
kernels of
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E^{\prime}(x)/W
where
W
is another line

W\subset E^{\prime}(x)_{x}
. Letting
W
be an infinitesimal deformation of

V^{\prime}
we get a tangent vector to
X_{0}
at the point
[E]
, represented
by a class
\eta\in H^{1}(\text{End}_{0}(E))
. We’ll give an expression
for this class using an exact sequence, below.

The tautological
1
-form on
Y_{0}
is a section of

f_{0}^{*}T_{X_{0}}^{\vee}
At the point
[L]\in Y_{0}
corresponding
to the line bundle
L
that projects to
[E]\in X_{0}
,
\alpha([L])\in\left(f_{0}^{*}T^{\vee}_{X_{0}}\right)_{[L]}=T^{\vee}_{X_{0},[E]}
can be paired with a tangent vector of the form

\eta
. The condition that we are at a point of the upper critical
locus means that the value of the pairing is zero.

Our point on
Y_{0}
actually represents a Higgs bundle over
C
,
because
Y_{0}
was the blow-up of
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\subset\mathsf{Higgs}_{0}
. The
underlying bundle is
E
(since we are assuming that we are not over
the wobbly locus), and the Higgs field
\theta:E\to E\otimes\omega_{C}
is induced by the tautological
1
-form of

{\widetilde{C}}
. In these terms, the pairing is just the pairing between

\theta\in H^{0}(\text{End}_{0}(E)\otimes\omega_{C})
and
\eta\in H^{1}(\text{End}_{0}(E))
. The upper critical locus condition is that this
pairing is
0
.

Let us now look more closely at the deformation class
\eta
. The
deformation of
V^{\prime}
is given by an element of
\text{Hom}(V^{\prime},E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime})
. Tensor

0\rightarrow E\rightarrow E^{\prime}(x)\rightarrow E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}\rightarrow 0

with
E^{\vee}
to get

0\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(E)\rightarrow E^{\vee}\otimes E^{\prime}(x)\rightarrow E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}\rightarrow 0.

Note that
E_{x}\rightarrow V^{\prime}
so
(V^{\prime})^{\vee}\rightarrow E_{x}^{\vee}
.
Compose with the connecting map for the previous exact sequence as
follows:

(V^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime})\rightarrow E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{1}(\operatorname{End}(E)).

The image of an element of
\operatorname{Hom}(V^{\prime},(E(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}))
is the
deformation class of
E
generated by doing the Hecke operation back
using the infinitesimally close
W
. The determinant of the new
bundle is the same as that of
E
, so the deformation class lies in
the trace-free part
H^{1}(\operatorname{End}_{0}(E))
.

The exact sequence for the fiber of the Hecke transformation is

0\rightarrow E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x)\rightarrow E_{x}\rightarrow V^{\prime}\rightarrow 0.

The exact sequence

0\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(E)\rightarrow E^{*}\otimes E^{\prime}(x)\rightarrow E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime})\rightarrow 0.

has as dual, taking
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(-,\omega_{C})
:

0\rightarrow E\otimes(E^{\prime})^{\vee}(-x)\otimes\omega_{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(E)\otimes\omega_{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}),\omega_{C})\rightarrow 0.

We have

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}),\omega_{C})=\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{O}}_{C},E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}))^{\vee}=\operatorname{Hom}((E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}),E_{x}).

This maps to
\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime},V^{\prime})
.
Use the residue identification
\omega_{C}(x)_{x}\cong{\mathbb{C}}

and hence
V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}(x)\cong V^{\prime}
, to say that

\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime},V^{\prime})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime},V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}(x))\cong\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x),V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}).

The previous map now becomes

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(E_{x}^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime}),\omega_{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x),V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}).

Altogether, the second map in the dual exact sequence becomes

\operatorname{End}(E)\otimes\omega_{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x),V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}),

and this is just the evaluation map evaluating a Higgs field on the
subspace
E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x)\subset E_{x}
and projecting the answer to the quotent

V^{\prime}
tensored with
\omega_{C}
.

The Serre dual of the map
(V^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes(E^{\prime}(x)_{x}/V^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{1}(\operatorname{End}(E))
is the action of the previous map on global sections:

H^{0}(\operatorname{End}(E)\otimes\omega_{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x),V^{\prime}\otimes\omega_{C}),

To say that this vanishes for a Higgs field
\theta:E\rightarrow E\otimes\omega_{C}
is equivalent to saying that the filtration with
subspace
E^{\prime}_{x}/V^{\prime}(-x)\subset E_{x}
and quotient
E_{x}\rightarrow V^{\prime}
is
respected by the Higgs field.

This is now the same thing as saying that our original Hecke
transformation was in the abelianized Hecke.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

For a general
\ell
,
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
does not intersect the inverse images in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
of the
32
points in
P
given by the
intersection of
P
with the trope conics.

Proof.

Recall that
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\rightarrow Y_{0}
is
the blow-up along a curve isomorphic to
{\widehat{C}}
, this curve
intersecting the planes of the exceptional locus in
Y_{0}
in a finite
set. The trope conics are conics in these planes, so their inverse
image in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has dimension
1
.
This projects to a dimension
1
subset of
X_{1}
, so a general line

\ell
does not intersect that. The same holds for the other piece. So,

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})
intersected with the
pullbacks of the trope conics, intersected with the pullback of

\ell
, will be empty.
∎

8.2
Description of the configuration

We look more closely at the details of the configuration inside and
above
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. Inside
\Sigma
, there are
16
lines

L_{\kappa,\Sigma}\subset\Sigma
lying over the (strict transforms
in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
of the) intersections
P\cap\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
of
P
with the trope planes, on which

\Sigma/{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
has a simple ramification. Lemma

8.2
says that the curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
does not meet these

L_{\kappa,\Sigma}
.

Over general points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}

the ramification of
\Sigma
consists of four sheets each of which is
a simple ramification. We also know that
\Sigma
has four ordinary
double points over each of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
. There are probably
other singularities for example over the intersection of
P
with the
trope conics, however the curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
does not touch those.

There is a spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{0}
on
Y_{0}
, which pulls back to
a line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
, such that

{\mathcal{E}}_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}}
is the pushforward of
\mathscr{L}_{\Sigma}

from
\Sigma
to
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. The Higgs field of

{\mathcal{E}}_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}}
is given by a holomorphic
1
-form

\alpha
on the smooth locus of
\Sigma
. It projects to a section of

\Omega^{1}_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell}(\log D)|_{\Sigma}
where the
divisor
D
is the union of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
and the strict transforms in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
of the
16
trope lines
P\cap\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}
.

Along a point where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
is horizontal over
\ell
, the
residue of the Higgs field consists of a sum of four nonzero nilpotent
transformations, since the Jordan type of the residue has to stay
fixed along the divisor because it corresponds to
monodromy. Furthermore, over
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
at least, the inverse image of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
in
\Sigma
is a union of four lines along which the map has
simple ramification. Thus, the form
\alpha
is nonvanishing in the
transverse direction to these lines so as a section of

\Omega^{1}_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell}(\log D)|_{\Sigma}
it is
nonvanishing along these lines except at the points over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. On
the other hand, the curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
is the vanishing locus of the section of

\Omega^{1}_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell}(\log D)|_{\Sigma}
. It
follows that
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\subset\Sigma
does not intersect the four lines over

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
except at points over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. In sum, the image of

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
only intersects
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. Similar
considerations show that this also holds for the horizontal part of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}-\{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\}
.

We would like to know how many branches of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
pass through the point

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. We will see by the local calculations below that each double point
of
\Sigma
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
corresponds to two branches of the polar curve
of zeros of the relative Higgs field. This says that the full curve

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has
2

branches in each of the four double points, thus it has
8
branches
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. A monodromy argument says that these have to be evenly
distributed, so
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has
4

branches passing through
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. The local argument will also show that
they intersect
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
transversally. Thus, if we denote by

[{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}]
the image of this curve in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
, we have

[{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}]\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=4.

We will discuss later the distribution of these
4
branches among the

4
double points of
\Sigma
.

We next consider intersection numbers inside the
\mathbb{F}_{1}

surface
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. Let
\mathsf{fib}
denote a general
fiber of
b:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\rightarrow\ell
. Then the
divisors
\mathsf{fib}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
generate the Picard group of

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
, with
\mathsf{fib}^{2}=0
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}^{2}=-1
and

\mathsf{fib}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=1
.

The curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has degree
8
over
\ell
,
so

[{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}]\cdot\mathsf{fib}=8.

The intersection numbers with
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathsf{fib}
uniquely determine
the class of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
inside

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
:

[{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tilde{a})_{\ell}]\sim 8\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}+12\mathsf{fib}.

On the other hand, we also have that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
has degree
3
over

\ell
since the intersection with a fiber is all the points in that
line intersected the Kummer, except the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
which
corresponds to the point of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
intersected that fiber. We have

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cdot\mathsf{fib}=3
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}=1
so

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\sim 3\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}+4\mathsf{fib}.

Combining these we get

[{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}]\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}=(8\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}+12\mathsf{fib})(3\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}+4\mathsf{fib})=36+32-24=44.

We already know the intersections over the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
, there are

4
branches. Also over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
there will similarly be
4
branches,
but
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
is a double point of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
(whereas the double point of

D
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
involved both
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
). Thus, the intersection
counts for
8
points over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
. This leaves
32
intersection points
over the ramification points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}/\ell
. We note that there are
8

such points, mapping to the
8
points in
\ell
where
\ell\subset X_{1}
intersects
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. We get an intersection number of
4

at each ramification point (a monodromy argument shows that they need
to be evenly distributed). This will come from two tacnodes between
the curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
and the divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
, in accordance with our calculations for the local contribution of
the direct image at a simple ramification point of the horizontal
divisor.

The curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has to meet the
ramified locus of
Y_{0}
over
\mathsf{Kum}
in two of the four sheets, as
each one will contribute a tacnode and from the above discussion there
should not be more than
2
tacnodes. The curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
meets the ramified locus in the other
two sheets. On the other hand, for the nodal points we’ll see that

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has one branch in each sheet
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
has the other
branch.

For each of the direct summands of the direct image Higgs bundle,
we will get two ramification points of the spectral variety over each
point of
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
. This agrees with the ramification
pattern of
Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
over
{\rm Wob}_{1}
.

We need to verify the part of Hypothesis
3.16
about
points of type
3.11.1(d)
, namely the ramification points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
over

\ell
.

Lemma 8.3
.

For a general line
\ell
, the value of the spectral
1
-form
\alpha

on the vertical direction in the tangent space of
Y_{0}
at any of the
ramification points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}/\ell
, is nonzero.

Proof.

As
\ell
varies, each of these ramification points varies and
constitutes a general point of
\mathsf{Kum}
. So it suffices to note that the
value of
\alpha
on the vertical direction is nonzero. This is
because the nilpotent residue of the Higgs field is nonzero along

\mathsf{Kum}
, which in turn is a consequence of the Bogomolov-Gieseker
inequality as explained in the proof of Lemma
8.9

below.
∎

8.3
Apparent singularities

The discriminant of
({\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}})

relative to
\ell
consists of the following kinds of points:

•

images of simple ramification points of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
over
\ell
, these are the
points of
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
and we expect singularities of the local
system at these points;

•

images of points where the Hecke line goes through a trope conic—
by Lemma
8.2

these points are not contained in the lower critical locus, so
by Proposition
12.2
they do not contribute singularities
of the higher direct image local system; and

•

images of the points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
has a
node—we’ll see for topological reasons below that these do not
contribute singularities, and indeed the Dolbeault higher direct image
consideration will also show that.

The following lemma gives the topological proof for the third part.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose given a point where the horizontal divisor has a simple normal
