crossing with both branches étale over the base, and suppose that the
monodromy transformations around each branch are direct sums of
identical size
2
unipotent Jordan blocks. Then such a point does not
contribute to the monodromy of the higher direct image local system.

Proof.

This description characterizes the nontrivial local rank
2
pieces of
the local system upstairs. However, we also obtain the same type of
situation if we start with a unipotent rank
2
local system having
singularities along a horizontal divisor that is simply ramified over
the base, then pull back to a simply ramified double cover of the
base. We will see in subsection
12.4.3
that the
monodromy for a simply ramified horizontal divisor with unipotent
local system, is a transformation of order
2
. Therefore, its
pullback by a ramified double cover has trivial monodromy
transformation.
∎

Even though the topological monodromy transformation for such a point
is trivial, we still need to use the description of
[
DPS16
]
to
get the description of the spectral line bundle for the higher direct
image at such a point, since the map is singular.

Corollary 8.5
.

The singularities of the higher direct image local system over
\ell

are located at the points of
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
.

Remark 8.6
.

In the situation of Lemma
8.4
, if we had monodromy over the
nodal horizontal divisor decomposing into size
2
blocks with a
reflection instead of a nilpotent monodromy transformations, then the
higher direct image would have the square of a nilpotent
transformation (subsection
12.4.1
), so again a nontrivial
nilpotent transformation. Applied to the Hecke situation, this means
that if we do the Hecke transformation starting with a local system
with parabolic weights
1/2
over the Kummer, we will get singularities
at the images of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
in
\ell
. These are probably the
intersection of
\ell
with a singular K3 surface inside
X_{1}
, the
image of the K3 inside the Hecke variety, and which is the
intersection of
X_{1}
with another quadric in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
.

8.3.1
General position arguments

Let us now look locally at the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. The horizontal divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\cup\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
has a node there, and the spectral variety
\Sigma

decomposes as a union of four ordinary double points.

The point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
is a place where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
has a node, with both
branches being étale over the base
\ell
. This corresponds to a
point where the plane
P
is tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
. Again,
\Sigma

decomposes into
4
pieces, and each piece restricted to the tangent
plane gives an ordinary double point.

In order to apply the construction of Theorems
3.17

and
12.12
, we need the following result.

Theorem 8.7
.

The points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are points of type
3.11.1(e)
in the
classification of Subsection
3.11
.

Proof.

The geometric picture shows that the inverse image of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
resp.
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}

in
\Sigma
decomposes into four ordinary double points. Recall that
over a general point of
\mathsf{Kum}
, the covering
Y_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}

decomposes into four local pieces each of which is a double cover
simply ramified over the local piece of
\mathsf{Kum}
. Each of these four
pieces leads to a simple double point in the fiber of
\Sigma
, either
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
or over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
.

Over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
, we are restricting to a transverse plane where it is
again a smooth double cover ramified over a smooth curve, then blowing
up the origin. The point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
corresponds to the place where the
exceptional divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
meets the strict transform
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
of
\mathsf{Kum}
, and
the double cover gives there an ordinary double point.

Over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, we have a plane that is tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
with
nondegenerate second fundamental form, such that the two branches of
the intersection between the plane and
\mathsf{Kum}
correspond to the two
branches of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
. The restriction of the local double cover to
the tangent plane is again an ordinary double point.

To complete the verification of the conditions required for the
classification of our points in the category “type
3.11.1(e)
”, we
need to show that the relative critical locus has two branches at each
node of
\Sigma
, with the branches having distinct tangent vectors in
the tangent cone of
\Sigma
.

A degree and monodromy calculation shows that the relative critical
locus, that we have identified also with
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
, has two branches at each node of

\Sigma
. We need to show that they are smooth with distinct tangent
vectors. This statement amounts to a general position argument
concerning the relationship between the local geometry at a general
point of
\mathsf{Kum}
, and the tautological
1
-form on the covering

Y_{0}/X_{0}
.

The cases of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are different although similar. The basic
idea is to find three (or more) directions in a plane that come from
the geometry of the moduli spaces, independent of the point in the
Hitchin base; then the tautological
1
-form is a fourth direction. If
the cross-ratio between these directions did not move, it means that
the data coming from the tautological
1
-form would be constant, and
we try to get a contradiction. That will show that the cross-ratio is
general for general points, which in turn gives the required general
position property.

The proofs for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
will be completed in detail in the
following subsections.
∎

Let
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}\subset Y_{0}
be the reduced inverse image of
\mathsf{Kum}
in

Y_{0}
. We saw in Proposition
5.12
that

Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Kum}
is a
4
-sheeted covering with an explicit
description.

Let
H_{\mathsf{Kum}}
be the set of points in the Hecke correspondence

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
which are intersection points of two
lines in the Hecke fiber over points of
\mathsf{Kum}
. Thus,
H_{\mathsf{Kum}}
is
the Kummer K3-surface obtained by blowing up the
16
nodes of
\mathsf{Kum}
,
with a map
H_{\mathsf{Kum}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Kum}
that is an isomorphism outside
of the nodes. We obtain a map
H_{\mathsf{Kum}}\rightarrow X_{1}
that we’ll
view as a rational map
\nu:\mathsf{Kum}\dashrightarrow X_{1}
, whose image
is the Kummer K3 surface in
X_{1}

[
Bea96
,
Dol20
,
GH94
,
Hud05
,
Keu97
]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose
x\in\mathsf{Kum}
, not a node. Let
z:=\nu(x)\in X_{1}
be the
image of the point of
H_{\mathsf{Kum}}
lying over
x
. Let
V_{x}\subset X_{0}

be the Hecke line over
z
. Then
V_{x}
is tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
at
x
.

Proof.

Let
d^{-1}(z)\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\widehat{a})
be the inverse
image, which is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
mapping isomorphically to the Hecke line

V_{x}
. If
x^{\prime}\in H_{\mathsf{Kum}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\widehat{a})
is the
point over
x
, so that
z=d(x^{\prime})
, then
d^{-1}(z)
passes
through
x^{\prime}
. The divisor
b^{-1}(\mathsf{Kum})\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\widehat{a})
is, locally near
x^{\prime}
, a union of two branches each of
which is a part of the
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle over the Jacobian of
C
. The
curve
d^{-1}(z)
meets each of the branches, so its intersection
with the divisor
b^{-1}(\mathsf{Kum})
has multiplicity
\geq 2
. This
intersection number is the same as the local intersection number of

V_{x}
with
\mathsf{Kum}
at
x
. The fact that it is
\geq 2
means that the
line
V_{x}
is tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
at
x
.
∎

The inverse image of
\mathsf{Kum}
in
Y_{0}
is
2Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
as a divisor,
since the map
Y_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}
is fully simply ramified along

Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
. At points where
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}/\mathsf{Kum}
is etale, there is a
specified tangent direction to
Y
, normal to
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
, namely the
directions that map to zero in the tangent space of
X_{0}
. Call this
subsheaf

N^{v}\hookrightarrow T(Y_{0})|_{Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}}.

Lemma 8.9
.

The restriction of the spectral
1
-form
\alpha
of
Y_{0}
to
N^{v}
is
nonzero.

Proof.

If it were zero everywhere, this would imply that the Higgs field on
the Higgs bundle
\pi_{*}(\mathscr{L}_{0})
does not have singularities over

\mathsf{Kum}
. Recall from Corollary
5.11
that for an
appropriate choice of
\mathscr{L}_{0}
, the second Chern character violates the
Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality. But, if there were no singularities of
the Higgs field along
\mathsf{Kum}
, then the Higgs field would be
logarithmic along just the trope part of the wobbly divisor. From
Corollary
5.5
, the trope part of the wobbly divisor has
normal crossings up to codimension
2
. Since the spectral variety
Y

is irreducible, the Higgs bundle is stable. This would contradict
Mochizuki’s Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality

[
Moc06
]
. Therefore, the restriction of
\alpha
to
N^{v}

is nonzero.

In fact, it is nonzero at all the points where it is well-defined over
the smooth locus of
\mathsf{Kum}
minus the trope conics, because the residue
of the Higgs field has to have constant Jordan type along the smooth
points of the wobbly divisor.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Consider the subspace
\ker(\alpha)\cap T(Y_{\mathsf{Kum}})
varying as a
function of the point in
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
. Over a general point
x\in\mathsf{Kum}
,
then the projections of these subspaces to
T_{x}(\mathsf{Kum})
vary as a
function of the point in the Hitchin base.

Proof.

If the subspaces depend only on
C
and
x\in\mathsf{Kum}
then, in
particular, their values on different sheets of
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
would be
the same. We’ll see that this is not the case.

Points of
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
are represented by
(L,\tilde{u}+\tilde{v})

where
\tilde{u},\tilde{v}\in{\widetilde{C}}
are points lying over their
images denoted
u,v\in C
, and
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(u+v-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. The restriction of
\alpha
to
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
is given by adding the
values of the tautological form on
{\widetilde{C}}
evaluated on
\tilde{u}

and
\tilde{v}
. Specialize near a point where
\tilde{u}
is not a
ramification of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
but
\tilde{v}
is a ramification
point. As
v
moves around the branch point,
\tilde{v}
changes
branches and the tautological form
\alpha
changes sign when viewed
as a dual element of
T_{v}(C)
. Then,
\alpha
is the sum of a fixed
part namely the tautological form at
\tilde{u}
, plus a part that
changes sign namely the value at
\tilde{v}
. Thus, the directions of

\ker(\alpha)
over these two points in different sheets of

Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
are different.
∎

8.3.2
Arguments for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}

Look first at the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. View it as a general point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathsf{Kum}\subset X_{0}
. The Hecke fiber over
x
has two lines that meet in a
point, and the image of this point in
X_{1}
is a point
z
where the
two lines meet. The line
\ell
in our picture is one of these
two. Going back, the line
\ell
corresponds to a plane
P\subset X_{0}
, transverse to
\mathsf{Kum}
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
and tangent to it at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}
. On the
other hand, the Hecke fiber over
z
is a line in
X_{0}
, and this line
is tangent to
P\cap\mathsf{Kum}
at
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
. Indeed, after blowing up the Hecke
line and the strict transform of
\mathsf{Kum}
both meet the exceptional
divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}}
at the same point, meaning that the Hecke line was tangent
to
\mathsf{Kum}
before blowing up.

The double cover
Y_{P}\rightarrow P
ramified along
\mathsf{Kum}
has a point

y
over
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
, and its tangent space at
y
is a
2
-dimensional
space containing the following subspaces: the vertical space
N^{v}(y)

of the ramification; the tangent space
T_{y}(Y_{P,\mathsf{Kum}})
of the
ramification divisor
Y_{P,\mathsf{Kum}}:=Y_{P}\cap Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
; and the two
directions of the pullback of the Hecke line
V_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}}
which pulls
back to a pair of crossed lines in the double cover since it is
tangent to
\mathsf{Kum}
(Lemma
8.8
). The cross-ratio of these four
points is fixed, because the
4
directions are symmetric for the
involution of the double cover. Furthermore, Lemma
8.9

implies that the restriction of the spectral
1
-form to
Y_{P}
is
nonzero. It therefore defines a
1
-dimensional subspace
A\subset T_{y}(Y_{P})
.

Proposition 8.11
.

For general global parameters
C,{\widetilde{C}}
, as the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
moves
around in
\mathsf{Kum}
, the subspace
A
moves with respect to the framing
of
T_{y}(Y_{P})
given by the four previously discussed directions.

Proof.

Proceed by contradiction: suppose that the subspace
A
has a fixed
direction with respect to the framing. We first show that it must then
be the direction
T_{y}(Y_{P,\mathsf{Kum}}
. By Lemma
8.9
,
A
is
not the direction
N^{v}(y)
. It seems likely that the two directions
coming from
V_{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}}
should interchange under a global symmetry or
monodromy operation, and if
A
were in a fixed direction it would
have to be invariant under that operation, which would imply that it
is the direction
T_{y}(Y_{P,\mathsf{Kum}}
. However, we have not been able to
specify such an interchange operation. So, instead, let us calculate at
a special point.

Recall from Proposition
5.12
that the points of

Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
are represented by
(L,\tilde{u}+\tilde{v})
where

\tilde{u},\tilde{v}\in{\widetilde{C}}
are points lying over their images
denoted
u,v\in C
, and
L^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(u+v-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Specialize near a point on
\mathsf{Kum}
where both
u
and
v
are
(different) branch points of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
. This corresponds to a
place where two other pieces of the movable ramification locus of

Y/X
meet the ramification over
\mathsf{Kum}
. Both
\tilde{u}
and

\tilde{v}
then branch. We can write this in local coordinates

y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
for
Y
over coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
for
X
, such
that
x_{1}=0
is the equation of
\mathsf{Kum}
,
y_{1}=0
is the equation of

Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
, and the map is given by
x_{i}=y_{i}^{2}
. The spectral
1
-form
along
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
is obtained from the tautological
1
-form on

{\widetilde{C}}
by adding the values at
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{v}
. The
tautological
1
-form on
{\widetilde{C}}
looks locally like
z^{2}dz
if
z

is the coordinate on
{\widetilde{C}}
. We can therefore write the leading
term as

\alpha=a(y)d(y_{1})+y_{2}^{2}d(y_{2})+y_{3}^{2}d(y_{3}).

Since
\alpha
comes from a linear form on the abelian variety, it
does not vanish at any point, thus
a(0)
has to be nonzero.

Consider a point
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
near to
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}=(0,0,0)
. The
Hecke line
V_{x}
becomes, in these coordinates, a curve tangent to

(x_{1}=0)
. Let us write it (again looking at the highest order term) as

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(b_{1}t^{2},\epsilon_{2}+b_{2}t,\epsilon_{3}+b_{3}t)

for some coefficients
b_{1}\neq 0
and
(b_{2},b_{3})\neq(0,0)
, and small

(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})
. Note that as
(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\rightarrow(0,0)
, the second order term
b_{1}t^{2}
approaches a
nonzero limit (
(0,0,0)
being itself a point of
\mathsf{Kum}
that is general with
respect to
C
although not necessarily with respect to
{\widetilde{C}}
).

Specifying a lifting of
(0,\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})
in
Y

i.e. extracting
\epsilon_{2}^{1/2}
and
\epsilon_{3}^{1/2}
, the curve
lifts into
Y
in two branches corresponding to
\pm b_{1}^{1/2}
. One
of these branches is

(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\left(b_{1}^{1/2}t,\epsilon_{2}^{1/2}(1+b_{2}t/2\epsilon_{2}+\ldots),\epsilon_{3}^{1/2}(1+b_{3}t/2\epsilon_{3}+\ldots)\right).

Its tangent vector is
(b_{1}^{1/2},b_{2}\epsilon_{2}^{-1/2}/2,b_{3}\epsilon_{3}^{-1/2}/2)
.

The vertical tangent vector is
(1,0,0)
. We use the middle direction
to normalize the horizontal tangent vector with respect to the
vertical one, in other words the middle vector should be the sum of
the same multiple of the vertical and normalized horizontal
vectors. The multiple is
b_{1}^{1/2}
, so the normalized horizontal
vector is

\left(0,b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{2}\epsilon_{2}^{-1/2}/2,b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{3}\epsilon_{3}^{-1/2}/2\right).

The values of
\alpha
on the vertical tangent vector is
a(0)
. On
the normalized horizontal vector, evaluating at the point

(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=(0,\epsilon_{2}^{1/2},\epsilon_{2}^{1/2})
, it is

\alpha(0,\epsilon_{2}^{1/2},\epsilon_{2}^{1/2})\cdot\left(0,b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{2}\epsilon_{2}^{-1/2}/2,b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{3}\epsilon_{3}^{-1/2}/2\right)=b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{2}\epsilon_{2}^{1/2}/2+b_{1}^{-1/2}b_{3}\epsilon_{3}^{1/2}/2.

This approaches
0
as
(\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})\rightarrow(0,0)
. This tells us that the kernel line
A
of
\alpha
on

T(Y_{P})
approaches the horizontal direction as we approach the
special point.

This shows that if
A
is some fixed direction with respect to the
framing, it must be the horizontal direction. Let’s now take up that
possibility. In that case, it means that the line
T_{y}(Y_{P,\mathsf{Kum}})
is
always in the kernel of
\alpha
. In particular, the restriction of

\alpha
to
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
has this fixed foliation as a kernel. But
Lemma
8.10
says that this does not happen, completing the
contradiction.
∎

The local piece of
\Sigma
corresponds to a locally defined rank
2

Higgs bundle on
X_{0}
. It comes originally from a rank
2
piece of

\mathscr{F}_{0}
whose spectral variety is a double cover of
X_{0}
simply
ramified at the point of
\mathsf{Kum}
. We’ll use the Higgs bundle to
calculate the lower critical locus, knowing that it is the image of
the upper critical locus so if we separate the two branches of the
lower critical locus that will separate them upstairs too.

Use a coordinate system
(x,t)
on
P\subset X_{0}
with

P\cap\mathsf{Kum}
given by
t=0
. The double cover looks locally
like
t=w^{2}
. We can write the form
\alpha
as

\alpha=(a^{+}(x,t)+wa^{-}(x,t))dx+(b^{+}(x,t)+wb^{-}(x,t))dw

=(a^{+}(x,t)+wa^{-}(x,t))dx+(wb^{+}(x,t)+tb^{-}(x,t))(dt/t).

Locally we assume that the line bundle is trivial
\mathscr{L}_{0}={\mathcal{O}}_{P}
. Then the direct image down to
P
, which is the rank
2
piece
of our Higgs bundle
\mathscr{F}_{0}|_{P}
, has basis
1,w
. In these terms, the
form
\alpha
leads to a Higgs field in matrix form, with coefficients
being functions of
(x,t)
:

\varphi=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a^{+}dx+b^{-}dt&ta^{-}dx+b^{+}dt\\
a^{-}dx+b^{+}(dt/t)&a^{+}dx+tb^{-}dt\end{array}\right).

We next blow up the point
(0,0)
with coordinates

(x,v)
with
t=xv
. This gives
dt=vdx+xdv
. The Higgs field becomes

\varphi=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(a^{+}+vb^{-})dx+xb^{-}dv&(xva^{-}+vb^{+})dx+xb^{+}dv\\
a^{-}dx+b^{+}(dx/x+dv/v)&(a^{+}+vb^{-})dx+x^{2}vb^{-}dv\end{array}\right).

This is the formula for the Higgs field on

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
. We may assume that the map
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\rightarrow\ell
is given by the function
u-v
, so for the
relative differentials it induces the relation
dx=dv
. The relative
Higgs field becomes

\varphi_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(a^{+}+vb^{-}+xb^{-})dv&(xva^{-}+vb^{+}+xb^{+})dv\\
(a^{-}+b^{+}/x+b^{+}/v)dv&(a^{+}+vb^{-}+x^{2}vb^{-})dv\end{array}\right).

The support of the cokernel of this matrix is given by its
determinant

{\rm det}(\varphi_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\overline{a})_{\ell}/\ell})=(a^{+}+vb^{-}+xb^{-})(a^{+}+vb^{-}+x^{2}vb^{-})-(xva^{-}+(x+v)b^{+})(a^{-}+((x+v)/xv)b^{+})

=(xv)^{-1}\left[xv(a^{+})^{2}-(x+v)^{2}(b^{+})^{2}+\ldots\right]

where the next terms have degree
\geq 3
in
x,v
.
The vanishing locus, away from the axes, is therefore given to
first order by the equation

(b^{+})^{2}x^{2}+(2(b^{+})^{2}-(a^{+})^{2})xv+(b^{+})^{2}v^{2}=0.

Dividing by
(b^{+})^{2}
and setting
c:=(a^{+}/b^{+})^{2}
this becomes

x^{2}+(2-c)xv+v^{2}.

Its discriminant is
(2-c)^{2}-4=c^{2}-4c
so if
c\neq 0,4
, in
other words the quotient is not
0
,
-2
or
2
, then there are two branches.

Corollary 8.12
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}
was a general point on
\mathsf{Kum}
then the quotient
a^{+}(0,0)/b^{+}(0,0)
is general.

Proof.

This restates the result of Proposition
8.11
.
∎

Therefore, at a general point on
\mathsf{Kum}
, the upper critical locus has
two branches meeting the exceptional divisor at two distinct
points. This completes the proof of Theorem
8.7
for the
point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
.

8.3.3
Arguments for
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}

At the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
the plane
P
is tangent to

\mathsf{Kum}
. Introduce coordinates
x,y
for the plane and
z
in the
transverse direction so that
\mathsf{Kum}
is given by
z=0
and the plane is
given by
z=xy
. The covering
Y
has coordinates
x,y,w
with

w^{2}=z
, and
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
has equation
w=0
.

We may scale the coordinates such that the map from the plane to the
line
\ell
is given by
t=x-y
. Notice that the fibers of the map are
not tangent to the two principal directions of
P\cap\mathsf{Kum}
, as we
have seen above that the two branches of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
at the point
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are
etale over
\ell
.

Write the spectral differential form on
Y
as

\alpha=a(x,y,w)dx+b(x,y,w)dy+c(x,y,w)dw.

We have
c(0,0,0)\neq 0
by Lemma
8.9
. Also,
\alpha

does not vanish identically on
Y_{\mathsf{Kum}}
, by Lemma
8.10
,
and the kernel of
\alpha
is not generically given by any
construction depending only on
C
(such as the lines
x=0
,
y=0
or

x=y
in our coordinate system). Thus, at our general point we can
assume that
a(0),b(0),a(0)+b(0)
are nonzero.

Proceed now with the calculation of the directions of the upper
critical locus. The covering
\Sigma
has equation
w^{2}=xy
mapping
to the plane
P
with coordinates
x,y
. Blow up the origin in the

x,y
plane, so we introduce a coordinate
v
with
y=xv
. The
exceptional divisor is
x=0
and
v
is the coordinate along it. Now
the form is
\alpha=adx+b(xdv+vdx)+cdw
and
\Sigma
has
equation
w^{2}=x^{2}v
. This normalizes to two branches
w=xv^{1/2}

and
w=-xv^{1/2}
. Let
u=v^{1/2}
be the coordinate at a general
point of the covering of the exceptional divisor, so we have
w=xu

(there are two branches as
u,-u
go to the same point
v
). We have

v=u^{2}
so
dv=2udu
. We have
y=xu^{2}
.

This normalization is smooth over
u\neq 0
(actually everywhere) and
the form becomes

\alpha=adx+b(2xudu+u^{2}dx)+c(xdu+udx).

The mapping function from the plane to the line is
t=x-y
so

dt=dx-dy
. Relative differentials are obtained by working modulo

dt
, identifying

dx\sim dy=2xudu+u^{2}dx.

Modulo
dt
we get

dx=\frac{2xu}{1-u^{2}}du.

Thus

\frac{\alpha^{\rm rel}}{du}=a\frac{2xu}{1-u^{2}}+b\left(2xu+\frac{2xu^{3}}{1-u^{2}}\right)+c\left(x+\frac{2xu^{2}}{1-u^{2}}\right).

The zeros of
\alpha^{\rm rel}
are given by the equation

a\cdot 2xu+b\cdot(2xu-2xu^{3}+2xu^{3})+c\cdot(x-xu^{2}+2xu^{2})

=(a+b)(2xu)+c(x+xu^{2}).

One can factor
x
out of this expression.

We notice that
a(0)+b(0)
is the value of
\alpha
on the tangent
direction of the Hecke line
t=0
, and
c(0)
is the value of
\alpha

in the vertical direction. In our current coordinate system, the unit
vectors of these directions are normalized to be related by the lines
described in the previous subsection: the Hecke line is
y=x,z=x^{2}

and its two lifts are
y=x,w=\pm x
.

Therefore, curiously enough, we are now in the same general position
setting as in the previous subsection. Proposition
8.11

implies that the ratio
(a(0)+b(0))/c(0)
moves as a function of the
point, so we may assume that it is general. The factored expression
for the zeros of the relative
1
-form
\alpha^{\rm rel}/xdu
along
the exceptional curve becomes

1+\frac{a(0)+b(0)}{c(0)}u+u^{2}=0.

There are two distinct points
u
in the exceptional divisor
corresponding to limits of zeros of
\alpha^{\rm rel}
. This gives
two branches of the upper critical locus with distinct tangent vectors
in the nodal point of our local piece of
\Sigma
.

This completes the proof of the
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
part of Theorem
8.7
.

8.4
Spectral line bundle for the higher direct image

Recall the
spectral line bundle
, on
Y_{0}
or
Y_{1}
,
is the line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{0}
or
\mathscr{L}_{1}
whose direct
image down to
X_{0}
or
X_{1}
respectively is the level
0
piece of
the parabolic Higgs bundles
(\mathscr{F}_{0,\bullet},\Phi_{0})
or

(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})
respectively. For
X_{0}
the
parabolic Higgs bundle has trivial parabolic structure (away from the
codimension
2
tacnode points). On
X_{1}
the parabolic structure has
levels
0
and
-1/2
, so this definition of spectral line bundle
involves a choice.

We would like to calculate the spectral line bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
on

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
corresponding to
the higher direct image of the pullback Higgs bundle

\mathscr{F}_{0,{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a_{\ell}}
down to
\ell
, in terms of the
spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{0}
over
Y_{0}
and its restriction

\mathscr{L}_{\ell}
to
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

For this, we use the statement of Theorem
3.17
, in
particular the part proven in Theorem
12.12
. The
previous discussion shows that the points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
are of
type
3.11.1(e)
, and the remaining points are covered by the other
parts of the classification in Subsection

3.11
. To use the theorem in the presence of
the points
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, we should take the answer

{\mathcal{U}}=\mathscr{L}_{\ell}\otimes\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\overline{a})_{\ell}/\ell}.

The normalization of the relative critical locus separates the two
branches at each of the nodes. By Theorem
7.12
and
Corollary
7.13
, the two components of the
abelianized Hecke are disjoint away from
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}
, so the normalization
that was denoted by
G
in Subsection
3.11
is
the same as the disjoint union

G={\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}={\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\sqcup{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}.

The prescription of Theorem
3.17
gives a parabolic
sheaf
F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
such that

F^{\prime}_{0}=f_{*}({\mathcal{U}}|_{G})

and
F^{\prime}_{-1/2}
is the standard subsheaf coming from the ramification
points of
f:G\rightarrow\ell
over
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
. Theorem

3.17
says that
F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
is a parabolic subsheaf
of the parabolic Higgs bundle
F_{\cdot}
we are looking for. On the
other hand, we know from general principles that
F_{\cdot}
has
parabolic degree
0
. Also,
F
and
F^{\prime}
agree except possibly over
the points
f(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}),f(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime})\in\ell
and these are different from the
points where there is a parabolic structure.

So, if we can show that the parabolic degree of
F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
is zero,
this will imply that
F_{\cdot}=F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
and we get the
computation of
F_{\cdot}
. The degree will be calculated in the next
subsection.

8.5
Degree calculation

Recall our notation that inside
Y_{0}
that
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
denotes the
exceptional divisor of the blow-up
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}:Y_{0}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
,
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}
denotes the inverse image of the hyperplane class of

X_{0}
.

Lemma 8.13
.

For the relative canonical class over the degree
1
moduli space, we
have

\omega_{{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}/X_{1}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(-2)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(1)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}.

If
\Xi
denotes the theta divisor on the Prym, or its pullbacks to

Y_{0}
,
Y_{1}
or
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
,
we have
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}=\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}=2\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
. We note that
\Xi^{3}=24
, concording with the calculations
in Propositions
4.8
and
5.8
.

Proof.

The canonical bundle
\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}
restricts to

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(-2)
on the fibers of the projection to
X_{1}
and to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(2)
on the fibers of the projection to
X_{0}
. It means that
it restricts to the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(2)
on the Hecke lines
over points of
X_{1}
, and to the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(1)
on the
Hecke conics over points of
X_{0}
. The Picard group of
{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}
is generated by these two things, and the pullbacks restrict
to trivial bundles on their own Hecke fibers, so we conclude

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(-2)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(-1)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}.

On the other hand, as
X_{1}
is an intersection of two quadrics in

{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
, its canonical class is
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(-6+2+2)={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(-2)
. We conclude the stated formula.

For the last parts, we note that the linear system
|\Xi|
that
produces the rational map
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
has base
points on the
16
points that we blow-up to get
Y_{0}
so on the
resulting map on the blow-up, we subtract
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
. The

6
-dimensional subsystem (anti-invariant part) of
|2\Xi|
that
provides the rational map
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}\dashrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
has
{\widehat{C}}
as
base locus so we subtract
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
. For the verification, the
formulas of Propositions
5.8
and
4.8

give

(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})^{3}=24\;\;\;\mbox{ and }\;(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1})^{3}=192

compatible with
\Xi^{3}=24
.
∎

Recall that
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}
are the exceptional divisors
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}
. We could denote their restrictions over
the point
\widehat{a}=(A,\tilde{t})\in{\widehat{C}}
by
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})

and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
. Those are thus the divisors in

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
that are strict transforms
of the divisors
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
.

We get that

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})

whereas

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})

because starting from
Y_{1}
we blow up lines contained in
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
to get

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.
This gives

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}=2\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})

and

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}=4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\tilde{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\tilde{a}).

This gives for the relative differentials

\omega_{{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}/X_{1}}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}={\mathcal{O}}(-2\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}})

={\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})).

Recall that—up to tensoring with degree
0
line bundles

(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{0}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{0})
resp.
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1})
—the
spectral line bundle on
Y_{0}
is
\mathscr{L}_{0}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{0}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})
and the spectral line bundle on
Y_{1}
is
\mathscr{L}_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1})
. It follows that up to numerical equivalence

\mathscr{L}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}={\mathcal{O}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))

and

\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}={\mathcal{O}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})).

Proposition 8.14
.

Define the line bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
over
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
as the pullback of the
spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{0}
on
Y_{0}
, tensored with

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell}
. Then
{\mathcal{U}}
is the
spectral line bundle for the
L^{2}
Dolbeault higher direct image Higgs
bundle on
\ell
. Over each of the two pieces of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
,
{\mathcal{U}}
is isomorphic to the
restriction of the spectral line bundle that we construct for
X_{1}
,
from
Y_{1}
to
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
(respectively

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
).

Proof.

It will be convenient to prove the second part first. Let us use the
expressions for the spectral line bundles that we are constructing on

Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
, as calculated prior to the statement of the
proposition. We get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{0}\otimes\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}/\ell}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}((4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})).

We would like to compare this with the spectral line bundle coming
from
Y_{1}
which is, as we have seen above,

\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})).

These two differ by
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})
. However, remember that
we are looking at the restriction over a line
\ell\subset X_{1}
. The
divisor components of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})
are obtained by blowing up

16
disjoint
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s in
Y_{1}
. These
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s are sixteen
fibers of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}={\widehat{C}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, so they map to
16
lines
in the wobbly locus
{\rm Wob}_{1}\subset X_{1}
. A general line
\ell
will
miss these. Thus, as restricted over
\ell
, the two expressions are
the same.

The same discussion holds on
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
. Thus,
\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{G}\cong{\mathcal{U}}|_{G}
.

Let
F_{\cdot}
denote the
L^{2}
Dolbeault pushforward (on
\ell
)
with its parabolic structure, and let
F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
be the parabolic
bundle on
\ell
whose spectral line bundle is
{\mathcal{U}}
over
G
. The
parabolic degree of
F_{\cdot}
is zero since it corresponds to a
harmonic bundle.

The spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{1}
pushes forward to a parabolic
Higgs bundle with first parabolic Chern class equal to
0
, over
X_{1}

and hence over a line
\ell\subset X_{1}
. The parabolic structure here
is also the standard one coming from the ramification over
{\rm Wob}_{1}
.
As we have identified the two line bundles
\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{G}
and
{\mathcal{U}}|_{G}
, and since the parabolic structures are standard coming from
ramification points of
G
over
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
, it follows that
the parabolic degree of
F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
is zero. Thus,
F_{\cdot}=F^{\prime}_{\cdot}
. We conclude that
{\mathcal{U}}
is the spectral line bundle for

F_{\cdot}
.
∎

8.6
Restriction to a line

We have been considering the local system corresponding to the
parabolic Higgs bundle
\left(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1}\right)

via the non-abelian Hodge and Riemann-Hilbert correspondences. So far
we focused on the restriction of this local system to a general line

\ell\subset X_{1}
. We show in this subsection that knowing this
restriction suffices in order to identify the local system on

X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
. Throughout the subsection,
\ell
denotes a general line
in
X_{1}
. Let
\ell^{\circ}:=\ell-(\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1})
.

Lemma 8.15
.

The local system on
\ell^{\circ}
constructed in Section

4
, is irreducible.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the spectral covering is irreducible. Recall
from Lemma
4.15
that the spectral covering has the
following description. The line
\ell
has a natural trigonal covering

k:C\rightarrow\ell
whose fiber over
x\in\ell
is identified with
the set of three lines through
x
that are different from
\ell
. The

8
branch points are the points of
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
.

We have the spectral cover
{\widetilde{C}}/C
. For a point
x\in\ell
we
can make the following set with
8
elements: it is the set of
liftings of the subset
k^{-1}(x)
to a subset of three elements of

{\widetilde{C}}
. This family determines a covering of
\ell
of degree
8
,
and that is the same as
Y_{1}\times_{X_{1}}\ell\rightarrow\ell
.

For
\ell
general, the set of
4
branch points of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
maps
to a subset of
4
distinct points in
\ell
. Indeed, the set of four
branch points is the inverse image of a general pair of points in

{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
under the hyperelliptic map
\text{\sf h}_{C}:C\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and a
general trigonal map
C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
does not identify opposite
points under the hyperelliptic involution on
C
, so the images of the
four branch points are distinct. Thus, as
x
moves around in

\ell^{\circ}
, we can change individually the parity of any one of
the liftings of the three points. This shows that the monodromy action
on the
8
points is transitive, so
Y_{1}\times_{X_{1}}\ell
is
irreducible. That was the spectral covering of the Higgs bundle on

\ell
corresponding to the restricted local system, so the local
system is irreducible.
∎

Proposition 8.16
.

Suppose
V
and
V^{\prime}
are two local systems on
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
, such that

V|_{\ell^{\circ}}\cong V^{\prime}|_{\ell^{\circ}}

and such that this local system on
\ell^{\circ}
is
irreducible. Then
V\cong V^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho:\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},o)\to GL_{N}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\rho^{\prime}\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},o)\to GL_{N}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the monodromy
representations of
V
and
V^{\prime}
with respect to a basepoint
o\in\ell^{\circ}
chosen in
\ell^{\circ}
. Let
\zeta:\pi_{1}(\ell^{circ},o)\to GL_{N}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the monodromy
representation of
V|_{\ell^{\circ}}
and
V^{\prime}|_{\ell^{\circ}}
,
assuming we choose framings making these representations the same.

Suppose
a\in\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},o)
. Then
\rho(a)
and
\rho^{\prime}(a)

are morphisms of representations of
\pi_{1}(\ell^{\circ},o)
between

\zeta
and its conjugate
\zeta^{a}
. Since
\zeta
is irreducible,
these two morphisms differ by a scalar. This gives a rank
1

character
\chi:\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},o)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
such
that
\rho\otimes\chi\cong\rho^{\prime}
, and
\chi
is trivial on
\pi_{1}(\ell^{\circ},o)
.

To finish the proof, we need to note that
\chi
is trivial. This is
because the map

H_{1}(\ell^{\circ},{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},{\mathbb{Z}})

(56)

is surjective. Indeed, this follows from the following

Claim 8.17
.

The fist homology group
H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},{\mathbb{Z}})
is cyclic and
generated by the linking loop in
X_{1}
going around some smooth point
of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
.

Proof.

Note first that the statement of the claim
makes sense since any two linking loops at smooth points of
{\rm Wob}_{1}

are conjugate in
\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1})
and hence are
homologous. This follows immediately since
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is irreducible
and hence the smooth locus of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
is connected. Using the
tubular neighborhood theorem we can view the linking loops as two
different fibers of the circle bundle in the normal bundle

N_{{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\operatorname{smooth}}/X_{1}}
and hence they are homotopy
equivalent up to a conjugation via a path connecting the two points in

{\rm Wob}_{1}^{\operatorname{smooth}}
over which these circle fibers sit.

Next recall that if
M
is a connected, oriented, not necessarily
compact,
C^{\infty}
-manifold and if
Z\subset M
is a connected,
oriented
C^{\infty}
submanifold of dimension
d
, which is closed in
the Eucledian topology, then
Z
defines a
d
-dimensional cohomology
class
[Z]\in H^{d}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, let
Z\subset\mathfrak{T}\subset M
be a tubular neighborhood of
Z
. Then for
every
k
we have canonical identifications
H^{k}(M,M-Z;{\mathbb{Z}})=H^{k}(\mathfrak{T},\partial\mathfrak{T};{\mathbb{Z}})
(by excision) and also

H^{k}(\mathfrak{T},\partial\mathfrak{T};{\mathbb{Z}})=H^{k-d}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})
(by
the Thom isomorphism theorem). In particular we get that

H^{d}((M,M-Z;{\mathbb{Z}})=H^{d}(\mathfrak{T},\partial\mathfrak{T};{\mathbb{Z}})=H^{0}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}},

where the last equality holds since
Z
is connected. But from the
long exact cohomology sequence of the pair
(M,M-Z)
we have a
canonical map

H^{d}(M,M-Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{d}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})

(57)

and we define
[Z]\in H^{d}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})
to be the image of
1\in{\mathbb{Z}}=H^{d}(M,M-Z;{\mathbb{Z}})
under the map (
57
). The cap product

[Z]\cap(-):H_{k}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{k-d}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{k-d}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})

fits in the classical
tube exact sequence
in homology

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{k-d+1}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}}
\textstyle{H_{k}(M-Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{k}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[Z]\cap(-)}
\textstyle{H_{k-d}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots,}

where the middle map is induced from the inclusion
M-Z\subset M
and
the tube map
\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}
sends a
(k-d+1)
-cycle
A
in
Z
to the

k
-cycle in
M-Z
which is the total space of the
S^{d-1}
-bundle

\partial\mathfrak{T}_{|A}\subset\partial\mathfrak{T}\subset M-Z
.

Taking this into account, consider
Z\subset M
to be the

C^{\infty}
manifolds underlying the smooth complex varietties

({\rm Wob}_{1}-\operatorname{Sing}({\rm Wob}_{1}))\subset(X_{1}-\operatorname{Sing}({\rm Wob}_{1}))
. Thus
Z
and
M
are oriented,
Z
is of real
codimension
2
, and since
X_{1}
and
{\rm Wob}_{1}
are irreducible,
both
Z
and
M
are connected. Furthermore
M-Z=X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}

as topoloical spaces and so the piece of the tube sequence
corresponding to
k=1
reads

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{0}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}}
\textstyle{H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{1}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[Z]\cap(-)}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots.}

Since
\operatorname{Sing}({\rm Wob}_{1})
is a compact subvariety of complex
codimension
2
in
X_{1}
we have that
H_{1}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})=H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})=H_{1}(X_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})=0
. Therefoe

\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}:H_{0}(Z:{\mathbb{Z}})\to H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})

is surjective. But the
H_{0}(Z;{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}
is generated by the class
of of a point in
Z
, and by the definition of
\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}

for any point
pt\in Z
, we have that
\mathsf{tube}_{Z/M}(pt)
is
the class of the circle in
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
linking to
Z={\rm Wob}_{1}-\operatorname{Sing}({\rm Wob}_{1})
this point. This proves the claim.
∎

Finally, observe that a general line
\ell
will intersect
{\rm Wob}_{1}

transversally at a set of
8
smooth points, and so any simple loop in

\ell
that goes once around a point
x\in\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
will be
a a loop in
X_{1}
that links to
{\rm Wob}_{1}
at
x
. Thus the image of

H_{1}(\ell^{\circ},{\mathbb{Z}})
in
H_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1},{\mathbb{Z}})
contains a
linking loop and so the map (
56
) is
surjective. This implies that
\chi
is trivial, and hence
V\cong V^{\prime}

which completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Apply this now to the Hecke transform, a rank
16
local
system on
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
.

Lemma 8.18
.

The rank
16
Hecke transform local system on
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
decomposes
as a direct sum of two rank
8
local systems which are isomorphic,
when restricted to a general line, to the local system constructed in
Section
4
.

Proof.

The arguments of this section show that the restriction of the rank

16
local system to a general line decomposes as a direct sum of two
copies of the rank
8
local system constructed in Section

4
. Furthermore, such a decomposition can be obtained by
considering the two pieces
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
and

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})
. We obtain a family of
decompositions over the lines, with the property that when two lines
intersect the decompositions correspond. Now, a complete intersection
of
X_{1}
with two general hyperplanes is an elliptic curve that can
degenerate into a cycle of
4
lines
Z=\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{3}\cup\ell_{4}
. We get the decomposition on each of these lines and
the decompositions coincide on the intersection points. This gives a
decomposition of the rank
16
local system over the singular curve

Z
, into two pieces of rank
8
. The map
\pi_{1}(Z-Z\cap{\rm Wob}_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1})
is surjective, so we get a
decomposition of local systems on
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 8.19
.

The rank
16
Hecke transform local system on
X_{1}-{\rm Wob}_{1}
decomposes
as a direct sum of two rank
8
local systems which are isomorphic to
the local system constructed in Section
4
.

Proof.

The lemma gives the decomposition, and the pieces restrict to a
general line to the given rank
8
local system. By Lemma
8.15

and Proposition
8.16
, the pieces of the decomposition
are globally isomorphic to the rank
8
local system constructed in
Section
4
.
∎

9
Hecke transformation from
X_{1}
to

X_{0}

The standard diagram for the direction of the Hecke transformation

(X_{1}\rightarrow X_{0})
shows the big Hecke correspondence fitting into

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}.}

Fix a point
a=(A,t)\in{\overline{C}}
where
A^{\otimes 2}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(t-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
and consider the Hecke correspondence
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
with its
diagram

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}.}

The objective in this section is to pull-back the constructed Higgs
bundle from
X_{1}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
and take the higher
direct image along
q
to
X_{0}
.

The big abelianized Hecke fits into a diagram of the form

\textstyle{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\rm ab}}
\scriptstyle{q^{\rm ab}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\times{\widehat{C}}.}

The two points
\widehat{a}
and
\tau\widehat{a}
over
a\in{\overline{C}}
give two abelianized Hecke varieties
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})
. These
are the same as the varieties with the same notation in the previous
section, and recall that their disjoint union is denoted by

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a}):={\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\sqcup{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a}).

The map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow Y_{1}
is the
blow-up along
16
lines contained in the wobbly locus, while the map

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})\rightarrow Y_{0}
is the blow-up along
a curve isomorphic to
{\widehat{C}}
(and that will be called by the same
name) inside
Y_{0}
.

9.1
Restriction to a line

As before, fix a general line
\ell\subset X_{0}
. Let
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
be the inverse image of
\ell
in the Hecke variety

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)
. As
\ell
is general, it does not meet the image
in
X_{0}
of the curve
{\widehat{C}}\subset Y_{0}
, so if
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
denotes the inverse image of
\ell
in

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
, the projection induces an
isomorphism

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}Y_{0,\ell}.

The same holds for the other piece
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

Recall from Corollary
7.10
that for
\ell
general,
the images of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
and

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
are disjoint in the
spectral variety

\Sigma:=Y_{1}\times_{X_{1}}{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}.

Thus, we may treat each piece separately. We will look mainly at

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
with the understanding that
the arguments for
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
are
the same.

Lemma 9.1
.

The Hecke correspondence
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
over

\ell
maps by a closed immersion onto a hyperplane section that we will
denote by
H_{\ell}\subset X_{1}
. The image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
is a curve of genus
25
. The map

\phi:{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}\rightarrow\ell

has degree
8
. It has:
16
ramification points over the
intersection points of
\ell
with the trope planes, and
4

ramification points over each of the
4
intersection points in
\ell\cap\mathsf{Kum}
. It has
32
other ’movable’ ramification points not
mapping to points in the wobbly
{\rm Wob}_{0}
, so there are altogether
64

branch points.

Proof.

Recall that one of the quadrics in the pencil is identified with the
Grassmanian of lines in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, embedded in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
by the Plücker
coordinates. The line
\ell
itself corresponds to a vector in
v(\ell)\in\bigwedge^{2}{\mathbb{C}}^{4}
. The condition for another line
\mathsfit{m}\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
to meet
\ell
is that its vector
v(\mathsfit{m})\bigwedge^{2}{\mathbb{C}}^{4}

satisfies
v(\mathsfit{m})\wedge v(\ell)=0
in
\bigwedge^{4}{\mathbb{C}}^{4}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
. This is a linear condition on
v(\mathsfit{m})
so it corresponds to a
hyperplane in
{\mathbb{P}}^{6}
. The image of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
in
X_{1}

corresponds to the subset of points in
X_{1}
whose corresponding line
meets
\ell
, in other words it is this hyperplane intersected with

X_{1}
. This yields the hyperplane section
H_{\ell}
.

Since
\ell
is general, its inverse image in
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
is the same as its inverse image in
Y_{0}
because
the abelianized Hecke is the blow-up of
Y_{0}
on a subset that maps to
a curve in
X_{0}
that will be missed by a general
\ell
. Now, we may
proceed to calculate the normal bundle of this curve in
Y_{0}
. It is
the pullback of the normal bundle of
\ell
in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
which is to say

{\mathcal{O}}(1)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, so that has degree
2
on
\ell
. Its pullback has
degree
16
. Now,
Y_{0}
is the blow-up of an abelian variety at
16

points; the exceptional divisors map to the trope planes so our
general
\ell
meets each of the exceptional divisors once. The
canonical bundle of
Y_{0}
is twice the exceptional divisor, so it has
degree
32
. The canonical bundle of our curve is the canonical of

Y_{0}
restricted to the curve, plus a divisor of degree
16
, so it
has degree
32+16=48
. Therefore, the image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
in
H_{\ell}
has genus
25
.

The ramification of this curve over
\ell
is just the restriction to

\ell
of the ramification of
Y_{0}/X_{0}
. We know that has fixed pieces
including a simple ramification over each trope plane, plus four
simple ramifications over general points of
\mathsf{Kum}
. The remaining
ramification points are really movable, as was noted in Lemma

5.13
(see also Corollary
4.23
for the
corresponding statement in the other direction).
∎

The ramification points of the horizontal divisor lie over
points of
\left(\bigcup_{\kappa}\mathsf{Trope}_{\kappa}\right)\cap\ell
.
They come from
16
lines inside
{\rm Wob}_{1}
, and correspond to the
first
16
ramification points mentioned in Lemma

9.1
. The locations of these lines in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
depend
on the choice of point
a=(A,t)
used to make the Hecke
correspondence.

We note that the modular spectral covering
\Sigma
has two
ramification points over each general point of the wobbly locus, so
these
16
points correspond to
32
points in the full abelianized
Hecke. They are distrubuted as
16
in each of the two pieces

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
and
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

Remark 9.2
.

The horizontal divisor in
H_{\ell}
is
{\rm Wob}_{1}\cap H_{\ell}
. It has
nodes and cusps coming from the nodes and cusps of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. However,
for a general
\ell
these do not meet the image of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
. In particular, they are not going to
contribute singularities to the higher direct image – this is another
version of the “apparent singularities” encountered in the previous
chapter.

The proof of this statement is that the nodal and cuspidal loci of the
wobbly locus
{\rm Wob}_{1}
are curves; they pull back to curves in

Y_{1}
. The centers of the blow-up
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})

are the
16
lines in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
that get blown up to form
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0,\kappa}
. These are transverse to both the nodal and cuspidal
loci. Therefore, the pullbacks of the nodal and cuspidal loci in

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
are
1
-dimensional. Their

1
-dimensional images in
X_{0}
do not meet a general line
\ell
so

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
does not meet the nodal and
cuspidal loci of
{\overline{W}}_{1}
and we get the statement of the remark.

Among the hypotheses
of Subsection
3.11
is the following statement.

Lemma 9.3
.

For a general line
\ell
, the intersection of the plane
H_{\ell}

with any of the
16
lines in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
that provide ramification of
the Hecke correspondence, is a general point on that line. At such a
point, the value of the spectral
1
-form
\alpha
on the vertical
direction in the tangent space of
Y_{1}
, at either of the two
ramification points of
Y_{1}/X_{1}
over this point, is nonzero.

Proof.

For the first part, we note that one of these
16
lines in
{\rm Wob}_{1}

gets blown up to an
\mathbb{F}_{1}
-surface, that then maps to the
corresponding trope plane in
X_{0}={\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. The exceptional divisor
blows down to a point, that we will call the origin, in the trope plane
(corresponding to the intersection in
Y_{0}
of the plane with the

{\widehat{C}}
curve). The location of the intersection of
H_{\ell}
with
the line corresponds to the slope of the line from this origin to the
intersection point of
\ell
with the trope plane. For
\ell
general,
this direction is a general point of the line.

Inside
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
, the curve
{\widehat{C}}
passes through
16
points that
are blown up to get
Y_{0}
. The identification
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}\cong{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
depends on
a
. Instead of blowing up the points, we blow
up the curve
{\widehat{C}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
to get
Y_{1}
, and this
generates
16
lines inside
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}\subset Y_{1}
. Let
\mathfrak{v}

denote one of these lines. The normal bundle of
\mathfrak{v}
in
Y_{1}

is
{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
, with the
{\mathcal{O}}
direction being the normal
bundle of
\mathfrak{v}
in
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
.

Let us look at how this maps to the normal bundle of the image line

\mathsfit{v}\subset{\rm Wob}_{1}
. The normal bundle of a line in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
is

{\mathcal{O}}(1)^{\oplus 4}
, and to get the normal bundle of the line in the
intersection of two quadrics we take the kernel of a map

{\mathcal{O}}(1)^{\oplus 4}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(2)^{\oplus 2}
. For a typical
line, this kernel will be
{\mathcal{O}}^{\oplus 2}
.

However, for a line in the wobbly locus, that we recall counts twice
in the set of four lines through each point of
X_{1}
, we claim that
the kernel is
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. This may be seen by recalling
that the lines in the wobbly locus are the tangent lines to the copy
of
{\overline{C}}
that forms the cuspical locus of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
, and when we move
to first order along this
{\overline{C}}
the tangent line undergoes a
deformation that vanishes at that point in the normal direction. Thus,
these first order deformations are sections of the normal bundle that
vanish. But, the bundle
{\mathcal{O}}^{\oplus 2}
does not have any nonzero
sections that vanish somewhere. The only other possibility is the
bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(1)
since it has to be a subbundle of

{\mathcal{O}}(1)^{\oplus 4}
. This proves the claim.

As we move in the curve
{\overline{C}}
, the line moves in
{\rm Wob}_{1}
so the
normal direction has sections, and it has to be the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)

subbundle. The map
{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(1)
has a zero at the points
where the line
\mathsfit{v}
crosses the cuspidal locus of
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. However,
by looking at the local picture of the covering
Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}

near such a cuspidal locus, we can see that the map from the full
tangent space of
Y_{1}
into the tangent space of
X_{1}
has image of
dimension
2
, so the map from the full normal bundle of
\mathfrak{v}\subset Y_{1}
to the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
piece in the normal bundle of
\mathsfit{v}\subset X_{1}
is surjective. We conclude that the bundle of vertical
directions, which is the kernel of this map

{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(1),

is
{\mathcal{O}}(-2)
sitting in
{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
as a saturated
subbundle. In particular it is not contained in any subbundle of the
form
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
. The map from this bundle of vertical normal
directions, into the space of normal directions at the original point
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, therefore does not have image in a plane. Thus, the
tautological
1
-form, that is a nonzero linear form on the tangent
space of
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
, does not vanish on the vertical normal direction
at a general point of
\mathfrak{v}
. This proves the second part of
the lemma, in view of the generality statement of the first part.
∎

Recall that the fiber of
q:{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}\rightarrow\ell

over a general point
\ell
is a smooth conic, but over points of the
Kummer it degenerates to a union of two lines.

Lemma 9.4
.

The branch points of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
lying
over points of the Kummer, all map to points of the Kummer K3 surface

H_{\mathsf{Kum}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\widehat{a})
, that is to say in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
they map to points where the two
lines in the fiber meet.

Proof.

The reasoning for this is as follows: the horizontal divisor in

{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
is the intersection of
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
(considered as a hyperplane
H_{\ell}
in
X_{1}
) with the
wobbly
{\rm Wob}_{1}
. But, the lines in the fibers over points of the
Kummer, represent general lines in
X_{1}
since
\ell
intersects the
Kummer in general points. A general line in
X_{1}
will intersect

{\rm Wob}_{1}
transversally.

We note that it is necessary to have four Jordan blocks of size two in
the monodromy of the Hecke-transformed local system (the rank
8

piece corresponding to our chosen branch of
H^{\rm ab}
out of two,
that we are hoping is our chosen flat bundle of rank
8
on
X_{0}
) at
each point of the Kummer. Otherwise, the Hecke transform back in the
other direction from
X_{0}
to
X_{1}
will not have the right rank.

So, these all have to come from simple ramification points of

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
over the Kummer
points. However, if those ramification points were to occur on smooth
points of the lines, that wouldn’t contribute anything to the
monodromy in the direct image (i.e. the residue of the Higgs field)
since the map is not singular at those locations. This heuristic
argument suggests that
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}

should have four points over each point where a fiber of
\phi
breaks
into two lines.

This can be shown in terms of bundles. Suppose
L\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}
is a
line bundle on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose direct image
V=\pi_{*}L
is stable
with trivial determinant, so it is a point in
X_{0}
, and suppose that

V
contains a line subbundle
U
of degree
0
. Then we claim that

V
also contains
U^{-1}
.

To see this, we note that the group
{\mathbb{Z}}/2\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts on

{\widetilde{C}}
, as may be seen for example by expressing

{\widetilde{C}}=C\times_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

as a fiber product with the double cover
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}

ramified at two points (so the ramification of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
is the
preimage in
C
of those two points of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
). Explicitly the
non-trivial elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
are given by the covering
involution
\tau:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}
for thr map
\pi:{\widetilde{C}}\to C
, the covering involution
\sigma:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\widetilde{C}}
for the
hyperelliptic map
\mathsf{h}_{{\widetilde{C}}}:{\widetilde{C}}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and their
composition
\rho=\tau\circ\sigma
.

Now, note that by construction the group element
\rho\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
acts trivially on the Prym of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
but acts
by
-1
on
\operatorname{Jac}(C)
. Applying
\rho
to the map
\pi^{*}(U)\rightarrow L
we obtain a map
\pi^{*}(U^{-1})\rightarrow L
and
hence by adjunction we get an injective map
U^{-1}\rightarrow V
.
This will give by degree considerations
V=U\oplus U^{-1}
. In the
Hecke correspondence this will correspond to a point at the
intersection of the two
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
components of the fiber of
q
over

V
(we recall that the affine parts of the two lines themselves were
bundles that were semistable but not polystable).

We can also note that
L\cong\pi^{*}(U)\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(a+b)

for an effective degree
2
divisor
a+b
on
{\widetilde{C}}
, amd that the
image divisor
\pi(a)+\pi(b)
is fixed by the determinant
condition for
V
. Thus, there are four choices of
(a,b)
lifting
this divisor to
{\widetilde{C}}
. We get the four claimed branches of

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
going through a crossing
point in
{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}
.
∎

Proposition 9.5
.

The map
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\rightarrow\Sigma
identifies the abelianized
Hecke as the upper critical locus (see Proposition
12.1
)

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}\cong\widetilde{{\rm Crit}}\left({\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}/\ell,\left(\mathscr{F}_{0,{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)_{\ell}},\Phi_{0}\right)\right).

These are smooth curves in
\Sigma
that decompose into a disjoint
union of two pieces corresponding to
\widehat{a}
and
\tau\widehat{a}
.

Proof.

This is similar to the proof of Proposition
8.1
,
using Lemma
9.3
for smoothness of the upper critical
locus at ramification points of the horizontal divisor.

For other points, we note that the abelianized Hecke is identified
with
Y_{0}
outside of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
, but
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})

has image equal to a curve in
X_{0}
that does not intersect a general

\ell
(as will be pointed out again in Lemma
9.6
below).
Thus, the inverse image of a general line is smooth in this open
subset of the abelianized Hecke. This shows that
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
is smooth away from the
ramification points of the horizontal divisor.

The pointwise identification between the abelianized Hecke and the
upper critical locus, whose proof is the same as for Proposition

8.1
, therefore gives an identification of subschemes
(again, neither of them has embedded points). We have seen that one or
the other is smooth at every point, so the are both
smooth. Disjointness of the two pieces comes from Corollary

7.10
.
∎

We next turn to the calculation of the direct image. Suppose given a
spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{1}
over
Y_{1}
whose direct image to
X_{1}

corresponds to our parabolic Higgs bundle. Recall that this means,
more precisely, that
f_{1*}(\mathscr{L}_{1})
is the parabolic level
0

piece of the Higgs bundle, with parabolic level
1/2
piece equal to

f_{1*}(\mathscr{L}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}))
.

The direct image formula for the holomorphic Dolbeault complex leads
to a line bundle over
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
. At
points where
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
goes through the
crossing points of the fibers over Kummer points, the contribution is
just
\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}\otimes\omega_{{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}_{\ell}/\ell}
, since the horizontal divisor does
not intervene. Recall that
\omega_{{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}_{\ell}/\ell}

may also be viewed as the quotient of the forms with logarithmic
singularities along the fiber, modulo logarithmic forms from the base.

In the notations of Subsection
3.11
, the
present situation is covered by the situation there, and there are no
points of type
3.11.1(e)
. Indeed we are in the “parabolic”
case where there is a parabolic structure with weights
0,1/2
on the
source space, and such points are not allowed as the horizontal divisor
does not have nodes.

The direct image calculations were summarized in
Theorem
3.17
and proven in
Theorem
12.10
based on
Proposition
12.7
. Near a point
\mathbf{q}

where the horizontal divisor (which is
{\rm Wob}_{1}\cap H_{\ell}
) has a
ramification over a point of a trope plane, the curve

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
also passes through that
point, and the contribution for the direct image is

\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}\otimes\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}(\mathbf{q}).

This formula would also hold near a point
\mathbf{q}
where the upper critical
locus intersects the ramification divisor
\mathsf{Ram}\subset\Sigma
over
points where the horizontal divisor is étale over the base. Indeed,
the proof of Theorem
12.10
included a
discussion of that possibility. The ramification divisor is the same
as the pullback of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
to
\Sigma
. However, in fact, these
kinds of points do not occur:

Lemma 9.6
.

For general
\ell
, the curve
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tilde{a})_{\ell}
viewed inside
Y_{1}
, only meets the divisor

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
at points
\mathbf{q}
where the horizontal divisor has a
ramification over a point of a trope plane intersected with
\ell
.

Proof.

The pullback of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
to
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
is the
sum
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})\cup\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
of the strict
transform
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
and the exceptional divisors over the

16
lines that form
\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})
. However,

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
is contracted and maps to a curve inside
Y_{0}
,
hence also in
X_{0}
. A general line does not meet this curve. Thus,
taking the inverse image of a general line
\ell
inside

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})
and projecting back to
Y_{1}
, gives
a curve that only meets
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
at points of the
16
lines that
generate ramification over a trope plane.
∎

Either using this lemma, or in any case by the remark of the preceding
paragraph, the contribution to the higher direct image coming from
points near
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
is the restriction to the curve
G
of

\mathscr{L}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1})|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}\otimes\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}.

This globalizes to other points of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
. For the points lying over the Kummer, notice
that the relative dualizing sheaf is the same as the relative sheaf of
logarithmic differentials that enters into the higher direct image
calculations, so the spectral line bundle is obtained by taking

\mathscr{L}_{1}
and tensoring with
\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}
near these
points. Since these points are not on
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
(as follows from
Lemma
9.4
), the contributions for those points are given by
the same expression. Putting these all together, we obtain the
computation of the spectral line bundle:

Proposition 9.7
.

Taking the Hecke transform and restricting to the line
\ell
, the
spectral line bundle on
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
is
the line bundle

\mathscr{L}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1})|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}\otimes\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}.

The same holds for the spectral line bundle on
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
, and these two are disjoint in
\Sigma
,
so this expression gives the spectral line bundle over the disjoint
union
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

9.2
Calculation of the pushforward

Calculate in the same way as at the end of the previous chapter.
Recall that if we write
\Xi
for the theta divisor on the Prym, or
its pullbacks to
Y_{0}
,
Y_{1}
or
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
, we have

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}=2\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}
and

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}=4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
.
Also

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})

whereas

\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a}),

and

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}=2\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})

\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}=4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a}).

Lemma 9.8
.

For the relative canonical class over the degree
0
moduli space, we
have

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)/X_{0}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(2)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(-1)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}

and this pulls back to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))
.

Proof.

As in Lemma
8.13
,

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(-2)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{1}}(-1)|_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)}.

On the other hand,
\omega_{X_{0}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}(-4)
, giving the first formula.
Then

\omega_{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(a)/X_{0}}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}={\mathcal{O}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})})

={\mathcal{O}}(2(2\Theta-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))-(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})))={\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})).

∎

Recall that the spectral line bundle on
Y_{0}
is

\mathscr{L}_{0}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{0}}(2\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{0})
and the spectral line bundle on

Y_{1}
is
\mathscr{L}_{1}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}_{1})
.
As before, we get

\mathscr{L}_{0}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}={\mathcal{O}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))

and

\mathscr{L}_{1}|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})}={\mathcal{O}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})).

From Proposition
9.7
, the spectral line bundle of
the Hecke transform restricted over
\ell
is the bundle

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{1}\otimes

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1})|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}\otimes\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a}))\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a}))

\displaystyle\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{H^{\rm ab}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))|_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(4\Xi+\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a})).

This compares with the spectral line bundle coming from
Y_{0}
which is

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}}(4\Xi-\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{0}(\widehat{a}))
.

As before, the divisor
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
is the exceptional divisor of
blowing-up
Y_{1}
along a copy of the curve
{\widehat{C}}
. The image of the
curve in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
does not intersect a general line
\ell
so

\boldsymbol{\mathit{Exc}}_{1}(\widehat{a})
does not intersect
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
. Thus, the spectral line bundle of the Hecke
transform coincides with the spectral line bundle coming from
Y_{0}
on

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
.

Proposition 9.9
.

Define the line bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
over
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
as the pullback of
\mathscr{L}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1})
on
Y_{1}
, tensored
with
\omega_{H_{\ell}/\ell}
. Then
{\mathcal{U}}
is the spectral line bundle
for the
L^{2}
Dolbeault higher direct image Higgs bundle on
\ell
.
Over each of the two pieces of
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a},\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
,
{\mathcal{U}}
is isomorphic to the restriction of the
spectral line bundle that we construct for
X_{0}
, from
Y_{0}
to

{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\widehat{a})_{\ell}
(respectively
{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}}^{\rm ab}(\tau\widehat{a})_{\ell}
).

Proof.

The above calculations show this, it is also described in Section

7
. Notice that the proof in this direction is
significantly less complicated than in the previous section since we
do not need to deal with the points of type
3.11.1(e)
.
∎

10
The big Hecke correspondences

In this section we will consider the “big” Hecke correspondences
fitting into a diagram of the form, for the
(X_{1}\rightarrow X_{0})

direction:

\textstyle{{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}}

or similarly in the opposite direction
(X_{0}\rightarrow X_{1})
pictured
below. We would like to show that the local system obtained by
pulling back our local system from
(X_{1},{\rm Wob}_{1})
then taking

R^{1}q_{*}
, is an exterior tensor product of our local system on

(X_{0},{\rm Wob}_{0})
by the initially given rank two local system on
C
,
pulled back to
{\overline{C}}
.

The main part of the proof on the spectral data was given in
Subsection
7.4
. The objective of this section
is to prove some complementary statements designed to deal with
possible apparent singularities of the higher direct image operation.
For this, we introduce the notion of
effective discriminant
divisor
, this is the part of the discriminant divisor on which the
higher direct image local system really does have singularities.

10.1
From
X_{1}
to
X_{0}

For the moment we will work in the direction from
X_{1}
to
X_{0}
as
pictured in the previous diagram. Let
\Delta\subset X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
be the discriminant divisor of the map
q
with respect to the
pair
({\overline{\mathcal{H}}},p^{-1}{\rm Wob}_{1})
. This includes points in

X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
over which the map
q
is not smooth, and points
over which the horizontal divisor
p^{-1}{\rm Wob}_{1}
is not étale. The
singularities of the
R^{1}q_{*}
local system are
a priori

contained in
\Delta
. Let
\Delta_{\rm eff}
be the
effective

singular divisor, namely the divisor over which the local system has
singularities. Thus
\Delta_{\rm eff}\subset\Delta
.

From the previous section we know the following statement:

Proposition 10.1
.

If
a
is a general point of
{\overline{C}}
then along the fiber
X_{0}\times\{a\}
, the singular divisor
\Delta_{\rm eff}
consists of just

{\rm Wob}_{0}\subset X_{0}
.

This proposition readily implies the following:

Corollary 10.2
.

The effective singular divisor consists of
{\rm Wob}_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
,
possibly union with a finite number of fibers of the form
X_{0}\times\{a_{i}\}
for points
a_{i}\in{\overline{C}}
.

Proof.

In general a divisor such as
\Delta_{\rm eff}
in a product

X_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
decomposes as

\Delta_{\rm eff}=\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm vert}+\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm horiz}+\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm mov}

where
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm vert}
is a sum of vertical
components
X_{0}\times\{a_{i}\}
,
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm horiz}
is a
sum of divisors of the form
D_{i}\times{\overline{C}}
, and
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm mov}
is given by a moving family of divisors parametrized
by
{\overline{C}}
. Over a general point of
{\overline{C}}
, the intersection of
these divisors with the fiber will be: for
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm vert}
, empty; for
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm horiz}
, the union of the

D_{i}
; and for
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm mov}
, a divisor that moves as
a function of the point. The proposition says that these all consist
of just
{\rm Wob}_{0}\subset X_{0}
, not moving as a function of the point of

{\overline{C}}
. It follows that
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm mov}=\varnothing
and

\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm horiz}={\rm Wob}_{0}\times{\overline{C}}
. There remains
the possibility of a nonempty
\Delta_{\rm eff}^{\rm vert}
.
∎

In order to rule out the possibility of having a vertical piece in

\Delta_{\rm eff}
we’ll just rule that out for the full discriminant
divisor.
