\operatorname{td}(Y/X)=(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9)

so
\operatorname{td}_{1}(Y/X)=-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2
.
If
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})
with
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we get

0=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}((-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2)+a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+(1-\mu)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})=(a-1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}+(b+1/2-\mu)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}.

Using the calculations of Proposition
4.8
this gives

32(a-1)+64(b+1/2-\mu)=0.

It follows from the condition that
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
that
\mu=1/2
or

\mu=1
.
∎

Remark 13.21
.

The case
\mu=1/2
corresponds to the case we have been treating,
in which we found our flat bundle. We claim that the numerical class
of the line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
is uniquely determined by the condition of

\operatorname{ch}_{2}
being extremal.

Notice that there is a relation on the coefficients
a
and
b
of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}

respectively,
that is fixed by requiring
\operatorname{ch}_{1}=0
. In the case
\mu=1/2
this
condition from the previous corollary becomes

a=1-2b
. This leaves a single
parameter, which we can view as being the coefficient
b
of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
.

The uniqueness of the value for which
\operatorname{ch}_{2}
is extremal may be
seen by calculating the parabolic Chern class, up to some correction
terms of the kind we have seen in Chapter
4
. The
correction terms are local at the non-normal crossings points of the
wobbly divisor, and don’t depend on the choice of
{\mathcal{L}}
. The resulting
function of the coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
in the divisor of
{\mathcal{L}}
is a
strictly concave quadratic function, whose maximum is at an integer
value; in case the reader is interested, the calculation is reproduced
below, but the interesting point is that this fact comes from the
factor
(1/2)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}
in the relative Todd class combined with the parabolic
level
\mu=1/2
. By the Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality, we can’t
choose an integral line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\operatorname{ch}_{2}>0
. It
follows that the integral value for which
\operatorname{ch}_{2}=0
is unique.

Here is an approach to the calculation referred to above. The
condition
\operatorname{ch}_{1}=0
tells us that
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
may be written
as
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})
for
0\leq\alpha<1/2
and
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})
for
1/2\leq\alpha<1
. Call
these two bundles
{\mathcal{E}}(b)
and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(b)
respectively. At all steps
below we will allow ourselves to ignore any terms that are constant as
functions of
b
. Set

c(b):=H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}}(b)),\;\;\;\Delta c(b):=H\cdot(\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(b))-\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}}(b))).

Using the convention that we ignore terms that are constant in
b
,
the integral formula for the second Chern class becomes much easier in
that it no longer depends on
\operatorname{ch}_{1}
:

H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}^{\rm par}=c(b)+\Delta c(b)/2.

From the GRR formula (and dropping terms not depending on
b
) we have:

\displaystyle c(b)

\displaystyle=\;\;\mbox{ the degree 3 terms of }\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\cdot(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(1+((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})+((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\cdot(((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2+((1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})(-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2))

\displaystyle=(1-2b)^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}/2+b(1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+b^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}/2-(1-2b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}-((1/2)-b+b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}-b\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}/2

\displaystyle=2b^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}+(b-2b^{2})\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+(b^{2}-b)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}/2

\displaystyle=2b^{2}\cdot 32+(b-2b^{2})\cdot 64+(b^{2}-b)\cdot 16=48(b-b^{2}).

Denote by
c^{\prime}(b)
the same formula for
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(b)
, calculated similarly to be

c^{\prime}(b)=-48(b+b^{2}).

One may alternatively check that
c^{\prime}(b)
and
c(b+1)
are the same up to
terms not depending on
b
—not a tautology,
this depends on the calculations of Proposition
4.8
.
We get

\Delta c(b)=c^{\prime}(b)-c(b)=-48(b+b^{2})-48(b-b^{2})=-96b.

Now

\displaystyle H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}^{\rm par}

\displaystyle=c(b)+\Delta c(b)/2\;+\mbox{ terms constant in }b

\displaystyle=48(b-b^{2})-48b\;+\mbox{ terms constant in }b

\displaystyle=-48b^{2}\;+\mbox{ terms constant in }b.

This has its extremum at
b=0
as claimed. One may note that this implies the spectral line bundle
is numerically
{\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})
, agreeing with the conclusion of Theorem
4.29
. This completes our
parenthetical calculation.

Moving on, in order to prove Corollary
13.3
we need to
rule out the possibility that
\mu=1
. This case corresponds to
the situation that our bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
has no parabolic structure along

{\rm Wob}^{\circ}
.

Corollary 13.22
.

With trivial parabolic structure, for
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(a\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+b\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})

and
{\mathcal{E}}=\pi_{*}({\mathcal{L}})
we have

H^{2}\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{1}({\mathcal{E}})=32(a-1)+64(b-1/2).

If this vanishes then
a=2-2b
.

Proof.

As in the proof of the previous corollary we get

\displaystyle H^{2}\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{1}({\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle=H^{2}\pi_{*}(\operatorname{td}_{1}(Y/X)+{\mathcal{L}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}((a-1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(b-1/2)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})

\displaystyle=32(a-1)+64(b-1/2)=32(a+2b-2).

This vanishes for
a=2-2b
.
∎

Put
m:=b-1
so
b=m+1
and
a=-2m
. (Note that this
m
is unrelated to the one used in the beginning of this section.)

Lemma 13.23
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})
and
{\mathcal{E}}=\pi_{*}({\mathcal{L}})
, then

H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}})=-48m^{2}-48m-8.

This value is
\leq-8
if
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We have that
\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}})
is the degree
2
part of

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left[(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\right.

\displaystyle+5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12)(1-\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2

\displaystyle\left.+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9)(1-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}+(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2)\right]

which is
\pi_{*}
of

5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9+(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}/2-(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2)(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}).

We get

\displaystyle H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}({\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}\cdot\left[5\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}/12+(\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}})/9+(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})^{2}/2\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}/2-(\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}/2)(-2m\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}+(m+1)\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}})\right].

This expands to:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{3}

\displaystyle(5/12+2m^{2}+2m)+

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}^{2}(1/9-2m(m+1)+1/2+m-(m+1))

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathit{F}}(1/9+(m+1)^{2}/2-(m+1)/2)

which, in view of Proposition
4.8
, becomes

\displaystyle 32(2m^{2}

\displaystyle+2m+5/12)+64(-2m^{2}-2m+1/9-1/2)+32(m^{2}/2+m/2+1/9)

\displaystyle=-48m^{2}-48m+40/3+32/3-32

\displaystyle=-48m^{2}-48m-8.

This is

-48m^{2}-48m-8=-48(m+1/2)^{2}+4.

The extremal value
m=-1/2
is not allowed since
m
is supposed to be
an integer. The extremal values for integers
m
are at
m=0
and
m=-1
and
there the values are
-8
.
∎

Let
\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
be a resolution of singularities of
the cusps of
{\rm Wob}
in codimension
2
.

Lemma 13.24
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{E}}
is a vector bundle over a surface
Z
. Let
b:\widetilde{Z}\rightarrow Z
be a birational map from another smooth
surface obtained by blowing up some points. Suppose

\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\bullet}
is a parabolic bundle on
\widetilde{Z}

that is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{E}}
over an open subset, complement of a
finite collection of points in
Z
, where
b
is an
isomorphism. Suppose
\operatorname{ch}_{1}^{\operatorname{par}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\bullet})=0
. Then

\operatorname{ch}_{2}^{\operatorname{par}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\bullet})\leq 0

with equality only in the case where the parabolic structure is
trivial and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{0}=b^{*}({\mathcal{E}})
.

Proof.

The corrections due to the parabolic structure are local. If any of
the corrections were
>0
then one could fill in such structures an
arbitrary number of times to a stable vector bundle and contradict the
Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality.

If any of the corrections is
=0
then one can fill that into an
irreducible flat unitary bundle. The parabolic case of the
Donaldson-Uhlenbeck-Yau theorem

[
SW01
,
Moc06
,
Moc09
]
would imply that the
resulting bundle is flat, but a flat bundle can not have a nontrivial
parabolic structure only on an exceptional divisor, so the parabolic
structure would have to be trivial.
∎

The previous lemma immediately implies the following

Corollary 13.25
.

If
{\mathcal{E}}=\pi_{*}({\mathcal{L}})
for
{\mathcal{L}}
a line bundle on
Y
, then for any
parabolic extension
\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\bullet}
of
{\mathcal{E}}|_{X^{\circ}}
across the exceptional divisors in
\widetilde{X}
such
that the parabolic first Chern class is
0
, then we have

H\cdot\operatorname{ch}_{2}^{\operatorname{par}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\bullet})\leq-8.

In particular, no such Higgs bundle can correspond to a local system.

Proof of Corollary
13.3
.

This corollary rules out the possibility of a parabolic level
\mu=0
,
so by Corollary
13.20
the level must be
\mu=1/2
.
The parabolic Higgs bundle therefore has the same structure, by Lemma

13.19
, as the parabolic Higgs bundle that we construct. This
proves Corollary
13.3
for the degree
1
moduli space, up
to the choice of line bundle of degree
0
on
Y
. The
Drinfeld-Laumon construction gives a Hecke eigensheaf with the
original rank
2
local system as eigenvalue. We have also shown the
Hecke eigensheaf property. On the one hand, this fixes the choice of
the line bundle of degree
0
on
Y
, and it also shows that our
construction coincides with the Drinfeld-Laumon construction on the
degree
0
moduli space.
∎

13.5
Tensor description in the degree
1
case

The description of the spectral variety given in Theorem
13.2
is
used above in the case of
m\gg 0
. However, it turns out that in our
special case of the moduli space
X_{1}
of bundles of rank
2
and odd
degree on a curve of genus
2
, this construction almost leads
directly to a description of the Hecke eigensheaf.

As pointed out to us by Hitchin, see
[
Hit22
]
, Atiyah showed in
1955
[
Ati55
]
that the moduli space of odd degree
{\mathbb{P}}GL(2)
-bundles
on
C
is a double covering of
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}=\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
. See
Proposition
13.29
and Theorem
13.31

below. Points of
X_{1}
are thus in correspondence with unordered
triples of points in the hyperelliptic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and up to a further
covering, with unordered triples of points of
C
. This allows us to
use the symmetric exterior tensor product
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
. Because of the coverings involved, we will first look at the
description over a general line.

Let
\Lambda
denote the eigenvalue rank
2
local system on
C
. This
leads to a rank
8
local system
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
on the
third symmetric power
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)
. If
(E,\theta)
is the rank

2
Higgs bundle associated to
\Lambda
, then the Higgs bundle
associated to
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
is
(E^{(3)},\theta^{(3)})
. By Lemma
13.4
, its spectral variety is

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
.

Suppose
\ell\subset X_{1}
is a general line, consisting of bundles

E
fitting into an exact sequence

0\rightarrow A\rightarrow E\rightarrow A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\rightarrow 0

for a degree
0
line bundle
A
. Set
M:=A^{\otimes-2}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})

be the resulting degree
3
line bundle, and let
|M|
be the linear
system of sections of
M
. We have
|M|\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)
. Points of
|M|
are lines in
H^{0}(Hom(A,A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\otimes\omega_{C}))
and this space is Serre dual to

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(A^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}),A)
, so
|M|
can be identified with the
projectivized space of extensions, which is
\ell
. Recall from
Subsection
4.5
that the elements of
|M|
are the fibers of
the trigonal map
C\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\cong\ell
associated to the
line
\ell
.

Theorem 13.26
.

The Hecke eigensheaf on
X_{1}
with eigenvalue
\Lambda
,
according to Drinfeld’s construction or our construction in
Section
4
, restricted to
\ell

becomes isomorphic to
\Lambda^{(\otimes 3)}|_{\ell}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
13.3
, the Hecke eigensheaf on
X_{1}
we
constructed in Section
4
is the same as that of
Drinfeld’s construction. By construction, the spectral variety of the
associated parabolic Higgs bundle
(\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet},\Phi_{1})

is the covering
Y_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
. The description in Lemma

4.15
of the covering
Y_{1}
over a line yields the
isomorphism

Y_{1}\times_{X_{1}}\ell\cong\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\times_{\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)}\ell.

These spaces are isomorphic to the subvariety
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})

of divisors on
{\widetilde{C}}
whose norm to
C
is in the linear system

|M|
.

The parabolic structure has levels
0,1/2
and one verifies that the
parabolic structure for
E^{(3)}
is the standard one coming from the
ramification points of the spectral covering, as is the case for

\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet}|_{\ell}
.

The spectral line bundle for
E^{(3)}
is pulled back from the line
bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}
on
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
obtained using the
spectral line bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
on
{\widetilde{C}}
.

Let us check first that this has the right degree to get a degree
0

parabolic bundle of rank
8
(this will provide a check of Lemma

13.4
). The spectral line bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
has degree
2
on

{\widetilde{C}}
. Let
{\widetilde{C}}^{3}_{M}
denote the subvariety in

{\widetilde{C}}^{\times 3}
consisting of ordered triples

(\tilde{t}_{1},\tilde{t}_{2},\tilde{t}_{3})
of points in
{\widetilde{C}}

for which the divisor
t_{1}+t_{2}+t_{3}=\pi(\tilde{t}_{1})+\pi(\tilde{t}_{2})+\pi(\tilde{t}_{3})
is in the linear system

|M|
. The map
{\widetilde{C}}^{3}_{M}\rightarrow{\widetilde{C}}
given by, say, the first
projection has degree
8
. Indeed once we fix one of the points

\tilde{t}_{1}
with image
t_{1}
in
C
, the remaining divisor
t_{2}+t_{3}

in
C
is fixed, leading to two choices of ordered triple of points;
there are
4
lifts of each pair
t_{2},t_{3}
to

\tilde{t}_{2},\tilde{t}_{3}
so the fiber over
\tilde{t}_{1}
has
8

points in all. Thus for each
i=1,2,3
the
pullback
\operatorname{pr}_{i}^{*}(L)
therefore has degree
16

on
{\widetilde{C}}^{3}_{M}
, and the tensor product of three of these has degree

48
.

The spectral line bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}
on
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})

pulls back to this bundle of degree
48
via the
6:1
map

{\widetilde{C}}^{3}_{M}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
, so
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}
has
degree
8
.

The map
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow\ell
ramifies at two
types of points: there are the points of the divisor

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\cap\boldsymbol{\mathit{E}}_{1}
that are of the form

\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}+\tilde{t}_{3}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
, where
t_{i}=t_{j}
but
\tilde{t}_{i}\neq\tilde{t}_{j}
for some
i\neq j
. There are two such points over
each point of
\ell\cap{\rm Wob}_{1}
. Then, there are ramification points

\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}+\tilde{t}_{3}
such that one of the

\tilde{t}_{i}
is a ramification point of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
. For each of
the
4
ramification points of
{\widetilde{C}}/C
, the other two points in
the trigonal fiber are specified and there are
4
ways of lifting
these to pairs of points in
{\widetilde{C}}
, so we get
16
such
ramification points. These constitute the movable ramification locus.

The parabolic degree of a bundle created using the standard parabolic
structure at some simple ramification points, and not at others, is
calculated by the same formula as the degree of a usual direct image,
but not counting the ramification points that are used for the
parabolic structure. Thus, we should count
16
ramification points
instead of
32
, and the required parabolic degree is one-half of this
number, that is to say
16/2=8
. This is indeed the degree of the
bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}
as is to be expected.

The pullback of the spectral line bundle from
Y_{1}
under the map

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow Y_{1}
also has degree
8
, since the
spectral line bundle is chosen to create a parabolic structure over

X_{1}
that has vanishing
\operatorname{ch}_{1}
.

To identify the two spectral line bundles, we need to show that these
two line bundles of degree
8
are the same, when the spectral line
bundle on
Y_{1}
is chosen as a function of
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
in the specified
way that will be described next.

We recall from Subsection
7.4
that the
spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{1}
on
Y_{1}
for the Hecke eigensheaf is
related to the spectral line bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}
on
{\widetilde{C}}
for the
original eigenvalue Higgs bundle in the following way.

First, one has from Lemma
7.5
the line bundle

\mathfrak{L}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}(-\pi^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}))}^{*}\boldsymbol{\xi}\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}^{-1}

on the Prym variety
{\mathcal{P}}
of degree
0
line bundles on
{\widetilde{C}}

with trivial norm down to
C
. This gives by translation the line
bundle

\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\boldsymbol{t}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}}-\pi^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})}^{*}\mathfrak{L}

on the Prym variety
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
of degree
3
line bundles with norm

{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Then

\mathscr{L}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}^{*}\mathfrak{L}_{1}\otimes f_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}(1)

is the spectral line bundle for the Hecke eigensheaf
\mathscr{F}_{1,\bullet}
.

The following lemma, whose proof will be given below, shows the comparison
between the two spectral line bundles on
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
.

Lemma 13.27
.

The pullback of
\mathscr{L}_{1}
by the map
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow Y_{1}
is isomorphic to
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}
.

To finish the proof of the theorem, we leave it to the reader to check
that the tautological
1
-form for
E^{(3)}
given in Lemma

13.4
is the same as the restriction of the tautological
form on
Y_{1}
. Therefore, the parabolic Higgs bundles are isomorphic,
giving the desired isomorphism of local systems.
∎

Proof of Lemma
13.27
:

Express the degree
0
line bundle as
A={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(a+b-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
. Thus

M={\mathcal{O}}_{C}(2a+2b-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
.
A point of
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
is a divisor
\tilde{y}=\tilde{y}_{1}+\tilde{y}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{3}
on
{\widetilde{C}}
, such that the
image divisor
y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}
on
C
is in
|M|
. This yields a line bundle

U_{3}(\tilde{y}):=\pi^{*}(A)(\tilde{y}_{1}+\tilde{y}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{3})={\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{a}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}+\tilde{b}+\tilde{b}^{\prime}+\tilde{y}_{1}+\tilde{y}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{3}-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime})

on
{\widetilde{C}}
, whose norm to
C

gives the line bundle
A^{\otimes 2}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}-2\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}})
, so
U_{3}(\tilde{t})
is a point of

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
. This describes the map
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{3}
that lifts in a unique way to a map to the blow-up
Y_{1}
.

The line bundle
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
extends to a line bundle
\mathfrak{L}_{1,\operatorname{Jac}}
on the Jacobian
\operatorname{Jac}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
, as may be seen by
its definition. The above description gives a map

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{3}({\widetilde{C}}).

Furthermore, compose with the translation
\operatorname{Jac}^{3}(C)\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}(C)
that relates
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
with
\mathfrak{L}
, to get a map

U_{\operatorname{Jac}}:\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})

defined by

U_{\operatorname{Jac}}(\tilde{y})=\tilde{a}+\tilde{a}^{\prime}+\tilde{b}+\tilde{b}^{\prime}+\tilde{y}_{1}+\tilde{y}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{3}-4\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}-3\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime}.

The pullback of
\mathfrak{L}
by this map is the tensor product of the
values of
\mathfrak{L}
on each of the points translated back to

\operatorname{Jac}^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
. All the points except those of
\tilde{y}
are
constant, so tensoring with those values leads to just tensoring with
constant lines.

Let
s:{\widetilde{C}}^{3}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
be the projection,
and let
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0}:=j^{*}\mathfrak{L}
denote the pullback of
\mathfrak{L}
to

{\widetilde{C}}
along the map
j:{\widetilde{C}}\rightarrow Jac^{0}({\widetilde{C}})
that
sends
\tilde{t}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\tilde{t}-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime})
.

We conclude that

s^{*}U_{\operatorname{Jac}}^{*}(\mathfrak{L})\cong\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{3}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0}).

This in turn implies that

U_{\operatorname{Jac}}^{*}(\mathfrak{L})\cong(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})^{(3)}.

Therefore, the restriction of
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
to
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
is
the same as the restriction of
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})^{(3)}
.

The restriction of the spectral line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{1}
to

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
is thus
(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})^{(3)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell}(1)
. We have
\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime})
.

The line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime})^{(3)}
on

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
is given by a divisor whose pullback to

{\widetilde{C}}^{3}
is the set of points
(\tilde{t}_{1},\tilde{t}_{2},\tilde{t}_{3})
such that one of the coordinates is either
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}
or

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime}
. The divisor in
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\widetilde{C}})
is the set of sums

\tilde{t}_{1}+\tilde{t}_{2}+\tilde{t}_{3}
containing either
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}
or

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime}
. This condition is equivalent to the condition that the sum

t_{1}+t_{2}+t_{3}
contains
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
. Restrict now to
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
. For
A
hence
M
general, there is a unique sum that may be
written as
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}+t_{2}+t_{3}
, so our divisor consists of all the lifts
of these points to
{\widetilde{C}}
. That, in turn, is the fiber of

\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})\rightarrow\ell=|M|
over the point
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}+t_{2}+t_{3}\in|M|
that is also described as the image of
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}
under
the trigonal map. We have now shown that the restriction of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime})^{(3)}
to
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
is
isomorphic to the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell}(1)
.

Now, the restriction of
\mathscr{L}_{1}
to
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}_{M}({\widetilde{C}})
is

(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})^{(3)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\ell}(1)=(\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}_{0})^{(3)}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{C}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime\prime})^{(3)}=\boldsymbol{\mathit{N}}^{(3)}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
13.27
, tying up what was needed
for the proof of Theorem
13.26
.
∎

We now sketch how to go from here to a global description over

X_{1}
. The proofs are left to the reader. Let
P\rightarrow X_{1}
be
the degree
4
map whose fiber over
x\in X_{1}
is the set of four
points corresponding to four lines through
x
. Thus
P\rightarrow\operatorname{Jac}(C)
is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
-bundle.

Lemma 13.28
.

Given any pair of two lines
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
passing through
x
, we
obtain a point of
C
. If
\ell_{3},\ell_{4}
is the opposite pair of
lines (so that altogether these are the four lines through
x
) then
the corresponding point of
C
is the conjugate by the hyperelliptic
involution
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
.

Given a point of
P
lying over
x\in X_{1}
it is one of the lines, so
there are three pairs of lines containing that one; this gives a point
of
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)
. Choosing a different point over
x
results in changing
two of these three by the hyperelliptic involution, and altogether the
four lines yield three pairs of pairs of points, hence three pairs of
conjugate points in
C
, hence three points of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. We get a
diagram

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})}

The hyperelliptic involution
\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}
acts on
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)
by acting on
all three of these points, so we can factor and obtain a diagram:

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)/\boldsymbol{\iota}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}).}

Here the vertical maps have degree
4
and the horizontal maps have
degree
32
.

Proposition 13.29
.

The bottom map is the map given by squaring coordinates in
{\mathbb{P}}^{5}
,
from
X_{1}
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}=\operatorname{Sym}^{3}({\mathbb{P}}^{1})
. There are
6
planes in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}

corresponding to the six Weierstrass points of
C
, and the map

X_{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
has ramification of order
2
along these. In
particular it maps to a covering
X^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
of degree
2

ramified on these
6
planes.

Remark 13.30
.

It would be good to look more closely at the various group actions of

({\mathbb{Z}}/2)^{n}
for
n=4,5,6
as well as the Heisenberg group. We will
not carry out this analysis here.

Theorem 13.31
.

The space
X^{\prime}
is the moduli space of
{\mathbb{P}}GL_{2}
bundles of odd
degree. Let
\Delta\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
be the discriminant. Then the
pullback of
\Delta
to
X^{\prime}
and
X_{1}
are the wobbly loci of those
spaces respectively.

We can form the local system
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
on
Sym^{3}(C)
.
There are two ways of descending
\Lambda
to a local system
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
with order two monodromy at the
6
points. For
each of these we get local systems
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
. The rank
8
local system
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
has
singularities on the big diagonal of
\operatorname{Sym}^{3}(C)
.

The rank
8
local system
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
has
singularities on the discriminant and the six planes in
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
, and
its pullbacks to
X^{\prime}
and
X_{1}
have singularities (generically finite
of order
2
) on the wobbly loci.

Theorem 13.32
.

The pullback of our Hecke eigenvector local system of
X_{1}
to
P
is
isomorphic to the pullback of
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}
to
P
. The
pullbacks of
\Lambda^{(\boxtimes 3)}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}
to
X^{\prime}
or
X_{1}
are
isomorphic to our Hecke eigenvector local system.

We note that the discriminant
\Delta
has a cuspidal locus along the
small diagonal; the pullback will give the cuspidal locus of the
wobbly locus.
